<meta>This page begins the Preface, following the Table of Contents, and introduces the book as an introductory guide to Theosophical principles.</meta>

# PREFACE.

The purpose of this book is exactly expressed in its title, “THE Key to Theosophy,” and needs only a few words of explanation. It is not a complete or exhaustive textbook of Theosophy, but only a key to unlock the door that leads to deeper study. It traces the broad outlines of the Wisdom Religion and explains its fundamental principles; at the same time, it addresses the various objections raised by the average Western inquirer and endeavors to present unfamiliar concepts in as simple a form and as clear a language as possible. That it should succeed in making Theosophy understandable without mental effort on the part of the reader would be too much to expect; but it is hoped that any remaining obscurity lies in the thought rather than the language, and is due to depth rather than confusion. To the mentally lazy or dull, Theosophy must remain a riddle; for in the mental world, as in the spiritual world, each person must progress by their own efforts. The writer cannot do the reader’s thinking for them, nor would the reader be any better off if such vicarious thought were possible. The need for an explanation like the present one has long been felt among those interested in the Theosophical Society and its work, and it is hoped that it

<summary>The author introduces the book as a foundational guide designed to explain the fundamental principles of the Wisdom Religion and address common questions from Western inquirers. The text emphasizes that readers must apply their own mental effort to grasp these profound concepts, as spiritual and mental progress requires individual work.</summary>

<keywords>Theosophy, Wisdom Religion, Western inquirer, Theosophical Society, mental effort, spiritual progress, Preface</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page concludes the Preface, continuing the author's explanation of the book's intent and addressing the relationship between Theosophy and Spiritualism.</meta>

# PREFACE.

will provide information, as free from technical jargon as possible, to many whose interest has been sparked but who are still simply puzzled rather than convinced.

Effort has been made to separate truth from falsehood in Spiritualist teachings regarding life after death, and to reveal the true nature of Spiritualist phenomena. Similar explanations in the past have brought much anger upon the author; Spiritualists, like many others, prefer to believe what is pleasant rather than what is true, and they become very angry with anyone who shatters a comforting delusion. Over the last year, Theosophy has been the target of every bitter attack from Spiritualism, as if those who possess a partial truth felt more hostility toward those who possess the whole truth than toward those who have none at all.

The author owes sincere thanks to the many Theosophists who have offered suggestions, asked questions, or otherwise helped during the writing of this book. The book will be more useful because of their help, and that will be their greatest reward.

H. P. B.

<summary>The author explains that the book aims to clarify spiritual phenomena and life after death while distinguishing Theosophical truths from Spiritualist delusions. Blavatsky notes the hostility received from Spiritualists and thanks fellow Theosophists for their contributions to the work.</summary>
<keywords>Theosophy, Spiritualism, life after death, spiritual phenomena, H. P. B., technical jargon, delusion, Preface</keywords>

# PREFACE
### TO THE
## SECOND EDITION.

---

In order to further simplify the study of Theosophy, which the “Key” has already made an easy task, I have added an extensive “Glossary” of all the technical terms found in it. Most of the definitions and explanations are transcriptions or abbreviations from the larger “Theosophical Glossary,” which will be published soon together with the treatise on “Archaic Symbolism.” It is hoped that both “Glossaries” will meet a long-felt need, and that the larger one will cover the entire range of occult terminology as completely as possible.

H. P. B.

**THEOSOPHICAL HEADQUARTERS**,
->19, AVENUE ROAD,<-
->LONDON, N.W.<-
->1890.<-

<meta>This page begins the main text of the work, following the supplemental preface for the second edition. It establishes the dialogue format between an Enquirer and a Theosophist to define the fundamental nature and origins of Theosophy.</meta>

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

## I.

### THEOSOPHY AND THE THEOSOPHICAL SOCIETY.

#### *THE MEANING OF THE NAME.*

ENQUIRER. <term>Theosophy</term> and its doctrines are often referred to as a recently invented religion. Is it a religion?

THEOSOPHIST. It is not. <term>Theosophy</term> is <term>Divine Knowledge</term> or Science.

ENQ. What is the real meaning of the term?

THEO. "<term>Divine Wisdom</term>," θεοσοφία (<term>Theosophia</term>) or Wisdom of the gods, as θεογονία (<term>theogonia</term>), genealogy of the gods. The word θεος means a god in Greek—one of the divine beings—certainly not "God" in the sense attached in our time to the term. Therefore, it is not "Wisdom of God," as translated by some, but *Divine Wisdom* like that possessed by the gods. The term is many thousand years old.

ENQ. What is the origin of the name?

THEO. It comes to us from the Alexandrian philosophers, called lovers of truth, <term>Philaletheians</term>, from φιλ (phil) "loving," and ἀλήθεια (aletheia) "truth." The name <term>Theosophy</term> dates from

<summary>This page defines Theosophy not as a religion, but as "Divine Wisdom" or science, tracing its etymological roots to Greek terms for the gods. The name is attributed to the Alexandrian philosophers known as Philaletheians, or lovers of truth.</summary>

<keywords>Theosophy, Divine Wisdom, etymology, Greek philosophy, Alexandrian philosophers, Philaletheians, Theogonia, Theosophia</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the definition and historical lineage of Theosophy, identifying the third-century "Eclectic" system as a key development while tracing its potential origins back to ancient Egypt.</meta>

->THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

the third century of our era and began with Ammonius Saccas and his disciples,\* who started the Eclectic Theosophical system.

**ENQ.** What was the object of this system?

**THEO.** First of all, to instill certain great moral truths into its disciples and all those who were "lovers of the truth." This is why the Theosophical Society adopted the motto: "There is no religion higher than truth."† The chief aim of the Founders

---

<note>\* Also called Analogeticists. As explained by Prof. Alex. Wilder, F.T.S., in his "Eclectic Philosophy," they were called this because of their practice of interpreting all sacred legends, narratives, myths, and mysteries by a rule or principle of analogy and correspondence. Thus, events described as occurring in the external world were regarded as expressing the operations and experiences of the human soul. They were also called Neo-Platonists. Although Theosophy, or the Eclectic Theosophical system, is generally attributed to the third century, if Diogenes Laertius is to be believed, its origin is much earlier; he attributed the system to an Egyptian priest, Pot-Amun, who lived in the early days of the Ptolemaic dynasty. The same author tells us that the name is Coptic and signifies one consecrated to Amun, the God of Wisdom. Theosophy is the equivalent of <term>Brahm-Vidya</term>, divine knowledge.</note>

<note>† Eclectic Theosophy was divided into three heads: (1) Belief in one absolute, incomprehensible, and supreme Deity, or infinite essence, which is the root of all nature and of all that exists, both visible and invisible. (2) Belief in man's eternal, immortal nature; because it is a radiation of the Universal Soul, it is of the same essence as that soul. (3) *Theurgy*, or "divine work," or producing a work of gods; from *theoï*, "gods," and *ergeïn*, "to work." The term is very old, but because it belongs to the vocabulary of the <term>MYSTERIES</term>, it was not in popular use. It was a mystic belief—practically proven by initiated adepts and priests—that by making oneself as pure as the incorporeal beings—that is, by returning to one's original purity of nature—man could move the gods to share Divine mysteries with him, and even cause them to become visible at times, either subjectively or objectively. It was the transcendental aspect of what is now called Spiritualism; but having been abused and misunderstood by the general public, it had come to be</note>

<summary>This page identifies Ammonius Saccas as the founder of the third-century Eclectic Theosophical system, which aimed to teach moral truths and followed the motto "There is no religion higher than truth." It also outlines the system's three fundamental beliefs regarding the Deity, the human soul, and the practice of theurgy.</summary>

<keywords>Ammonius Saccas, Eclectic Theosophy, Analogeticists, Alexander Wilder, Neo-Platonists, Pot-Amun, Brahm-Vidya, Theurgy, Universal Soul, Divine Truth</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanation of the Theosophical Society's goals, specifically the reconciliation of all religions, and provides a detailed historical background on Ammonius Saccas and the Neo-Platonic school regarding theurgy and universal ethics.</meta>

->THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

of the Eclectic Theosophical School was one of the three objects of its modern successor, the Theosophical Society: namely, to reconcile all religions, sects, and nations under a common system of ethics based on eternal truths.

**ENQ.** What have you to show that this is not an impossible dream?

---

<note>regarded by some as necromancy and was generally forbidden. A distorted practice of the theurgy of Iamblichus still lingers in the ceremonial magic of some modern Kabbalists. Modern Theosophy avoids and rejects both these kinds of magic and "necromancy" as being very dangerous. Real divine theurgy requires an almost superhuman purity and holiness of life; otherwise, it degenerates into mediumship or black magic. The immediate disciples of Ammonius Saccas, who was called *Theodidaktos*, "God-taught"—such as Plotinus and his follower Porphyry—rejected theurgy at first, but were finally reconciled to it through Iamblichus, who wrote a work to that effect entitled "De Mysteriis," under the name of his own master, a famous Egyptian priest called Abammon. Ammonius Saccas was the son of Christian parents, and, having been repelled by dogmatic spiritualistic Christianity from his childhood, became a Neo-Platonist; like Jacob Boehme and other great seers and mystics, he is said to have had divine wisdom revealed to him in dreams and visions. Hence his name of *Theodidaktos*. He resolved to reconcile every system of religion and, by demonstrating their identical origin, to establish one universal creed based on ethics. His life was so blameless and pure, and his learning so profound and vast, that several Church Fathers were his secret disciples. Clement of Alexandria speaks very highly of him. Plotinus, the "St. John" of Ammonius, was also a man universally respected and esteemed, and of the most profound learning and integrity. When thirty-nine years of age, he accompanied the Roman Emperor Gordian and his army to the East to be instructed by the sages of Bactria and India. He had a School of Philosophy in Rome. Porphyry, his disciple, whose r<unclear>ea</unclear>l name was Malek (a Hellenized Jew), collected all the writings of his maste<unclear>r. P</unclear>orphyry was himself a great author and gave an allegorical interpretation to some parts of Homer’s writings. The system of meditation the Philaletheians resorted to was ecstasy, a system akin to Indian Yoga practice. What is known of the Eclectic School is due to Origen, Longinus, and Plotinus, the immediate disciples of Ammonius.— (See *Eclectic Philosophy*, by A. Wilder.)</note>

<summary>The page describes the Theosophical Society's goal to reconcile all religions under a universal ethical system, drawing parallels to the work of Ammonius Saccas and the Neo-Platonists. It provides a historical overview of key figures like Plotinus and Porphyry, their studies in the East, and their views on divine theurgy and meditation.</summary>

<keywords>Ammonius Saccas, Theodidaktos, Plotinus, Porphyry, Theurgy, Neo-Platonism, Universal Creed, Ethics, Eclectic School, Iamblichus, Jacob Boehme, Alexander Wilder</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the primary goals of the Theosophical Society, specifically focusing on the historical precedent set by Ammonius Saccas and the Neo-Platonic school for reconciling diverse religious systems through a universal "Wisdom-religion."</meta>

->THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

and that all the world’s religions *are* based on one and the same truth?

**THEO.** Their comparative study and analysis. The “Wisdom-religion” was one in antiquity; and the sameness of early religious philosophy is proved to us by the identical doctrines taught to the Initiates during the MYSTERIES, an institution that was once spread everywhere. “All the old forms of worship indicate the existence of a single Theosophy that preceded them. The key that is to open one must open all; otherwise it cannot be the right key.” (Eclect. Philo.)

### THE POLICY OF THE THEOSOPHICAL SOCIETY.

**ENQ.** In the days of Ammonius there were several great ancient religions, and many were the sects in Egypt and Palestine alone. How could he reconcile them?

**THEO.** By doing what we are trying to do again now. The Neo-Platonists were a large group and belonged to various religious philosophies*; so do our Theosophists. In those days, the Jew Aristobulus affirmed that the ethics of Aristotle represented

---

<note>* It was under Philadelphus that Judaism established itself in Alexandria, and immediately the Greek teachers became the dangerous rivals of the College of Rabbis of Babylon. the *esoteric* teachings of the Law of Moses; Philo Judæus endeavored to reconcile the *Pentateuch* with Pythagorean and Platonic philosophy; and Josephus proved that the Essenes of Carmel were simply the copyists and followers of the Egyptian Therapeutæ (the healers). So it is in our day. We can show the line of descent of every Christian religion, as of every sect, even the smallest. These latter are the minor twigs or shoots grown on the larger branches; but shoots and branches spring from the same trunk—the WISDOM-RELIGION. To prove this was the aim of Ammonius, who endeavored to persuade Gentiles and Christians, Jews and idolaters, to lay aside their contentions and strifes, remembering only that they were all in possession of the same truth under various coverings, and were all the children of a common mother.* This is the aim of Theosophy likewise.

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

THEO. An almost countless number of well-known writers. Mosheim, one of them, says that:—

> “Ammonius taught that the religion of the masses went hand in hand with philosophy, and with her had shared the fate of being by degrees corrupted and obscured with mere human conceits, superstitions, and lies; that it ought, therefore, to be brought back to its original purity by purging it of this dross and explaining it upon philosophical principles; and that the entire goal Christ had in view was to reinstate and restore to its original integrity the wisdom of the ancients; to reduce within bounds the universally prevailing dominion of superstition; and in part to correct, and in part to exterminate the various errors that had found their way into the different popular religions.”

This, again, is precisely what the modern <term>Theosophists</term> say. Only while the great <term>Philaletheian</term> was supported and helped in the policy he pursued by two Church Fathers, Clement and Athenagoras, by all the learned Rabbis of the Synagogue, the Academy, and the Groves, and while he taught a common doctrine for all, we, his followers on the same line, receive no recognition, but, on the contrary, are abused and persecuted. People 1,500 years ago are thus shown to have been more tolerant than they are in this enlightened century.

ENQ. Was he encouraged and supported by the Church because, despite his heresies, Ammonius taught Christianity and was a Christian?

THEO. Not at all. He was born a Christian, but never accepted Church Christianity. As said of him by the same writer:

> “He had only to present his instructions according to the ancient pillars of Hermes, which Plato and Pythagoras knew before, and from them constituted their philosophy. Finding the same in the prologue of the Gospel according to St. John, he very properly supposed that the

### *THE WISDOM-RELIGION WAS ESOTERIC IN ALL AGES.*

**ENQ.** Since Ammonius never committed anything to writing, how can one feel sure that such were his teachings?

**THEO.** Neither did Buddha, Pythagoras, Confucius, Orpheus, Socrates, or even Jesus leave behind them any writings. Yet most of these are historical personages, and their teachings have all survived. The disciples of Ammonius (among whom were Origen and Herennius) wrote treatises and explained his ethics. Certainly the latter are as historical, if not more so, than the Apostolic writings. Moreover, his pupils—Origen, Plotinus, and Longinus (counselor of the famous Queen Zenobia)—have all left voluminous records of the Philaletheian System—so far, at all events, as their public profession of faith was known, for the school was divided into exoteric and *esoteric* teachings.

**ENQ.** How have the latter tenets reached our day, since you hold that what is properly called the WISDOM-RELIGION was esoteric?

**THEO.** The WISDOM-RELIGION was always one, and being the last word of possible human knowledge, was, therefore, carefully preserved. It preceded by long ages the Alexandrian Theo-

**ENQ.** Where and by whom was it so preserved?

**THEO.** Among Initiates of every country; among profound seekers after truth—their disciples; and in those parts of the world where such topics have always been most valued and pursued: in India, Central Asia, and Persia.

**ENQ.** Can you give me some proofs of its esotericism?

**THEO.** The best proof you can have of the fact is that every ancient religious, or rather philosophical, cult consisted of an esoteric or secret teaching, and an exoteric (outward public) worship. Furthermore, it is a well-known fact that the MYSTERIES of the ancients comprised within every nation the “greater” (secret) and “lesser” (public) MYSTERIES—for example, in the celebrated solemnities called the *Eleusinia*, in Greece. From the Hierophants of Samothrace and Egypt, and the initiated Brahmins of the India of old, down to the later Hebrew Rabbis, all preserved their real *bona fide* beliefs in secret for fear of profanation. The Jewish Rabbis called their secular religious series the *Mercavah* (the exterior body), “the vehicle,” or the covering which contains the hidden soul—that is, their highest secret knowledge. Not one of the ancient nations ever imparted through its priests its real philosophical secrets to the masses, but allotted to the latter only the husks. Northern Buddhism has its “greater” and its “lesser” vehicle, known as the *Mahayana*, the esoteric, and the *Hinayana*, the exoteric, schools. Nor can you blame them for such secrecy; for surely you would not think of feeding your flock of sheep

Examples might be brought from every country to this effect.

**ENQ.** What is your explanation of it?

**THEO.** Real ecstasy was defined by Plotinus as "the liberation of the mind from its finite consciousness, becoming one and identified with the infinite." This is the highest condition, says Professor Wilder, but not one of permanent duration, and it is reached only by the very, *very* few. It is, indeed, identical with that state which is known in India as <term>Samadhi</term>. The latter is practiced by the <term>Yogis</term>, who facilitate it physically by strict abstinence from food and drink, and mentally by an incessant endeavor to purify and elevate the mind. Meditation is silent and *unspoken* prayer, or, as Plato expressed it, "the ardent turning of the soul toward the divine; not to ask for any particular good (as in the common meaning of prayer), but for good itself—for the universal Supreme Good" of which we are a part on earth, and out of the essence of which we have all emerged. **ENQ.** <term>Theosophy</term>, then, is not, as some believe, a newly devised scheme?

**THEO.** Only ignorant people would refer to it that way. It is as old as the world in its teachings and ethics, if not in name, and it is also the broadest and most universal system of all.

**ENQ.** How is it, then, that <term>Theosophy</term> has remained so unknown to the nations of the Western Hemisphere? Why should it have been a sealed book to races admittedly the most cultured and advanced?

**THEO.** We believe there were nations as cultured in days of old and certainly more spiritually “advanced” than we are. But there are several reasons for this deliberate ignorance. **ENQ.** You have brought forward proofs that such secrecy has existed; but what was the real cause for it?

**THEO.** The causes for it were: First, the perversity of average human nature and its selfishness, which always tends toward the gratification of *personal* desires at the expense of neighbors and next of kin. Such people could never be entrusted with *divine* secrets. Second, their unreliability in keeping sacred and divine knowledge from desecration. It is the latter that led to the perversion of the most sublime truths and symbols, and to the gradual transformation of spiritual matters into anthropomorphic, concrete, and coarse imagery—in other words, to the diminishing of the idea of God and to idolatry.

### *THEOSOPHY IS NOT BUDDHISM.*

**ENQ.** You are often spoken of as “Esoteric Buddhists.” Are you then all followers of <term>Gautama Buddha</term>?

**THEO.** No more than musicians are all followers of <term>Wagner</term>. Some of us are Buddhists by religion; yet there are far more <term>Hindus</term> and <term>Brahmins</term> than Buddhists among us, and more Christian-born Europeans and Americans than *converted* Buddhists. The mistake has arisen from a misunderstanding of the real meaning of the title of <term>Mr. Sinnett</term>’s excellent work, “Esoteric Buddhism.” That last word should have been spelled *with one “d” instead of two*, as then “Budhism” would have meant what it

This has passed root and branch into the esoteric teachings that <term>Gautama</term> imparted to his chosen <term>Arhats</term> only.

His esoteric teachings were simply the *Gupta Vidya* (secret knowledge) of the ancient <term>Brahmins</term>, the key to which their modern successors have, with few exceptions, completely lost. And this *Vidya* has passed into what is now known as the *inner teachings* of the <term>Mahayana</term> school of Northern Buddhism. Those who deny it are simply ignorant pretenders to Orientalism. I advise you to read the <term>Rev. Mr. Edkins</term>’ *Chinese Buddhism*—especially the chapters on the Exoteric and Esoteric schools.

**ENQ.** But are not the ethics of <term>Theosophy</term> identical to those taught by <term>Buddha</term>?

**THEO.** Certainly, because these ethics are the soul of the <term>Wisdom-Religion</term> and were once the common property of the initiates of all nations. But <term>Buddha</term> was the first to embody these lofty ethics in his public teachings and to make them the foundation and the very essence of his public system. This is where the immense difference lies between <term>exoteric Buddhism</term> and every other religion. For while in other religions ritualism and dogma hold the primary and most important place, in Buddhism it is the ethics that have always been most emphasized. This explains the resemblance—amounting almost to identity—between the ethics of <term>Theosophy</term> and those of the religion of <term>Buddha</term>.

**ENQ.** Are there any major points of difference?

# II.
## EXOTERIC AND ESOTERIC THEOSOPHY.

->*WHAT THE MODERN THEOSOPHICAL SOCIETY IS NOT.*<-

ENQUIRER: Your doctrines, then, are not a revival of Buddhism, nor are they entirely copied from Neo-Platonic Theosophy?

THEOSOPHIST: They are not. But to these questions I cannot give you a better answer than by quoting from a paper read on "Theosophy" by Dr. J. D. Buck, F.T.S., before the last Theosophical Convention in Chicago, America (April 1889). No living theosophist has better expressed and understood the real essence of Theosophy than our honored friend Dr. Buck:

> "The Theosophical Society was organized for the purpose of spreading Theosophical doctrines and for the promotion of the Theosophical life. The present Theosophical Society is not the first of its kind. I have a volume entitled: 'Theosophical Transactions of the Philadelphian Society,' published in London in 1697; and another with the following title: 'Introduction to Theosophy, or the Science of the Mystery of Christ; that is, of Deity, Nature, and Creature, embracing the philosophy of all the working powers of life, magical and spiritual, and forming a practical guide to the most sublime purity, sanctity, and evangelical perfection; also to the attainment of divine vision, and the"

holy angelic arts, potencies, and other prerogatives of regeneration,’ published in London in 1855. The following is the dedication of this volume:—

> “To the students of Universities, Colleges, and schools of Christendom: To Professors of Metaphysical, Mechanical, and Natural Science in all its forms: To men and women of education generally, of fundamental orthodox faith: To Deists, Arians, Unitarians, Swedenborgians, and other defective and ungrounded creeds, rationalists, and skeptics of every kind: To just-minded and enlightened Mohammedans, Jews, and oriental Patriarch-religionists: but especially to the gospel minister and missionary, whether to the barbaric or intellectual peoples, this introduction to Theosophy, or the science of the foundation and mystery of all things, is most humbly and affectionately dedicated.”

In the following year (1856) another volume was issued, a royal octavo of 600 pages in diamond type, titled *Theosophical Miscellanies*. Of the last-named work, only 500 copies were issued for free distribution to libraries and universities. These earlier movements, of which there were many, originated within the Church among persons of great piety and earnestness and of unblemished character; all of these writings were in orthodox form, using Christian expressions, and, like the writings of the eminent Churchman William Law, would only be distinguished by the ordinary reader for their great earnestness and piety. These were one and all but attempts to derive and explain the deeper meanings and original significance of the Christian Scriptures, and to illustrate and unfold the Theosophic life. These works were soon forgotten and are now generally unknown. They sought to reform the clergy and revive genuine piety, and were never welcomed. That one word, “Heresy,” was sufficient to bury them in the limbo of all such Utopias. At the time of the Reformation, John Reuchlin made a similar attempt with the same result, though he was the intimate and trusted friend of Luther. Orthodoxy never desired to be informed and enlightened. These reformers were told, as Paul was by Festus, that too much learning had made them mad and that it would be dangerous to go further. # THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

secular power, and coming to the core of the matter, these writings were Theosophical in the strictest sense and pertain solely to man’s knowledge of his own nature and the higher life of the soul. The present Theosophical movement has sometimes been declared an attempt to convert Christendom to Buddhism, which simply means that the word "heresy" has lost its terrors and relinquished its power. Individuals in every age have more or less clearly understood Theosophical doctrines and woven them into the fabric of their lives. These doctrines belong exclusively to no religion and are confined to no specific society or time. They are the birthright of every human soul. Orthodoxy must be worked out by each individual according to his nature, his needs, and his varying experience. This may explain why those who have imagined Theosophy to be a new religion have searched in vain for its creed and ritual. Its creed is loyalty to truth, and its ritual is "to honor every truth by using it."

> "How little the masses of mankind understand this principle of Universal Brotherhood, and how seldom they recognize its transcendent importance, can be seen in the diversity of opinions and false interpretations regarding the Theosophical Society. This Society was organized on this single principle—the essential Brotherhood of Man—as briefly outlined and imperfectly described here. It has been attacked as being both Buddhist and anti-Christian, as if it could be both at once, when both Buddhism and Christianity—as presented by their inspired founders—make brotherhood the single essential element of doctrine and life. Theosophy has also been regarded as something 'new under the sun,' or at best as old mysticism masquerading under a new name. While it is true that many societies founded upon and united to support the principles of altruism or essential brotherhood have used various names, it is also true that many have also been called Theosophical, sharing the same principles and aims as the current society of that name. For all of these societies, the essential doctrine has been the same, and everything else has been incidental; however, this does not change the fact that many people are attracted to the incidental details while overlooking or ignoring the essentials."

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

No better or more explicit answer—provided by a man who is one of our most esteemed and earnest Theosophists—could be given to your questions.

ENQ. Which system do you prefer or follow, in that case, besides Buddhist ethics?

THEO. None, and all. We hold to no religion, just as we hold to no philosophy in particular: we cull the good we find in each. But here, again, it must be stated that, like all other ancient systems, Theosophy is divided into *Exoteric* and *Esoteric* Sections.

ENQ. What is the difference?

THEO. The members of the Theosophical Society at large are free to profess whatever religion or philosophy they like, or none if they so prefer, provided they are in sympathy with and ready to carry out one or more of the three objects of the Association. The Society is a philanthropic and scientific body for the propagation of the idea of brotherhood on *practical* instead of *theoretical* lines. The Fellows may be Christians or Muslims, Jews or Parsees, Buddhists or Brahmins, Spiritualists or Materialists; it does not matter. But every member must be either a philanthropist, a scholar, a researcher of Aryan and other ancient literature, or a student of psychic matters. In short, he has to help, if he can, in carrying out at least one of the objects of the program. Otherwise, he has no reason for becoming a "Fellow." Such is the majority of the exoteric Society, composed of "attached" and "unattached" members.<note>An "attached member" means one who has joined some particular branch of the T.S. An "unattached" member is one who belongs to the Society at large and has a diploma from the Headquarters (Adyar, Madras), but is connected with no branch or lodge.</note> These may, or may not, become

<summary>This page explains that the Theosophical Society does not adhere to any single religion but seeks truth in all of them, focusing on the practical application of universal brotherhood. It also distinguishes between the Society's exoteric and esoteric sections and defines the requirements and types of membership.</summary>
<keywords>Theosophy, Exoteric, Esoteric, Universal Brotherhood, Theosophical Society membership, religious pluralism, Adyar, Madras</keywords>

THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

They are Theosophists *de facto*. They are members by virtue of having joined the Society; but the Society cannot make a Theosophist of someone who has no sense for the *divine fitness* of things, or of someone who understands Theosophy in his own—if the expression may be used—sectarian and egotistical way. “Handsome is as handsome does” could be paraphrased in this case to say: “A Theosophist is one who practices Theosophy.”

### THEOSOPHISTS AND MEMBERS OF THE “T.S.”

ENQ. This applies to lay members, as I understand. And what of those who pursue the esoteric study of Theosophy; are they the real Theosophists?

THEO. Not necessarily, until they have proved themselves to be such. They have entered the inner group and pledged themselves to carry out, as strictly as they can, the rules of the occult body. This is a difficult undertaking, as the foremost rule of all is the entire renunciation of one’s personality—that is, a pledged member has to become a thorough altruist, never to think of himself, and to forget his own vanity and pride in the thought of the good of his fellow creatures, as well as that of his fellow brothers in the esoteric circle. If the esoteric instructions are to benefit him, he has to live a life of abstinence in everything, of self-denial and strict morality, doing his duty to all people. The few real Theosophists in the T.S. are among these members. This does not imply that outside of the T.S. and the inner circle, there are no Theosophists; for there

are, and more than people know of; certainly far more than are found among the lay members of the T.S.

ENQ. Then what is the point of joining the so-called Theosophical Society in that case? Where is the incentive?

THEO. None, except for the advantage of receiving esoteric instructions—the genuine doctrines of the “Wisdom-Religion”—and, if the real program is carried out, deriving much help from mutual aid and sympathy. Union is strength and harmony, and well-regulated simultaneous efforts produce wonders. This has been the secret of all associations and communities since mankind began.

ENQ. But why could a man of well-balanced mind and singleness of purpose—one, say, of indomitable energy and perseverance—not become an Occultist and even an Adept if he works alone?

THEO. He might; but there are ten thousand chances to one that he will fail. One reason among many is that no books on Occultism or Theurgy exist in our day which reveal the secrets of alchemy or medieval Theosophy in plain language. All are symbolic or written in parables; and as the key to these has been lost for ages in the West, how can a man learn the correct meaning of what he is reading and studying? Therein lies the greatest danger, one that leads to unconscious *black magic* or the most helpless *mediumship*. He who does not have an Initiate for a master had better leave this dangerous study alone. Look around you and observe. While two-thirds of civilized society ridicule the mere notion that there is anything in *Theosophy, Occultism, Spiritualism*, or in the *Kabala*, the other third is composed of the most heterogeneous and opposite

Others rush single-handedly into the study of the *Kabala*, *Psychism*, *Mesmerism*, *Spiritualism*, or some form or another of *Mysticism*. The result: no two people think alike, and no two agree upon any fundamental occult principles, though many claim for themselves the *ultima thule* <note>The furthest point or highest degree reachable.</note> of knowledge and would make outsiders believe that they are full-blown adepts. Not only is there no scientific and accurate knowledge of Occultism accessible in the West—not even of true astrology, the only branch of Occultism which, in its exoteric <note>Public or intended for the uninitiated.</note> teachings, has definite laws and a definite system—but no one has any idea of what real Occultism means. Some limit ancient wisdom to the *Kabala* and the Jewish *Zohar*, which each person interprets in his own way according to the dead-letter of the rabbinical methods. Others regard Swedenborg or Boehme as the ultimate expressions of the highest wisdom; while others again see in *mesmerism* the great secret of ancient magic. Every one of those who put their theory into practice is rapidly drifting, through ignorance, into black magic. Those who escape from it are fortunate, as they have neither test nor criterion by which they can distinguish between the true and the false.

**ENQ.** But how is one to know whether the ounce is real gold or only a counterfeit?

**THEO.** A tree is known by its fruit, a system by its results. When our opponents are able to prove to us that any solitary student of <term>Occultism</term> throughout the ages has become a saintly adept like <term>Ammonius Saccas</term>, or even a <term>Plotinus</term>, or a <term>Theurgist</term> like <term>Iamblichus</term>, or achieved feats such as are claimed to have been done by <term>St. Germain</term>, without any master to guide him—and all this without being a medium, a self-deluded psychic, or a charlatan—then we shall confess ourselves mistaken. But until then, <term>Theosophists</term> prefer to follow the proven natural law of the tradition of the <term>Sacred Science</term>. There are mystics who have made great discoveries in chemistry and physical sciences, almost bordering on alchemy and <term>Occultism</term>; others who, by the sole aid of their genius, have rediscovered portions, if not the whole, of the lost alphabets of the “Mystery language,” and are, therefore, able to read <term>Hebrew scrolls</term> correctly; others still, who, being seers, have caught wonderful glimpses of the hidden secrets of Nature. But all these are *specialists*. One is a theoretical inventor, another a Hebrew—that is, a sectarian <term>Kabalist</term>—a third is a <term>Swedenborg</term> of modern times, denying all and everything outside of his own particular science or religion. Not one of them can boast of having produced a universal or even a national benefit through this, not even for himself. With

**ENQ.** Is the production of such healing adepts the aim of <term>Theosophy</term>?

**THEO.** Its aims are several; but the most important of all are those which are likely to lead to the relief of human suffering in any or every form, moral as well as physical. And we believe the former to be far more important than the latter. <term>Theosophy</term> has to teach ethics; it has to purify the soul if it would relieve the physical body, whose ailments—except for cases of accidents—are all hereditary. It is not by studying <term>Occultism</term> for selfish ends, for the gratification of one’s personal ambition, pride, or vanity, that one can ever reach the true goal: that of helping suffering mankind. Nor is it by studying one single branch of the <term>esoteric</term> philosophy that a person becomes an <term>Occultist</term>, but by studying, if not mastering, them all.

**ENQ.** Is help, then, to reach this most important aim, given only to those who study the <term>esoteric</term> sciences?

**THEO.** Not at all. Every lay member is entitled to general instruction if he only wants it; but few are willing to become what are called "working members," and most prefer to remain the

### *THE DIFFERENCE BETWEEN THEOSOPHY AND OCCULTISM.*

**Enq.** You speak of Theosophy and Occultism; are they the same?

**Theo.** Not at all. A person may be a very good Theosophist indeed, whether inside or *outside* of the Society, without being an Occultist in any way. But no one can be a true Occultist without being a real Theosophist; otherwise, he is simply a black magician, whether consciously or unconsciously.

**Enq.** What do you mean?

**Theo.** I have already said that a true Theosophist must practice the highest moral ideals, must strive to realize his unity with all of humanity, and work tirelessly for others. Now, if an Occultist does not do all of this, he must be acting selfishly for his own personal benefit; and if he has acquired more practical power than other ordinary people, he immediately becomes a far more dangerous enemy to the world and those around him than the average mortal. This is clear.

**Enq.** Then is an Occultist simply a person who possesses more power than other people?

**Theo.** Far more—if he is a *practical* and truly learned Occultist, and not one in name only. Occult sciences are *not*, as described in encyclopedias, "those imaginary sciences of the"

<meta>This page continues the definition of Occult sciences, completing the sentence from the previous page which challenged the encyclopedic description of these subjects as "imaginary."</meta>

->THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

Middle Ages which related to the *supposed* action or influence of occult qualities or supernatural powers, such as alchemy, magic, necromancy, and astrology,” for they are real, actual, and very dangerous sciences. They teach the secret potency of things in Nature, developing and cultivating the hidden powers “latent in man,” thereby giving him tremendous advantages over more ignorant mortals. Hypnotism, which has now become so common and a subject of serious scientific inquiry, is a good instance in point. *Hypnotic* power was discovered almost by accident, the way to it having been prepared by mesmerism; and now an able hypnotist can do almost anything with it, from forcing a man, unconsciously to himself, to play the fool, to making him commit a crime—often acting as a proxy for the hypnotist,* and *for the benefit of the latter.* Is this not a terrible power if left in the hands of unscrupulous persons? And please remember that this is only one of the minor branches of Occultism.

**Enq.** But are not all these occult sciences, magic, and sorcery considered by the most cultured and learned people as relics of ancient ignorance and superstition?

**Theo.** Let me remind you that this remark of yours cuts both ways. The “*most cultured* and learned” among you also regard Christianity and every other religion as a relic of ignorance and superstition. People are beginning to believe now, at any rate, in hypnotism, and some—even of the *most cultured*—in Theosophy and phenomena. But who among them, except preachers and blind fanatics, will admit to a belief in *Biblical* miracles? And this is where the point of difference comes in.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of occult sciences, distinguishing between a general Theosophist's faith and an Occultist's scientific practice, before transitioning into a critique of Spiritualism.</meta>

->THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

There are very good and pure Theosophists who may believe in the supernatural, including divine miracles, but no Occultist will do so. For an Occultist practices *scientific* Theosophy, based on accurate knowledge of Nature’s secret workings; but a Theosophist, practicing the powers called abnormal *without* the light of Occultism, will simply tend toward a dangerous form of mediumship, because, although holding to Theosophy and its highest conceivable code of ethics, he practices it in the dark, based on sincere but *blind* faith. Anyone, Theosophist or Spiritualist, who attempts to cultivate one of the branches of Occult science—for example, Hypnotism, Mesmerism, or even the secrets of producing physical phenomena, etc.—without the knowledge of the philosophical rationale of those powers, is like a rudderless boat launched on a stormy ocean.

### THE DIFFERENCE BETWEEN THEOSOPHY AND SPIRITUALISM.

**Enq.** But do you not believe in Spiritualism?
**Theo.** If by “Spiritualism” you mean the explanation which Spiritualists give for some abnormal phenomena, then we decidedly do not. They maintain that these manifestations are all produced by the “spirits” of deceased mortals, generally their relatives, who return to earth, they say, to communicate with those they have loved or to whom they are attached. We deny this point-blank. We assert that the spirits of the dead cannot return to earth—except in rare and exceptional cases, which I may speak of later; nor do they communicate with men except by *entirely subjective means*. That which

**Enq.** Do you reject the phenomena as well?

**Theo.** Certainly not—except for cases of deliberate fraud.

**Enq.** How do you account for them, then?

**Theo.** In many ways. The causes of such manifestations are not nearly as simple as Spiritualists would like to believe. Most importantly, the *deus ex machina* <note>The driving force or "god from the machine."</note> of so-called "materializations" is usually the astral body or "double" of the medium or of someone present. This astral body is also the creator or operating force in the manifestations of slate-writing, "Davenport"-style <note>Referring to the Davenport brothers, famous 19th-century mediums known for their "spirit cabinet" performances.</note> manifestations, and so on.

**Enq.** You say "usually"; then what produces the rest?

**Theo.** That depends on the nature of the manifestations. Sometimes they are the astral remains, the *Kamalokic* "*shells*" of the vanished *personalities* that once were; at other times, they are Elementals. "*Spirit*" is a word with many broad meanings. I really do not know what Spiritualists mean by the term; but we understand them to claim that physical phenomena are produced by the reincarnating *Ego*, the *Spiritual* and immortal "*individuality*." We entirely reject this hypothesis. The *Conscious Individuality* of the deceased cannot materialize, nor can it return from its own mental *Devachanic* sphere to the plane of earthly objectivity.

**Enq.** But many of the communications received from the "spirits" show not only intelligence, but a knowledge of facts unknown to—

Theosophy rejects the idea that the conscious, reincarnating Ego can return to the physical world from the Devachanic sphere.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the source of intelligence in spiritualist communications, moving from the idea of "spirit" influence to the Theosophical explanation of the "inner Self."</meta>

<page-num>29</page-num>
->THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

...the medium, and sometimes not even consciously present in the mind of the investigator or any of those who make up the audience.

**THEO.** This does not necessarily prove that the intelligence and knowledge you speak of belong to spirits or come from *disembodied* souls. Sleepwalkers have been known to compose music and poetry and to solve mathematical problems while in a trance state, without ever having learned music or mathematics. Others answered intelligently to questions put to them and even, in several cases, spoke languages—such as Hebrew and Latin—of which they were entirely ignorant when awake; all this in a state of profound sleep. Will you, then, maintain that this was caused by "spirits"?

**ENQ.** But how would you explain it?

**THEO.** We assert that because the divine spark in man is one and identical in its essence with the Universal Spirit, our "spiritual Self" is practically omniscient, but it cannot manifest its knowledge due to the obstacles of matter. Now, the more these obstacles are removed—in other words, the more the physical body is paralyzed regarding its own independent activity and consciousness, as in deep sleep, a deep trance, or even in illness—the more fully the *inner Self* can manifest on this plane. This is our explanation for those truly wonderful phenomena of a higher order in which undeniable intelligence and knowledge are shown. As for the lower order of manifestations, such as physical phenomena and the cliches and common talk of the typical "spirit," to explain even the most important of the teachings we hold on the subject—

<summary>The Theosophist argues that extraordinary intelligence shown in trances comes from the inherently omniscient "inner Self" rather than external spirits, as the physical body's limitations are bypassed during sleep or illness.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the source of intelligence in spiritualist communications. It completes the previous page's final sentence regarding the space required to explain Theosophical teachings and transitions into the Theosophical view on mediums and the state of the Ego during sleep or trance.</meta>

<page-num>30</page-num>

-># THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

—would take up more space and time than can be assigned to it at present. We have no desire to interfere with the beliefs of the <term>Spiritualists</term> any more than with any other belief. The *onus probandi* <note>burden of proof</note> must fall on those who believe in "spirits." And at the present moment, while still convinced that the higher sort of manifestations occur through disembodied souls, their leaders and the most learned and intelligent among the Spiritualists are the first to confess that not *all* the phenomena are produced by spirits. Gradually they will come to recognize the whole truth; but meanwhile, we have no right nor desire to convert them to our views. This is especially true since, in cases of purely psychic and spiritual manifestations, we believe in the intercommunication of the spirit of the living person with that of disembodied personalities.*

---

* We say that in such cases it is not the spirits of the dead who descend to earth, but the spirits of the living that ascend to the pure <term>Spiritual Souls</term>. In truth, there is neither ascending nor descending, but a change of state or condition for the <term>medium</term>. As the body of the medium becomes paralyzed or "entranced," the <term>spiritual Ego</term> is freed from its restraints and finds itself on the same plane of consciousness as the disembodied spirits. Therefore, if there is any spiritual attraction between the two, they can communicate, as often occurs in dreams. The difference between a <term>mediumistic</term> and a <term>non-sensitive</term> nature is this: the liberated spirit of a medium has the opportunity and ease of influencing the passive organs of its entranced physical body, making them act, speak, and write at its will. The <term>EGO</term> can make the body repeat, like an echo and in human language, the thoughts and ideas of the disembodied entity as well as its own. But the *non-receptive* or *non-sensitive* organism of someone who is very "positive" <note>psychically resistant or active rather than passive</note> cannot be influenced in this way. Therefore, although there is hardly a human being whose <term>Ego</term> does not hold free intercourse, during the sleep of the body, with those whom it loved and lost, yet—because of the positiveness and non-receptivity of the physical envelope and brain—no recollection, or only a very dim, dream-like remembrance, lingers in the memory of the person once they are awake.

<summary>The author explains that while some spiritualist phenomena involve disembodied souls, it is actually the living spirit that "ascends" to meet them during a trance. The difference between a medium and a non-sensitive person lies in the ability of the physical brain to receive and remember these spiritual communications.</summary>

<keywords>Spiritualism, mediumship, Spiritual Ego, consciousness, trance states, communication with the dead, psychic sensitivity, Theosophy</keywords>

-># THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

ENQUIRER: Does this mean that you reject the philosophy of Spiritualism entirely?

THEOSOPHIST: If by "philosophy" you mean their crude theories, yes, we do.

In truth, they have no philosophy. Their best, most intellectual, and most earnest defenders say as much. Their fundamental and only undeniable truth—namely, that phenomena occur through mediums controlled by invisible forces and intelligences—cannot and will not be denied by anyone, except a blind materialist of the "Huxley big toe" school <note>A mocking reference to Thomas Henry Huxley and the materialist view that the soul or consciousness is merely a product of physical matter, no more significant than a bodily appendage.</note>. Regarding their philosophy, however, let me read to you what the capable editor of *Light* says of them and their philosophy; the Spiritualists will find no wiser or more devoted champion than him. This is what "M.A. Oxon." <note>The pen name of William Stainton Moses, a prominent Spiritualist and cleric.</note>, one of the very few philosophical Spiritualists, writes regarding their lack of organization and blind bigotry:

> It is worthwhile to look steadily at this point, for it is of vital importance. We have an experience and a knowledge compared to which all other knowledge is relatively insignificant. The ordinary Spiritualist grows angry if anyone ventures to challenge his assured knowledge of the future and his absolute certainty of the life to come. Where other men have stretched out feeble hands, groping into the dark future, he walks boldly as one who has a map and knows his way. Where other men have stopped short at a pious hope or have been content with an inherited faith, he boasts that he knows what they only believe, and that from his rich stores of evidence, he can supplement the fading faiths built only on hope. He is magnificent in how he deals with humanity's most cherished expectations. "You hope," he seems to say, "for that which I can demonstrate. You have accepted a traditional belief in what I can experimentally prove according to the strictest scientific method. The old beliefs are fading; come out from them and be separate. They contain as much falsehood as truth. Only by building on a sure foundation—"

> "When one comes to deal with this magnificent person in a practical way, what is the result? It is very curious and very disappointing. He is so sure of his ground that he takes no trouble to ascertain the interpretation that others put upon his facts. The wisdom of the ages has concerned itself with the explanation of what he rightly regards as proven; but he does not turn even a passing glance toward its researches. He does not even agree altogether with his fellow Spiritualist. It is the same story once again of the elderly Scottish woman who, together with her husband, formed a 'church.' They had exclusive keys to Heaven—or, rather, she had, for she was 'not certain about Jamie.' So the infinitely divided, subdivided, and re-subdivided sects of Spiritualists shake their heads and are 'not certain about' one another. Again, the collective experience of mankind is solid and unchanging on this point: that union is strength, and disunion is a source of weakness and failure. Shoulder to shoulder, drilled and disciplined, a rabble becomes an army, each man a match for a hundred of the untrained men that may be brought against it. Organization in every department of human work means success, saving of time and labor, profit, and development. Lack of method, lack of plan, haphazard work, fitful energy, undisciplined effort—these mean bungling failure. The voice of humanity attests to this truth. Does the Spiritualist accept the verdict and act on the conclusion? Truly, no. He refuses to organize. He is a law unto himself and a thorn in the side of his neighbors." — *Light*, June 22, 1889.

ENQUIRER: I was told that the Theosophical Society was originally founded to crush Spiritualism and the belief in the survival of individuality in man?

THEOSOPHIST: You are misinformed. Our beliefs are all founded on that immortal individuality. However, like so many others, you confuse personality with individuality. Your Western psychologists do not seem to have established any clear distinction...

between the two. Yet it is precisely that difference which provides the keynote for understanding Eastern philosophy, and which lies at the root of the divergence between Theosophical and Spiritualist teachings. And though it may draw even more hostility toward us from some Spiritualists, I must state here that it is Theosophy which is the *true* and unalloyed Spiritualism, while the modern movement of that name is, as currently practiced by the masses, simply transcendental materialism.

ENQ. Please explain your idea more clearly.

THEO. What I mean is that although our teachings insist upon the identity of spirit and matter, and although we say that spirit is *potential* matter, and matter is simply crystallized spirit (*for example*, as ice is solidified steam), yet since the original and eternal condition of *all* is not spirit but *meta-spirit*, so to speak (visible and solid matter being simply its periodic manifestation), we maintain that the term spirit can only be applied to the *true individuality*.

ENQ. But what is the distinction between this "true individuality" and the "I" or "Ego" of which we are all conscious?

THEO. Before I can answer you, we must agree on what you mean by "I" or "Ego." We distinguish between the simple fact of self-consciousness—the simple feeling that "I am I"—and the complex thought that "I am Mr. Smith" or "Mrs. Brown." Because we believe in a series of births for the same Ego, or *reincarnation*, this distinction is the fundamental pivot of the whole idea. You see, "Mr. Smith" really means a long series of daily experiences strung together by the thread of

We Theosophists, therefore, distinguish between this bundle of "experiences," which we call the *false* (because it is so finite and fleeting) personality, and that element in man to which the feeling of "I am I" is due. It is this "I am I" which we call the *true* individuality; and we say that this "Ego" or individuality plays, like an actor, many parts on the stage of life.* Let us call every new life on earth of the same *Ego* a night on the stage of a theater. One night the actor, or "Ego," appears as "Macbeth," the next as "Shylock," the third as "Romeo," the fourth as "Hamlet" or "King Lear," and so on, until he has run through the whole cycle of incarnations. The Ego begins his life-pilgrimage as a sprite, such as "Ariel" or "Puck"; he plays the part of an extra, acts as a soldier, a servant, or one of the chorus; he then rises to "speaking parts" and plays leading *roles*, interspersed with insignificant parts, until he finally retires from the stage as "Prospero," the magician.

<term>ENQ.</term> I understand. You say, then, that this true *Ego* cannot return to earth after death. But surely the actor is free, if he has preserved the sense of his individuality, to return if he likes to the scene of his former actions?

<term>THEO.</term> We say he cannot, simply because such a return to earth would be incompatible with any state of pure bliss after death,

### WHY IS THEOSOPHY ACCEPTED?

<term>ENQ.</term> I understand to a certain extent; but I see that your teachings are far more complicated and metaphysical than either Spiritualism or current religious thought. Can you tell me, then, what has caused this system of Theosophy which you support to arouse so much interest and so much hostility at the same time?

<term>THEO.</term> I believe there are several reasons for it. Among other causes that may be mentioned, *firstly*, there is the great reaction against the grossly materialistic theories now prevalent among scientific teachers. *Secondly*, there is a general dissatisfaction with the man-made theology of the various Christian Churches and the ever-increasing number of conflicting sects. *Thirdly*, there is an ever-growing perception that creeds which are so obviously self-contradictory and mutually exclusive cannot be true, and that unproven claims cannot be real. This natural distrust of conventional religions is only strengthened by their complete failure to maintain morality and to purify society and the masses. *Fourthly*, there is a conviction held by many, and knowledge possessed by a few, that there must be a philosophical and religious system somewhere that is scientific

**ENQ.** But how did this system come to be put forward just now?

**THEO.** Simply because the time was found to be ripe. This fact is shown by the determined effort of so many earnest students to reach *the truth*, at whatever cost and wherever it may be concealed. Seeing this, its custodians permitted at least some portions of that truth to be proclaimed. Had the formation of the Theosophical Society been postponed a few years longer, one half of the civilized nations would have become rank materialists by this time, and the other half anthropomorphists and phenomenalists.

**ENQ.** Are we to regard Theosophy in any way as a revelation?

**THEO.** In no way whatever—not even in the sense of a new and direct disclosure from some higher, supernatural, or at least *superhuman beings*. It is a revelation only in the sense of an “unveiling” of old, very old truths to minds previously ignorant of them—ignorant even of the existence and preservation of any such archaic knowledge.*

**ENQ.** You spoke of "Persecution." If truth is as Theosophy represents it, why has it met with such opposition and not been generally accepted?

**THEO.** Again, for many and various reasons, one of which is the hatred people feel for "innovations," as they call them. Selfishness is essentially conservative and hates being disturbed. It prefers an easy-going, undemanding *lie* to the greatest truth, if the latter requires the sacrifice of even one's smallest comfort. The power of mental inertia is great in anything that does not promise immediate benefit and reward. Our age is pre-eminently unspiritual and matter-of-fact. Moreover, there is the unfamiliar character of Theosophical teachings; the highly complex and obscure nature of the doctrines, some of which flatly contradict many of the human whims cherished by sectarians, which have eaten into the very core of popular beliefs. If we add to this the personal effort and great purity of life required of those who would become disciples of the *inner circle*, and the very limited class to which an entirely unselfish code appeals, it will be easy to see why Theosophy is doomed to such slow, difficult work. It is essentially the philosophy of those who suffer and have lost all hope of being helped out of the mire of life by any other means. Moreover, the history of any system of belief or morals newly introduced into a foreign soil shows that its beginnings were impeded by

Plato regarded the mysteries as most sacred, and Clemens Alexandrinus, who had himself been initiated into the Eleusinian mysteries, declared "that the doctrines taught there contained the end of all human knowledge." We wonder: were Plato and Clemens two scoundrels or two fools—or both?</note>

**ENQ.** All this refers more to the ethics and philosophy of the <term>T.S.</term> Can you give me a general idea of the Society itself, its objects, and its statutes?

**THEO.** This was never kept secret. Ask, and you shall receive accurate answers.

**ENQ.** But I heard that you were bound by pledges?

**THEO.** Only in the *<term>Arcane</term>* or "Esoteric" Section.

**ENQ.** And also, that some members after leaving did not consider themselves bound by them. Are they right?

**THEO.** This shows that their idea of honor is an imperfect one. How can they be right? As was well stated in *The Path*, our theosophical publication in New York, when dealing with such a case:

> "Suppose that a soldier is tried for breaking his oath and discipline, and is dismissed from the service. In his rage at the justice he has brought upon himself, and of whose penalties he was clearly forewarned, the soldier turns to the enemy with false information—becoming a spy and a traitor—as revenge against his former Chief, and claims that his punishment has released him from his oath of loyalty to a cause."

Do you think he is justified? Don't you think he deserves to be called a dishonorable man, a coward?

**ENQ.** I believe so; but some think otherwise.

**THEO.** So much the worse for them. But we will talk about this subject later, if you please.

### III.

## THE WORKING SYSTEM OF THE T.S.<note>See the official rules of the T.S. in Appendix A at the end. Note well: “T.S.” is an abbreviation for “Theosophical Society.”</note>

---

->*THE OBJECTIVES OF THE SOCIETY.*<-

<term>ENQ.</term> What are the objectives of the "Theosophical Society"?

<term>THEO.</term> There are three, and they have been the same from the beginning. 
(1.) To form the nucleus of a Universal Brotherhood of Humanity without distinction of race, color, or creed. (2.) To promote the study of <term>Aryan</term> and other scriptures, of world religions and sciences, and to uphold the importance of ancient Asiatic literature—specifically the Brahmanical, Buddhist, and Zoroastrian philosophies. (3.) To investigate the hidden mysteries of Nature in every possible aspect, and especially the psychic and spiritual powers latent in humanity. These are, broadly stated, the three chief objectives of the Theosophical Society.

<term>ENQ.</term> Can you give me some more detailed information about these?

<term>THEO.</term> We may divide each of the three objectives into as many explanatory clauses as may be found necessary.

<term>ENQ.</term> Then let us begin with the first objective. What methods would you use to promote such a feeling of brotherhood among races that are known to have the most diverse religions, customs, beliefs, and ways of thinking?

<term>THEO.</term> Allow me to add what you seem hesitant to say. Of course, we know that—with the exception of two remnants of ancient races, the Parsees and the Jews—every nation is divided not only against all other nations, but even against itself. This is most obvious among the so-called civilized Christian nations. This explains your wonder and the reason why our first objective appears to you as a Utopia. Is it not so?

<term>ENQ.</term> Well, yes; but what do you have to say against that view?

<term>THEO.</term> Nothing against the fact itself, but much regarding the necessity of removing the causes that make Universal Brotherhood a Utopia at present.

<term>THEO.</term> First and foremost is the natural selfishness of human nature. This selfishness, instead of being rooted out, is daily strengthened and stimulated into a fierce and unstoppable feeling by current religious education, which tends not only to encourage but actually to justify it. People's ideas about right and wrong have been entirely distorted by the literal acceptance of the Jewish Bible. All the unselfishness of the altruistic teachings of Jesus has become merely a theoretical subject for preaching from the pulpit, while the principles of practical selfishness taught in the Mosaic Bible—against which Christ preached so unsuccessfully—have become deeply embedded in the inner life of Western nations. ### *THE COMMON ORIGIN OF MAN.*

<term>ENQ.</term> How?
<term>THEO.</term> Simply by demonstrating on logical, philosophical, metaphysical, and even scientific grounds that:—(a) All men have the same spiritual and physical origin, which is the fundamental teaching of *Theosophy*. (b) As mankind is essentially of one and the same essence, and that essence is one—infinite, uncreated, and eternal, whether we call it *God* or *Nature*—nothing, therefore, can affect one nation or one man without affecting all other nations and all other men. This is as certain and as obvious as the fact that a stone thrown into a pond will, sooner or later, set in motion every single drop of water in it.
<term>ENQ.</term> But this is not the teaching of Christ, but rather a pantheistic notion.
<term>THEO.</term> That is where your mistake lies. It is purely *Christian*, although *not Judaic*, and therefore, perhaps, your Biblical nations prefer to ignore it.
<term>ENQ.</term> This is a wholesale and unjust accusation. Where are your proofs for such a statement?
<term>THEO.</term> They are ready at hand. Christ is alleged to have said: “Love each other” and “Love your enemies”; for “if you love

ENQ. I have heard you say that science proves the identity of our physical origin, while the Wisdom-Religion proves that of our spiritual origin. Yet we do not see Darwinists showing much brotherly love.

THEO. Exactly. This is what demonstrates the failure of materialistic systems and proves that we Theosophists are right. A shared physical origin does not appeal to our higher and deeper feelings. Matter, when stripped of its soul and spirit—or its divine essence—cannot speak to the human heart. But the identity of the soul and spirit of the real, immortal human, as Theosophy teaches us, would lead us far toward true charity and brotherly goodwill once it is proven and deep-rooted in our hearts.

ENQ. But how does Theosophy explain the common origin of humanity?

THEO. By teaching that the *root* of all nature, both objective and subjective, and everything else in the universe, visible and invisible, *is, was, and ever will be* one absolute essence, from which everything starts and to which everything returns. This is Aryan philosophy, fully represented only by the Vedantins,

and the Buddhist system. With this object in view, it is the duty of all Theosophists to promote in every practical way, and in all countries, the spread of *non-sectarian* education.

ENQ. What do the written statutes of your Society advise its members to do besides this? On the physical plane, I mean?

THEO. In order to awaken brotherly feeling among nations, we have to assist in the international exchange of useful arts and products through advice, information, and cooperation with all worthy individuals and associations (provided, however, as the statutes add, "that no benefit or percentage shall be taken by the Society or the 'Fellows' for its or their corporate services"). For instance, to take a practical illustration: the organization of society depicted by Edward Bellamy in his magnificent work "Looking Backwards" admirably represents the Theosophical idea of what should be the first great step toward the full realization of universal brotherhood. The state of things he depicts falls short of perfection because selfishness still exists and operates in the hearts of men. But in the main, selfishness and individualism have been overcome by the feeling of solidarity and mutual brotherhood; and the scheme of life described there reduces the causes that tend to create and foster selfishness to a minimum.

ENQ. Then as a Theosophist, will you take part in an effort to realize such an ideal?

THEO. Certainly; and we have proved it by action. Have you not heard of the Nationalist clubs and party which have sprung up in America since the publication of Bellamy's book? They are now coming prominently to the front and will continue to do so

more and more as time goes on. Well, these clubs and this party were originally started by Theosophists. One of the first, the Nationalist Club of Boston, Massachusetts, has Theosophists as President and Secretary, and the majority of its executive board belong to the T.S. <note>Theosophical Society</note> In the constitution of all their clubs, and of the party they are forming, the influence of Theosophy and of the Society is clear, for they all take as their basis—their first and fundamental principle—the Brotherhood of Humanity as taught by Theosophy. In their declaration of Principles, they state: “The principle of the Brotherhood of Humanity is one of the eternal truths that govern the world’s progress on lines which distinguish human nature from animal nature.” What can be more Theosophical than this? But it is not enough. What is also needed is to impress upon people the idea that, if the root of mankind is *one*, then there must also be one truth which finds expression in all the various religions—except in the Jewish religion, as you do not find it expressed even in the Kabala.

**ENQ.** This refers to the common origin of religions, and you may be right there. But how does it apply to practical brotherhood on the physical plane?

**THEO.** First, because that which *is* true on the metaphysical plane must also be true on the physical plane. Secondly, because there is no more fertile source of hatred and strife than religious differences. When one party or another considers themselves the sole possessor of absolute truth, it becomes only natural that they should think their neighbor is absolutely gripped by Error or the Devil. But once a person is led to see that none of

<meta>This page continues the discussion on religious brotherhood and the interconnectedness of humanity, moving from metaphysical truths to a physical analogy of human unity.</meta>

<page-num>46</page-num>
<header>THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.</header>

none of them has the *whole* truth, but that they are mutually complementary, and that the complete truth can be found only in the combined views of all, after that which is false in each of them has been sifted out—then true brotherhood in religion will be established. The same applies in the physical world.

**ENQ.** Please explain further.

**THEO.** Take an example. A plant consists of a root, a stem, and many shoots and leaves. Just as humanity, as a whole, is the stem which grows from the spiritual root, the stem is the unity of the plant. Hurt the stem and it is obvious that every shoot and leaf will suffer. It is the same with mankind.

**ENQ.** Yes, but if you injure a leaf or a shoot, you do not injure the whole plant.

**THEO.** And therefore you think that by injuring *one* person you do not injure humanity? But how do *you* know? Are you aware that even materialistic science teaches that any injury, however slight, to a plant will affect the whole course of its future growth and development? Therefore, you are mistaken, and the analogy is perfect. If, however, you overlook the fact that a cut in the finger may often make the whole body suffer and react on the entire nervous system, I must all the more remind you that there may well be other spiritual laws operating on plants and animals as well as on mankind. Although you do not recognize their action on plants and animals, you may deny their existence.

**ENQ.** What laws do you mean?

**THEO.** We call them <term>Karmic laws</term>; but you will not understand the full meaning of the term unless you study <term>Occultism</term>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the interconnectedness of humanity, using the analogy of a plant to argue that harm to one individual affects the whole, before transitioning to the practical methods of the Theosophical Society's second object.</meta>

<page-num>47</page-num>
<header>THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.</header>

However, my argument did not rest on the assumption of these laws, but really on the analogy of the plant. Expand the idea, apply it universally, and you will soon find that in true philosophy every physical action has its moral and everlasting effect. Hurt a man by doing him bodily harm; you may think that his pain and suffering cannot spread by any means to his neighbors, and least of all to people of other nations. We affirm that it *will*, in due time. Therefore, we say that unless every person is brought to understand and accept as an axiomatic truth that by wronging one person we wrong not only ourselves but the whole of humanity in the long run, no brotherly feelings such as those preached by all the great Reformers—most notably by Buddha and Jesus—are possible on earth.

### OUR OTHER OBJECTS.

**ENQ.** Will you now explain the methods by which you propose to carry out the second object?

**THEO.** To collect for the library at our headquarters in Adyar, Madras (and for the local libraries of the Fellows of their branches), all the good works we can regarding the world's religions. To put into written form correct information regarding various ancient philosophies, traditions, and legends, and spread this in practical ways, such as the translation and publication of original works of value, along with extracts and commentaries, or through the oral instruction of individuals learned in their respective fields.

<summary>The author explains that harming any individual eventually impacts all of humanity, arguing that universal brotherhood requires recognizing this interconnectedness. --- Page 64 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the methods of the Theosophical Society, moving from the dissemination of ancient philosophies to the third object: the development of latent spiritual powers and the study of nature's hidden laws.</meta>

<page-num>48</page-num>
<header>THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.</header>

**ENQ.** And what about the third object: to develop in humanity its latent spiritual or psychic powers?

**THEO.** This must also be achieved through publications in those places where lectures and personal teachings are not possible. Our duty is to keep alive the spiritual intuition in humanity. We must oppose and counteract—after proper investigation and proof of its irrational nature—bigotry in every form, whether religious, scientific, or social, and *hypocrisy* above all, whether it appears as religious sectarianism or as a belief in miracles or anything supernatural. What we must do is seek to obtain *knowledge of all the laws of nature and spread it.* We should encourage the study of those laws least understood by modern people—the so-called *Occult Sciences*—which are based on the *true knowledge of nature* instead of, as is currently the case, on *superstitious beliefs based on blind faith and authority*. Popular folklore and traditions, however fanciful they may be at times, may lead to the discovery of long-lost but important secrets of nature when they are carefully examined. The Society, therefore, aims to pursue this line of inquiry in the hope of widening the field of scientific and philosophical observation.

### *ON THE SACREDNESS OF THE PLEDGE*

**THEO.** The ethics are there, ready and clear enough for anyone who would follow them. They are the essence and the best of the world’s ethics, gathered from the teachings of all the world’s
