great reformers. Therefore, you will find represented in them Confucius and Zoroaster, Laotze and the Bhagavad Gita, the precepts of Gautama Buddha and Jesus of Nazareth, of Hillel and his school, as well as Pythagoras, Socrates, Plato, and their schools.

**ENQ.** Do the members of your Society carry out these precepts? I have heard of great dissensions and quarrels among them.

**THEO.** Very naturally, since although the reform (in its present shape) may be called new, the men and women to be reformed have the same human, sinning natures as of old. As already said, the earnest working members are few; but many are sincere and well-disposed people who try their best to live up to the Society's and their own ideals. Our duty is to encourage and assist individual fellows in self-improvement—intellectual, moral, and spiritual—not to blame or condemn those who fail. We have, strictly speaking, no right to refuse admission to anyone—especially in the *Esoteric Section* of the Society, where “he who enters is as one newly born.” But if any member, despite his sacred pledges on his word of honor and immortal *Self*, chooses to continue after that “new birth” as a new man with the vices or defects of his old life, and still indulges in them in the Society, then, of course, he is more than likely to be asked to resign and withdraw; or, in case of his refusal, to be expelled. We have the strictest rules for such emergencies.

**ENQ.** Can some of them be mentioned?

**THEO.** They can. To begin with, no Fellow in the Society, whether *exoteric* or *esoteric*, has a right to force his personal opinions

As for the inner section, now called the <term>Esoteric</term>, the following rules were established and adopted as far back as 1880: “No <term>Fellow</term> shall use for selfish purposes any knowledge shared with him by any member of the first section (now a higher ‘degree’); violating this rule is punished by expulsion.” Now, however, before any such knowledge can be shared, the applicant must bind himself by a solemn oath not to use it for selfish purposes, nor to reveal anything said except by permission.

**ENQ.** But if a person is expelled from, or resigns from, the section, is he free to reveal anything he may have learned, or to break any part of the pledge he has taken?

**THEO.** Certainly not. His expulsion or resignation only releases him from the obligation of obedience to the teacher, and from

<meta>This page continues the discussion regarding the binding nature of the pledge of secrecy for members who leave or are expelled from the Esoteric Section.</meta>

that of taking an active part in the work of the <term>Society</term>, but surely not from the sacred pledge of secrecy.

**ENQ.** But is this reasonable and just?

**THEO.** Most assuredly. To any man or woman with the slightest sense of honor, a pledge of secrecy taken even on one’s *word of honor*—and much more so to one’s <term>Higher Self</term>, the God within—is binding until death. And though he may leave the Section and the <term>Society</term>, no man or woman of honor will think of attacking or injuring a body to which he or she has been so pledged.

**ENQ.** But is this not going rather far?

**THEO.** Perhaps so, according to the low standards of the present time and morality. But if it does not bind as far as this, what use is a *pledge* at all? How can anyone expect to be taught secret knowledge if he is free to release himself from all the obligations he has taken whenever he pleases? What security, confidence, or trust would ever exist among people if pledges such as this were to have no real binding force at all? Believe me, the law of retribution (<term>Karma</term>) would very soon overtake one who so broke his pledge, and perhaps as quickly as the contempt of every honorable person would, even on this physical plane. As well expressed in the New York “*Path*” just cited on this subject, “*A pledge, once taken, is forever binding in both the moral and the occult worlds. If we break it once and are punished, that does not justify us in breaking it again, and as long as we do, so long will the mighty lever of the Law (of Karma) react upon us.*” (The *Path*, July, 1889.)

# IV.

## THE RELATIONS OF THE THEOSOPHICAL SOCIETY TO THEOSOPHY

->*ON SELF-IMPROVEMENT.*<-

ENQ. Is moral elevation, then, the principal thing emphasized in your <term>Society</term>?

THEO. Undoubtedly! He who wants to be a true <term>Theosophist</term> must bring himself to live as one.

ENQ. If so, then, as I remarked before, the behavior of some members strangely contradicts this fundamental rule.

THEO. Indeed it does. But this cannot be helped among us, any more than among those who call themselves Christians and act like fiends. This is no fault of our statutes and rules, but that of human nature. Even in some exoteric public branches, the members pledge themselves on their “<term>Higher Self</term>” to live *the life prescribed by <term>Theosophy</term>*. They have to bring their *<term>Divine Self</term>* to guide their every thought and action, every day and at every moment of their lives. A true <term>Theosophist</term> ought “to deal justly and walk humbly.”

ENQ. What do you mean by this?

<summary>The author explains that the primary goal of the Theosophical Society is moral self-improvement, requiring members to align their daily actions with their "Higher Self." It is acknowledged that while some members fail to live up to these standards, this is due to human nature rather than any flaw in the Society's principles.</summary>

<keywords>Theosophical Society, Theosophy, self-improvement, Higher Self, Divine Self, ethics, human nature, moral elevation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Section IV, focusing on the concept of self-improvement through altruism and the "Higher Self." The Theosophist expands on the moral requirements for members, emphasizing that true progress comes from working for others rather than for oneself.</meta>

<header>THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.</header>

**THEO.** Simply this: the one self has to forget itself for the many selves. Let me answer you in the words of a true Philaletheian, an <term>F.T.S.</term>, who has beautifully expressed it in the *Theosophist*: “What every person needs first is to find himself, and then take an honest inventory of his subjective possessions; and, however bad or bankrupt it may be, it is not beyond redemption if we set about it in earnest.” But how many actually do? All are willing to work for their own development and progress; very few for the progress of others. To quote the same writer again: “People have been deceived and deluded long enough; they must break their idols, put away their shams, and go to work for themselves—no, there is one little word too many there, for he who works only for himself had better not work at all; rather, let him work for others, for everyone. For every flower of love and charity he plants in his neighbor’s garden, a loathsome weed will disappear from his own, and so this garden of the gods—Humanity—shall blossom like a rose. In all Bibles and all religions, this is plainly set forth—but scheming men first misinterpreted and finally weakened, materialized, and corrupted them. It does not require a new revelation. Let every person be a revelation to themselves. Once let a person's immortal spirit take possession of the temple of his body, driving out the money-changers and every unclean thing, and his own divine humanity will redeem him; for when he is thus at one with himself, he will know the ‘builder of the Temple.’”

**ENQ.** This is pure altruism, I confess.

**THEO.** It is. And if only one Fellow of the <term>T.S.</term> out of ten would practice it, ours would indeed be a body of the elect. But there

...are those among the outsiders who will always refuse to see
the essential difference between Theosophy and the Theosophical
Society—the idea and its imperfect embodiment. Such people

on the pure spirit which sheds its divine light upon it. Is this
fair to either? They throw stones at an association that tries
to work toward, and for the propagation of, its ideal with

reveling in high places, as under the Roman Empire during its
decadence. Fair-minded people, at any rate, ought to remember
that the person who does all he can does as much as the one who
has achieved the most in this world of relative possibilities.
This is a simple truism, an axiom supported for believers in the
Gospels by the parable of the talents told by their Master:
the servant who doubled his *two* talents was rewarded as much
as that other fellow-servant who had received *five*. To every
person it is given “according to his individual ability.”

### *THE ABSTRACT AND THE CONCRETE.*

ENQ. Please clarify this difference a little more.

THEO. The Society is a large body of men and women, composed of the most diverse elements. <term>Theosophy</term>, in its abstract meaning, is Divine Wisdom, or the sum of the knowledge and wisdom that underlies the Universe—the unity of eternal GOOD; and in its concrete sense, it is the sum total of that same wisdom as allotted to humans by nature on this earth, and no more. Some members earnestly endeavor to realize and, so to speak, to embody <term>Theosophy</term> in their lives, while others desire only to know about it, not to practice it. Others still may have joined the Society merely out of curiosity, or a passing interest, or perhaps, again, because some of their friends belong to it. How, then, can the system be judged by the standard of those who would take the name without any right to it? Is poetry or its muse to be measured only by those would-be poets who offend our ears? The Society can be regarded as the embodiment of <term>Theosophy</term> only in its abstract motives; it can never presume to call itself its

concrete vehicle so long as human imperfections and weaknesses are all represented in its body; otherwise, the Society would only be repeating the great error and the resulting sacrileges of the so-called Churches of Christ. If Eastern comparisons may be permitted, <term>Theosophy</term> is the shoreless ocean of universal truth, love, and wisdom, reflecting its radiance on the earth, while the <term>Theosophical Society</term> is only a visible bubble on that reflection. <term>Theosophy</term> is divine nature, visible and invisible, and its Society is human nature trying to ascend to its divine parent. <term>Theosophy</term>, finally, is the fixed eternal sun, and its Society is the fleeting comet trying to settle into an orbit to become a planet, ever revolving within the attraction of the sun of truth. It was formed to assist in showing people that such a thing as <term>Theosophy</term> exists, and to help them ascend toward it by studying and internalizing its eternal truths.

ENQ. I thought you said you had no tenets or doctrines of your own?

THEO. We do not. The Society has no wisdom of its own to support or teach. It is simply the storehouse of all the truths uttered by the great <term>seers</term>, <term>initiates</term>, and <term>prophets</term> of historic and even prehistoric ages—at least, as many as it can obtain. Therefore, it is merely the channel through which a portion of the truth, found in the collective teachings of humanity’s great teachers, is poured out into the world.

reached by isolated individuals, though never without the guidance of a master at first. But most of their followers, when they became masters in their turn, diminished the universality of these teachings into the narrow groove of their own sectarian dogmas. The commandments of *a* chosen master alone were then adopted and followed to the exclusion of all others—if they were followed at all, take note, as in the case of the <term>Sermon on the Mount</term>. Each religion is thus a fragment of the divine truth, made to focus a vast panorama of human imagination which claimed to represent and replace that truth.

ENQ. But <term>Theosophy</term>, you say, is not a religion?

THEO. Most assuredly it is not, since it is the essence of all religion and of absolute truth, a drop of which only underlies every creed. To resort once more to metaphor: <term>Theosophy</term>, on earth, is like the white ray of the <term>spectrum</term>, and every religion is only one of the seven <term>prismatic colors</term>. Ignoring all the others and cursing them as false, every specific colored ray claims not only priority, but to be that white ray itself, and condemns even its own tints from light to dark as heresies. Yet, as the sun of truth rises higher and higher on the horizon of human perception, and each colored ray gradually fades out until it is finally reabsorbed in its turn, humanity will at last no longer be cursed with artificial polarizations, but will find itself bathing in the pure, colorless sunlight of eternal truth. And this will be *<term>Theosophia</term>*.

ENQ. Your claim is, then, that all the great religions are derived from <term>Theosophy</term>, and that it is by assimilating it that the world will be finally saved from the curse of its great illusions and errors?

<summary>The text argues that individual religions are merely fragmented, sectarian interpretations of a single divine truth. It uses the metaphor of a spectrum to illustrate how Theosophy is the pure white light from which all colored religious "rays" originate and will eventually return.</summary>
<keywords>Theosophy, sectarian dogmas, Sermon on the Mount, divine truth, spectrum metaphor, prismatic colors, Theosophia, religious unity</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the Enquirer and the Theosophist, moving from the metaphor of the spectrum of truth to the practical goals of the Theosophical Society in unifying human knowledge.</meta>

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

THEO. Precisely so. And we add that our <term>Theosophical Society</term> is the humble seed which, if watered and left to live, will finally produce the <term>Tree of Knowledge of Good and Evil</term> which is grafted on the <term>Tree of Life Eternal</term>. For it is only by studying the various great religions and philosophies of humanity, by comparing them dispassionately and with an unbiased mind, that people can hope to arrive at the truth. It is especially by finding out and noting their various points of agreement that we may achieve this result. For no sooner do we arrive—either by study, or by being taught by someone who knows—at their inner meaning, than we find, almost in every case, that it expresses some great truth in Nature.

ENQ. We have heard of a <term>Golden Age</term> that was, and what you describe would be a <term>Golden Age</term> to be realized at some future day. When shall it be?

THEO. Not before humanity, as a whole, feels the need of it. A maxim in the Persian “<term>Javidan Khirad</term>” <note>An ancient Persian work on practical philosophy and wisdom.</note> says: 
> “Truth is of two kinds—one manifest and self-evident; the other demanding incessantly new demonstrations and proofs.” 
It is only when this latter kind of truth becomes as universally obvious as it is now dim, and therefore liable to be distorted by <term>sophistry</term> and <term>casuistry</term>; it is only when the two kinds will have become once more one, that all people will be brought to see alike.

ENQ. But surely those few who have felt the need of such truths must have made up their minds to believe in something definite? You tell me that, the Society having no doctrines of its own, every member may believe as he chooses and accept what he pleases. This looks as if the <term>Theosophical Society</term> was bent upon

<summary>The Theosophist explains that the Society aims to reach universal truth by identifying the commonalities between world religions and philosophies. This process is necessary to achieve a future Golden Age where self-evident and demonstrated truths are united.</summary>
<keywords>Theosophical Society, Tree of Knowledge of Good and Evil, Tree of Life Eternal, Golden Age, Javidan Khirad, sophistry, casuistry, religious comparison, universal truth</keywords>

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

**THEO.** What is meant by the Society having no tenets or doctrines of its own is that no specific doctrines or beliefs are mandatory for its members; but, of course, this applies only to the body as a whole. The Society, as you were told, is divided into an <term>outer body</term> and an <term>inner body</term>. Those who belong to the latter have, of course, a philosophy, or—if you prefer it—a religious system of their own.

**ENQ.** May we be told what it is?

**THEO.** We make no secret of it. It was outlined a few years ago in the *<term>Theosophist</term>* and “*<term>Esoteric Buddhism</term>*,” and can be found still more elaborated in the “*<term>Secret Doctrine</term>*.” It is based on the oldest philosophy of the world, called the <term>Wisdom-Religion</term> or the <term>Archaic Doctrine</term>. If you like, you may ask questions and have them explained.

# V.

## THE FUNDAMENTAL TEACHINGS OF THEOSOPHY.

---

### *ON GOD AND PRAYER.*

**ENQ.** Do you believe in God?

**THEO.** That depends on what you mean by that term.

**ENQ.** I mean the God of the Christians, the Father of Jesus, and the Creator: the Biblical God of Moses, in short.

**THEO.** We do not believe in such a God. We reject the idea of a personal, or an <term>extra-cosmic</term> and <term>anthropomorphic</term> God, who is nothing but the gigantic shadow of *man*—and not even of man at his best. We say—and can prove—that the God of theology is a bundle of contradictions and a logical impossibility. Therefore, we will have nothing to do with him.

**ENQ.** Please state your reasons.

**THEO.** There are many reasons, and they cannot all be addressed here. But here are a few. This God is called "infinite" and "absolute" by his devotees, is he not?

**ENQ.** I believe he is.

<summary>The Theosophist explains that they reject the concept of a personal, anthropomorphic, or "extra-cosmic" God, arguing that the theological definition of such a being is logically impossible. They begin to challenge the traditional Christian view of God by questioning the consistency of the terms "infinite" and "absolute."</summary>

<keywords>Theosophy, God, personal God, anthropomorphism, theology, Christianity, infinite, absolute, extra-cosmic, logic</keywords>

--- Page 78 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue on the nature of God, moving from the rejection of an anthropomorphic God to a discussion of the logical impossibility of an absolute being acting as a personal creator.</meta>

->THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

**THEO.** Then, if infinite—that is, limitless—and especially if absolute, how can he have a form and be a creator of anything? Form implies limitation, and a beginning as well as an end; and, in order to create, a Being must think and plan. How can the ABSOLUTE be supposed to think—that is, to have any relation whatever to that which is limited, finite, and conditioned? This is a philosophical and a logical absurdity. Even the Hebrew Kabbalah rejects such an idea and, therefore, makes of the one and the Absolute Divine Principle an infinite Unity called <term>Ain-Soph</term>.<note>Ain-Soph, **אין סוף** = τὸ πᾶν = ἄπειρος, the endless or boundless, within and with Nature; the non-existent which IS, but is not *a Being*.</note> In order to create, the Creator has to become active; and as this is impossible for ABSOLUTENESS, the infinite principle had to be shown becoming the cause of evolution (not creation) in an indirect way—that is, through the emanation from itself (another absurdity, this time due to the translators of the Kabbalah)<note>How can the non-active eternal principle emanate or emit? The Parabrahm of the Vedantins does nothing of the kind; nor does the Ain-Soph of the Chaldean Kabbalah. It is an eternal and periodical law which causes an active and creative force (the logos) to emanate from the ever-concealed and incomprehensible One Principle at the beginning of every maha-manvantara, or new cycle of life.</note> of the <term>Sephiroth</term>.

**ENQ.** What about those Kabbalists who, while being such, still believe in Jehovah, or the <term>Tetragrammaton</term>?

**THEO.** They are at liberty to believe in what they please, as their belief or disbelief can hardly affect a self-evident fact. The Jesuits tell us that two and two are not always four to a

<summary>The Theosophist argues that an absolute and infinite being cannot be a personal creator because action and thought imply limitation. They explain that the Kabbalah describes an indirect process of evolution via the Sephiroth to avoid the logical contradiction of an absolute principle becoming active.</summary>

<keywords>Absolute, Ain-Soph, Sephiroth, Kabbalah, evolution vs creation, Tetragrammaton, Parabrahm, Logos, Maha-manvantara, logic, divinity</keywords>

**ENQ.** Then you are Atheists?

**THEO.** Not as far as we know, and not unless the label of "Atheist" is to be applied to those who disbelieve in an anthropomorphic God. We believe in a Universal Divine Principle, the root of ALL, from which all proceeds, and within which all shall be absorbed at the end of the great cycle of Being.

**ENQ.** This is the old, old claim of Pantheism. If you are Pantheists, you cannot be Deists; and if you are not Deists, then you must answer to the name of Atheists.

**THEO.** Not necessarily so. The term "Pantheism" is again one of many misused terms, whose real and original meaning has been distorted by blind prejudice and a one-sided view of it. If you accept the Christian etymology of this compound word, and form it from *pan*, "all," and *theos*, "god," and then imagine and teach that this means that every stone and every tree in Nature is a God or the ONE God, then, of course, you will be right, and make Pantheists into idol-worshippers in addition to their legitimate name. But you will hardly be as successful if you analyze the origin of the word Pantheism esoterically, as we do.

**ENQ.** What is, then, your definition of it?

**THEO.** Let me ask you a question in my turn. What do you understand by Pan, or Nature?

<summary>The Theosophist argues that they are not atheists, but rather believers in a Universal Divine Principle, and begins to redefine Pantheism against common misconceptions of object worship.</summary>

-># THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

**ENQ.** Nature is, I suppose, the sum total of things existing around us; the aggregate of causes and effects in the world of matter, the creation or universe.

**THEO.** Hence, the personified sum and order of known causes and effects; the total of all finite agencies and forces, as utterly disconnected from an intelligent Creator or Creators, and perhaps “conceived of as a single and separate force”—as in your cyclopedias?

**ENQ.** Yes, I believe so.

**THEO.** Well, we neither take into consideration this objective and material nature, which we call an evanescent illusion, nor do we mean by <term>πᾶν</term> Nature in the sense of its accepted derivation from the Latin *Natura* (becoming, from *nasci*, to be born). When we speak of the Deity and make it identical—and therefore coeval—with Nature, the eternal and uncreated nature is meant, and not your aggregate of flitting shadows and finite unrealities. We leave it to the hymn-makers to call the visible sky or heaven God’s Throne, and our earth of mud His footstool. Our DEITY is neither in a paradise, nor in a particular tree, building, or mountain: it is everywhere, in every atom of the visible as of the invisible Cosmos, in, over, and around every invisible atom and divisible molecule; for IT is the mysterious power of evolution and involution, the omnipresent, omnipotent, and even omniscient creative potentiality.

**ENQ.** Stop! Omniscience is the prerogative of something that thinks, and you deny to your Absoluteness the power of thought.

**THEO.** We deny it to the ABSOLUTE, since thought is something limited and conditioned. But you evidently forget that in

**ENQ.** Then your Absolute thinks?

**THEO.** No, IT does not; for the simple reason that it is *Absolute Thought* itself. Nor does it exist, for the same reason, as it is absolute existence, and *Be-ness*, not a Being. Read the superb Kabbalistic poem by Solomon Ben Jehudah Gabirol, in the *Kether-Malchut*, and you will understand:—"You are one, the root of all numbers, but not as an element of numeration; for unity does not admit multiplication, change, or form. You are one, and in the secret of your unity the wisest of men are lost, because they do not know it. You are one, and your unity is never diminished, never extended, and cannot be changed. You are one, and no thought of mine can set a limit for you, or define you. You ARE, but not as one who exists, for the understanding and vision of mortals cannot attain your existence, nor determine for you the where, the how and the why," etc., etc. In short, our Deity is the eternal, incessantly evolving, not *creating*, builder of the universe; that *universe itself* unfolding out of its own essence, not being made. It is a sphere, without circumference, in its symbolism, which has but one ever-acting attribute embracing all other existing or thinkable attributes—ITSELF. It is the one law, giving the impulse to manifested, eternal, and immutable laws, within that never-manifesting, *because* absolute LAW, which in its manifesting periods is *The ever-Becoming*.

*THEO.* I do not think so, as simple logic can hardly be regarded as blasphemy. If we were to exclude the Omnipresent Principle from one single mathematical point of the universe, or from a particle of matter occupying any conceivable space, could we still regard it as infinite?

### *IS IT NECESSARY TO PRAY?*

*ENQ.* Do you believe in prayer, and do you ever pray?
*THEO.* We do not. We act, instead of talking.
*ENQ.* You do not offer prayers even to the Absolute Principle?
*THEO.* Why should we? As people who are well-occupied, we can hardly afford to lose time addressing verbal prayers to a pure abstraction. The Unknowable is capable of relations only within its parts to each other, but is nonexistent in regard to any finite relations. The visible universe depends for its existence and phenomena on its mutually acting forms and their laws, not on prayer or prayers.
*ENQ.* Do you not believe at all in the efficacy of prayer?
*THEO.* Not in prayer taught in so many words and repeated externally, if by prayer you mean the outward petition to an unknown God as the recipient, which was inaugurated by the Jews and popularized by the Pharisees.
*ENQ.* Is there any other kind of prayer?

<summary>The Theosophist argues that the Omnipresent Principle must exist in all matter and explains that Theosophists do not practice outward, verbal prayer to an abstract Absolute.</summary>
<keywords>Omnipresent Principle, Absolute Principle, prayer, the Unknowable, logic, Pharisees, divine infinity</keywords>

--- Page 83 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue regarding the nature of prayer, moving from the rejection of outward petition to the definition of "Will-Prayer" as an internal command directed toward the divine principle within man.</meta>

-># THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

THEO. Most decidedly; we call it **WILL-PRAYER**, and it is rather an internal command than a petition.

ENQ. To whom, then, do you pray when you do so?
THEO. To "our Father in heaven"—in its <term>esoteric</term> meaning.

ENQ. Is that different from the one given to it in theology?
THEO. Entirely so. An <term>Occultist</term> or a <term>Theosophist</term> addresses his prayer to *his Father who is in secret* (read, and try to understand, Matthew chapter 6, verse 6), not to an extra-cosmic and therefore finite God; and that "Father" is in man himself.

ENQ. Then you make of man a God?
THEO. Please say "God" and not *a* God. In our sense, the inner man is the only God we can have cognizance of. And how can this be otherwise? Grant us our postulate that God is a universally diffused, infinite principle, and how can man alone escape from being soaked through *by*, and *in*, the Deity? We call our "Father in heaven" that deific essence of which we are cognizant within us, in our heart and spiritual consciousness, and which has nothing to do with the <term>anthropomorphic</term> conception we may form of it in our physical brain or its fancy: "Do you not know that you are the temple of God, and that the spirit of (the absolute) God dwells in you?" * Yet,

<summary>The Theosophist defines "Will-Prayer" as an internal command addressed to the "Father in secret," which is the divine essence within the human heart rather than an external deity. --- Page 84 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue regarding the nature of prayer, defining it as a process of internal spiritual transmutation and warning against the selfish use of will-power.</meta>

-># THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

Let no one anthropomorphize that essence within us. Let no Theosophist, if they would hold to divine rather than human truth, say that this "God in secret" listens to, or is distinct from, either finite man or the infinite essence—for all are one. Nor, as just remarked, should they say that a prayer is a petition. It is a mystery instead; an occult process by which finite and conditioned thoughts and desires, which are unable to be assimilated by the absolute spirit that is unconditioned, are translated into spiritual wills and the Will; such a process is called "spiritual transmutation." The intensity of our ardent aspirations changes prayer into the "philosopher's stone," or that which transmutes lead into pure gold. As the only homogeneous essence, our "will-prayer" becomes the active or creative force, producing effects according to our desire.

ENQ. Do you mean to say that prayer is an occult process bringing about physical results?

THEO. I do. *Will-Power* becomes a living power. But woe to those Occultists and Theosophists who, instead of crushing out the desires of the lower personal *ego* or physical man, and saying—while addressing their Higher Spiritual EGO immersed in <term>Atma-Buddhic</term> light—"Thy will be done, not mine," etc., send up waves of will-power for selfish or unholy purposes! For this is black magic, abomination, and spiritual sorcery.

-># THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

ENQ. David prayed to the Lord of Hosts to help him smite the Philistines and slay the Syrians and the Moabites, and "the Lord preserved David wherever he went." In that, we only follow what we find in the Bible.

THEO. Of course you do. But since you delight in calling yourselves Christians—not Israelites or Jews, as far as we know—why do you not instead follow what Christ says? He distinctly commands you not to follow "those of old times" or the Mosaic law, but bids you to do as he tells you, and warns those who would kill by the sword that they, too, will perish by the sword. Christ has given you one prayer of which you have made a lip-prayer and a boast, and which none but the *true Occultist* understands. In it you say, in your literal meaning: "Forgive us our debts, as we forgive our debtors," which you never do. Again, he told you to love your enemies and do good to those who hate you. It is surely not the "meek prophet of Nazareth" who taught you to pray to your "Father" to slay and give you victory over your enemies! This is why we reject what you call "prayers."

ENQ. But how do you explain the universal fact that all nations and peoples have prayed to and worshipped a God or Gods? Some have adored and tried to appease devils and harmful spirits, but this only proves the universality of the belief in the effectiveness of prayer.

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue concerning the nature of prayer, moving from the criticism of its use in warfare and hypocrisy to a deeper analysis of the term's meaning and its psychological effects.</meta>

-># THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

Theo. It is explained by that other fact that prayer has several other meanings besides the one given to it by the Christians. It means not only a plea or petition, but meant, in days of old, far more an invocation and incantation. The <term>mantra</term>, or the rhythmically chanted prayer of the Hindus, has precisely such a meaning, as the Brahmins consider themselves higher than the common <term>devas</term> or "Gods." A prayer may be an appeal or an incantation for a curse (as in the case of two armies praying simultaneously for mutual destruction) just as much as for a blessing. And as the great majority of people are intensely selfish, and pray only for themselves, asking to be given their "daily bread" instead of working for it, and begging God not to lead them "into temptation" but to deliver them (the petitioners only) from evil, the result is, that prayer, as now understood, is doubly harmful: (*a*) It kills self-reliance in man; (*b*) It develops in him an even more ferocious selfishness and egotism than he is already endowed with by nature. I repeat, that we believe in "communion" and simultaneous action in unison with our "Father in secret"; and in rare moments of ecstatic bliss, in the merging of our higher soul with the universal essence, attracted as it is towards its origin and center, a state called during life *Samadhi*, and after death, *Nirvana*. We refuse to pray to created finite beings—that is, gods, saints, angels, etc.—because we regard it as idolatry. We cannot pray to the ABSOLUTE for reasons explained before; therefore, we try to replace fruitless and useless prayer with meritorious and good-producing actions.

<summary>The author explains that while traditional prayer often encourages selfishness and a lack of self-reliance, Theosophy favors spiritual "communion" and the attainment of states like Samadhi. Rather than petitioning the Absolute or finite beings, Theosophists believe in replacing prayer with practical, good-producing actions.</summary>
<keywords>prayer, mantra, Hindus, Brahmins, devas, Samadhi, Nirvana, Absolute, self-reliance, communion, idolatry, altruism</keywords>

--- Page 87 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue on the nature of prayer, contrasting the Theosophical view with traditional Christian beliefs and interpreting the teachings of Buddha and Jesus from an esoteric perspective.</meta>

-># THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

ENQ. Christians would call it pride and blasphemy. Are they wrong?

THEO. Entirely so. It is they, on the contrary, who show Satanic pride in their belief that the Absolute or the Infinite—even if there were such a thing as the possibility of any relation between the unconditioned and the conditioned—will stoop to listen to every foolish or egotistical prayer. And it is they again who virtually blaspheme by teaching that an omniscient and omnipotent God needs spoken prayers to know what He has to do! This—understood esoterically—is corroborated by both Buddha and Jesus. The one says, "Seek nothing from the helpless Gods—do not pray! *But rather act; for darkness will not brighten.* Ask nothing from silence, for it can *neither speak nor hear*." And the other—Jesus—recommends: "Whatever you shall ask in my name (that of *<term>Christos</term>*) that will I do." Of course, this quotation, if taken in its literal sense, goes against our argument. But if we accept it esoterically, with the full knowledge of the meaning of the term "*<term>Christos</term>*," which to us represents *<term>Atma-Buddhi-Manas</term>*, the "SELF," it comes to this: the only God we must recognize and pray to, or rather act in unison with, is that spirit of God of which our body is the temple, and in which it dwells.

THEO. It is so recorded, but those "prayers" are precisely that kind of communion just mentioned with one’s "Father in

Would you, even as a simple mortal man, pay your laborer a full day's wage if you did most of his work for him while he sat under an apple tree praying for you to do so the whole time? This idea of passing one's whole life in moral idleness, and having one's hardest work and duty done by another—whether God or man—is most revolting to us, as it is most degrading to human dignity.

**ENQ.** Perhaps so, yet it is the idea of trusting in a personal Savior to help and strengthen one in the battle of life which is the fundamental idea of modern Christianity. ### ON THE SOURCE OF THE HUMAN SOUL:

**ENQ.** How, then, do you explain that man is endowed with a Spirit and Soul? Where do these come from?

**THEO.** From the Universal Soul. They are certainly not bestowed by a personal God. Where does the moisture in a jellyfish come from? From the Ocean that surrounds it, in which it lives, breathes, and has its being, and to which it returns when it dissolves.

**ENQ.** So you reject the teaching that the Soul is given to, or breathed into man, by God?

**THEO.** We are forced to. The “Soul” mentioned in chapter 2 of Genesis (verse 7) is, as stated there, the “living Soul” or *Nephesh* (the vital, animal soul) with which God (we say “nature” and unchangeable law) endows man just like every animal. It is not at all the thinking Soul or mind; least of all is it the immortal Spirit.

**ENQ.** Well, let us put it another way: is it God who endows man with a human rational Soul and immortal Spirit?

**THEO.** Again, because of how you phrased the question, we must object to it. Since we do not believe in a personal God, how can we believe that He endows man with anything? But granting, for the sake of argument, a God who takes upon himself the risk of creating a new Soul for every newborn baby, all that can be said is that such a God can hardly be considered as having any wisdom or foresight. There are certain other difficulties and the impossibility of reconciling this with the claims made for mercy, justice, equity, and

<summary>The author explains that the human soul originates from a Universal Soul rather than a personal God, distinguishing the animal soul (Nephesh) from the higher rational mind and immortal Spirit.</summary>

### *THE BUDDHIST TEACHINGS ON THE ABOVE.*

**ENQ.** What does Buddhism teach with regard to the Soul?

**THEO.** It depends on whether you mean exoteric, popular Buddhism, or its esoteric teachings. The former explains itself in the *Buddhist Catechism* in this way: "It considers 'Soul' a word used by the ignorant to express a false idea. If everything is subject to change, then man is included, and every material part of him must change. That which is subject to change is not permanent, so there can be no immortal survival of a changing thing." This seems plain and definite. But when we come to the question that the new personality in each succeeding rebirth is the aggregate of "Skandhas," <note>In Buddhist terminology, the five functions or aspects that constitute a sentient being.</note> or the

<summary>The dialogue concludes the critique of a personal God's role in the creation of souls and introduces the Buddhist view that the "soul" is a false concept based on changing aggregates.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of rebirth and the distinction between the temporary personality and the permanent individuality in Buddhist philosophy.</meta>

-># THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

attributes of the *old* personality, and ask whether this new aggregation of *Skandhas* is a *new* being likewise, in which nothing has remained of the last, we read that: "In one sense it is a new being; in another, it is not. During this life the *Skandhas* are continually changing; while the man A. B. at forty is identical in terms of personality with the youth A. B. at eighteen, yet by the continual waste and repair of his body and the change of his mind and character, he is a different being. Nevertheless, the man in his old age justly reaps the reward or suffering resulting from his thoughts and actions at every previous stage of his life. So the new being of the rebirth—being the same *individuality* as before (but not the same personality), with only a changed form or new aggregation of *Skandhas*—justly reaps the consequences of his actions and thoughts in the previous existence." This is complex metaphysics and plainly does not express a disbelief in the Soul by any means.

**ENQ.** Is not something like this mentioned in *Esoteric Buddhism*?

**THEO.** It is, for this teaching belongs both to *Esoteric Buddhism* (or Secret Wisdom) and to exoteric Buddhism (the religious philosophy of Gautama Buddha).

**ENQ.** But we are distinctly told that most Buddhists do not believe in the Soul’s immortality?

**THEO.** Neither do we, if you mean by Soul the *personal Ego* or life-soul—*Nephesh*. But every learned Buddhist believes in the *individual* or *divine Ego*. Those who do not are mistaken in their judgment. They are as wrong on this point as those Christians who mistake the theological interpolations of the

Both reformers were ardent philanthropists and practical altruists—preaching most unmistakably Socialism of the noblest and highest type: self-sacrifice to the very end. "Let the sins of the whole world fall upon me so that I may relieve man's misery and suffering!" cries Buddha; . . . "I would not let one cry out whom I could save!" exclaims the Prince-beggar, dressed in the discarded rags of the burial grounds. "Come unto me, all you who labor and are heavily burdened, and I will give you rest," is the appeal to the poor and the disinherited made by the "Man of Sorrows," who has nowhere to lay his head. The teachings of both are boundless love for humanity, charity, forgiveness of injury, selflessness, and pity for the deluded masses; both show the same contempt for riches and make no distinction between *meum* and *tuum* <note>"Mine" and "thine"—referring to private property or personal ownership.</note>. **ENQ.** But surely Buddha must have rejected the soul’s immortality, if all the Orientalists and his own priests say so!

**THEO.** The Arhats began by following the policy of their Master, and the majority of the subsequent priests were not initiated, just as in Christianity; and so, little by little, the great esoteric truths became almost lost. A case in point is that, of the two existing sects in Ceylon, the Siamese believe death to be the absolute annihilation of individuality and personality, while the other explains Nirvana as we theosophists do.

**ENQ.** But why, in that case, do Buddhism and Christianity represent the two opposite poles of such belief?

**THEO.** Because the conditions under which they were preached were not the same. In India, the Brahmins, jealous of their superior knowledge and excluding every caste from it except their own, had driven millions of men into idolatry and almost fetishism. Buddha had to strike a death-blow to an overabundance of unhealthy imagination and fanatical superstition resulting from ignorance, such as has rarely been known before or since. Better a philosophical atheism than such ignorant worship for those—

<meta>This page continues the explanation of why spiritual reformers like Buddha and Jesus often used silence or parables to protect complex metaphysical truths from being misunderstood by the masses, specifically addressing Buddha's refusal to confirm or deny the existence of the "Ego."</meta>

<language>English</language>

<page-num>81</page-num>
<header>->THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-</header>

and who live and die in mental despair. He had to arrest first of all this muddy torrent of superstition, to uproot *errors* before he gave out the truth. And as he could not give out *all*, for the same good reason as Jesus, who reminds *his* disciples that the Mysteries of Heaven are not for the unintelligent masses, but for the elect alone, and therefore “he spoke to them in parables” (Matt. xiii. 11)—so his caution led Buddha to conceal *too much*. He even refused to say to the monk Vacchagotta whether there was, or was not an Ego in man. When pressed to answer, “the Exalted one maintained silence.”*

**ENQ.** This refers to Gautama, but in what way does it relate to the Gospels?

---

* Buddha gives to Ananda, his initiated disciple, who inquires for the reason of this silence, a plain and unequivocal answer in the dialogue translated by Oldenburg from the *Samyuttaka Nikaya*:—“If I, Ananda, when the wandering monk Vacchagotta asked me: ‘Is there the Ego?’ had answered ‘The Ego is,’ then that, Ananda, would have confirmed the doctrine of the Samanas and Brahmanas, who believed in permanence. If I, Ananda, when the wandering monk Vacchagotta asked me, ‘Is there not the Ego?’ had answered, ‘The Ego is not,’ then that, Ananda, would have confirmed the doctrine of those who believed in annihilation. If I, Ananda, when the wandering monk Vacchagotta asked me, ‘Is there the Ego?’ had answered, ‘The Ego is,’ would that have served my purpose, Ananda, by producing in him the knowledge: all existences (dhamma) are non-ego? But if I, Ananda, had answered, ‘The Ego is not,’ then that, Ananda, would only have caused the wandering monk Vacchagotta to be thrown from one bewilderment to another: ‘My Ego, did it not exist before? But now it exists no longer!’” This shows, better than anything, that Gautama Buddha withheld such difficult metaphysical doctrines from the masses in order not to perplex them further. What he meant was the difference between the personal temporary Ego and the Higher Self, which sheds its light on the imperishable Ego, the spiritual “I” of man.

**THEO.** Read history and think about it. At the time the events narrated in the Gospels are alleged to have happened, there was a similar intellectual fermentation taking place throughout the whole civilized world, only with opposite results in the East and the West. The old gods were dying out. While the civilized classes followed the unbelieving Sadducees into materialistic negations and mere dead-letter Mosaic form in Palestine, and into moral dissolution in Rome, the lowest and poorer classes chased after sorcery and strange gods, or became hypocrites and Pharisees. Once more, the time for a spiritual reform had arrived. The cruel, anthropomorphic, and jealous God of the Jews, with his bloodthirsty laws of “an eye for an eye and a tooth for a tooth,” of the shedding of blood and animal sacrifice, had to be relegated to a secondary place and replaced by the merciful “Father in Secret.” The latter had to be shown, not as an extra-cosmic God, but as a divine Savior of the man of flesh, enshrined in his own heart and soul, in the poor as well as in the rich. No more here than in India could the secrets of initiation be divulged, lest by giving that which is holy to the dogs and casting pearls before swine, both the *Revealer* and the *things revealed* should be trampled underfoot. Thus, the reticence of both Buddha and Jesus—whether the latter lived through the historical period assigned to him or not, and who both equally abstained from plainly revealing the Mysteries of Life and Death—led in the one case to the blank negations of Southern Buddhism, and in the other, to the three clashing forms of the Christian Church and the three hundred sects in Protestant England alone.

# VI.

## THEOSOPHICAL TEACHINGS AS TO NATURE AND MAN.

---

->*THE UNITY OF ALL IN ALL.*<-

**ENQ.** Having told me what God, the Soul, and Man are *not*, in your view, can you inform me what they *are*, according to your teachings?

**THEO.** In their origin and in eternity, the three—like the universe and everything within it—are one with the absolute Unity, that unknowable divine essence I spoke about some time ago. We do not believe in a *creation*, but in the periodical and consecutive appearances of the universe from the subjective onto the objective plane of being, at regular intervals of time covering periods of immense duration.

**ENQ.** Can you elaborate on the subject?

**THEO.** Take as a first comparison, to help toward a more correct conception, the solar year; and as a second, the two halves of that year, which each produce a day and a night of six months' duration at the North Pole. Now imagine, if you can, instead of a solar year of 365 days, ETERNITY. Let the sun

<vocab>God, Soul, Man, absolute Unity, divine essence, creation, subjective, objective, Solar year, Eternity</vocab>

<summary>The Theosophist explains that God, the Soul, and Man are one with an absolute Unity and that the universe manifests through cyclical periods rather than a single act of creation.</summary>
<keywords>absolute Unity, divine essence, cyclical manifestation, subjective and objective planes, eternity, cosmology</keywords>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanation of the cyclical nature of the universe, comparing its manifestation to the long polar days and nights and introducing the concepts of Manvantara and Pralaya, as well as the distinction between eternal reality and material illusion.</meta>

represent the universe, and the polar days and nights of 6 months each—days and nights lasting each 182 trillions and quadrillions of years, instead of 182 days each. As the sun rises every morning on our objective horizon out of its (to us) subjective and antipodal space, so does the Universe emerge periodically on the plane of objectivity, issuing from that of subjectivity—the antipodes of the former. This is the “Cycle of Life.” And as the sun disappears from our horizon, so does the Universe disappear at regular periods, when the “Universal night” sets in. The Hindus call such alternations the “Days and Nights of Brahma,” or the time of *Manvantara* and that of *Pralaya* (dissolution). Westerners may call them Universal Days and Nights if they prefer. During the latter (the nights) *All is in All*; every atom is resolved into one Homogeneity.

### EVOLUTION AND ILLUSION.

**ENQ.** But who is it that creates the Universe each time?

**THEO.** No one creates it. Science would call the process evolution; the pre-Christian philosophers and the Orientalists called it emanation: we, Occultists and Theosophists, see in it the only universal and eternal reality casting a periodical reflection of itself on the infinite spatial depths. This reflection, which you regard as the objective material universe, we consider a temporary illusion and nothing else. That alone which is eternal is real.

**ENQ.** At that rate, you and I are also illusions.

<summary>The text explains the cyclical manifestation of the universe as the "Days and Nights of Brahma," alternating between periods of activity and dissolution. It describes the material universe not as a creation, but as a temporary, illusory reflection of an eternal reality.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of reality, contrasting the fleeting nature of the human personality with the eternal cause behind manifestation.</meta>

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

Theo. As fleeting personalities, we are one person today and another tomorrow. Would you call the sudden flashes of the *Aurora borealis*, the Northern lights, a "reality," even though it is as real as can be while you look at it? Certainly not; it is the cause that produces it—if permanent and eternal—which is the only reality, while the other is merely a passing illusion.

Enq. All this does not explain to me how this illusion called the universe originates; how conscious *being* proceeds to manifest itself from the unconsciousness that *is*.

Theo. It is unconsciousness only to our finite consciousness. Truly, we may paraphrase verse 5 in the 1st chapter of St. John and say: "and (Absolute) light (which is darkness) shines in darkness (which is illusory material light); and the darkness does not comprehend it." This absolute light is also absolute and immutable law. Whether by radiation or emanation—we need not quarrel over terms—the universe passes out of its homogeneous subjectivity onto the first plane of manifestation; we are taught that there are seven such planes. With each plane, it becomes denser and more material until it reaches our plane. On this plane, the only world that is approximately known and understood in its physical composition by Science is the planetary or Solar system—one that is <term>sui generis</term>, we are told.

Enq. What do you mean by <term>sui generis</term>?

Theo. I mean that although the fundamental law and the universal working of the laws of Nature are uniform, our Solar system (like every other such system among the millions in the Cosmos) and even our Earth, still has its own program of mani-

<meta>This page completes the word "manifestations" from the previous page and continues the discussion on the diversity of life in the universe and the methods used by Seers to verify spiritual truths.</meta>

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

manifestations differing from the respective programs of all others. We speak of the inhabitants of other planets and imagine that if they are *men, that is,* thinking entities, they must be like us. The fancy of poets, painters, and sculptors never fails to represent even the angels as a beautiful copy of man—*plus* wings. We say that all this is an error and a delusion; because, if on this little earth alone one finds such a diversity in its flora, fauna, and mankind—from seaweed to the cedar of Lebanon, from the jellyfish to the elephant, from the Bushman and negro to the Apollo Belvedere—alter the cosmic and planetary conditions, and there must be, as a result, quite a different flora, fauna, and mankind. The same laws will fashion quite a different set of things and beings even on this, our plane, including all our planets. How much more different, then, must external nature be in other solar systems, and how foolish it is to judge other *stars*, worlds, and human beings by our own, as physical science does!

**Enq.** But what is your data for this assertion?

**Theo.** What science in general will never accept as proof—the cumulative testimony of an endless series of Seers who have testified to this fact. Their spiritual visions—real explorations by and through physical and spiritual senses unhindered by blind flesh—were systematically checked and compared with one another, and their nature was sifted. All that was not corroborated by unanimous and collective experience was rejected, while only that was recorded as established truth which, in various ages, under different climates, and throughout an untold series of constant observations, was found to agree and receive

<summary>The Theosophist explains that life in other solar systems must differ from Earth's due to varying cosmic conditions and asserts that these truths are verified through the consistent, collective observations of Seers.</summary>

constantly receive further corroboration. The methods used by our scholars and students of the psycho-spiritual sciences do not differ from those of students of the natural and physical sciences, as you can see. Only our fields of research are on two different planes, and our instruments are not made by human hands, which is perhaps the reason they are even more reliable. The retorts, accumulators, and microscopes of the chemist and naturalist may get out of order; the telescope and the astronomer's horological instruments may be damaged; but our recording instruments are beyond the influence of weather or the elements.

**Enq.** And therefore you have implicit faith in them?

**Theo.** Faith is a word not to be found in theosophical dictionaries; we say knowledge based on observation and experience. There is this difference, however: while the observation and experience of physical science lead scientists to nearly as many "working" hypotheses as there are minds to create them, our *knowledge* agrees to add to its body of information only those facts which have become undeniable and which are fully and absolutely demonstrated. We do not have two different beliefs or hypotheses on the same subject.

---

**Enq.** Is it on such data that you came to accept the strange theories we find in *Esoteric Buddhism*?

**Theo.** Just so. These theories may be slightly incorrect in their minor details, and even faulty in their explanation by lay students; nevertheless, they are *facts* in nature and come closer to the truth than any scientific hypothesis.

### *ON THE SEPTENARY CONSTITUTION OF OUR PLANET.*

**ENQ.** I understand that you describe our earth as forming part of a chain of earths?

**THEO.** We do. But the other six “earths,” or globes, are not on the same plane of objectivity as our earth is; therefore, we cannot see them.

**ENQ.** Is that because of the great distance?

**THEO.** Not at all, for we see planets and even stars at immeasurably greater distances with our naked eye; rather, it is because those six globes are outside our physical means of perception, or plane of being. It is not only that their material density, weight, or structure are entirely different from those of our earth and the other known planets; but they are (to us) on an entirely different *layer* of space, so to speak—a layer that cannot be perceived or felt by our physical senses. And when I say “layer,” please do not allow your imagination to suggest layers like strata or beds laid one over the other, for this would only lead to another absurd misconception. What I mean by “layer” is that plane of infinite space which by its nature cannot be perceived by our ordinary waking perceptions, whether mental or physical. It exists in nature outside of our normal mentality or consciousness, outside of our three-dimensional space, and outside of our division of time. Each of the seven fundamental planes (or layers) in space—viewed, of course, as a whole, as the pure space of <term>Locke’s</term> definition, and not as our

<summary>The Theosophist explains that Earth is part of a sevenfold chain of globes, most of which are invisible to us because they exist on different planes of being and space beyond our three-dimensional perception. These "layers" of space are not physical strata but different states of existence that fall outside normal human consciousness and time.</summary>
<keywords>Septenary Constitution, planetary chain, plane of objectivity, physical perception, layers of space, three-dimensional space, consciousness, John Locke</keywords>

**ENQ.** What do you mean by a different set of senses? Is there anything on our human plane that you could bring as an illustration of what you say, just to give a clearer idea of what you might mean by this variety of senses, spaces, and respective perceptions?

**THEO.** None; except, perhaps, that which for Science would be a rather handy peg on which to hang a counter-argument. We have a different set of senses in dream-life, do we not? We feel, talk, hear, see, taste and function in general on a different plane; the change of our state of consciousness is evidenced by the fact that a series of acts and events spanning years, as we think, pass ideally through our mind in one instant. Well, that extreme rapidity of our mental operations in dreams, and the perfect naturalness, for the time being, of all the other functions, show us that we are on quite another plane. Our philosophy teaches us that, as there are seven fundamental forces in nature, and seven planes of being, so there are seven states of consciousness in which man can live, think, remember and exist. To list these here is impossible, and for this one has to turn to the study of Eastern metaphysics. But in these two states—the waking and the dreaming—every ordinary mortal, from a learned philosopher down to a poor uneducated savage, has good proof that such states differ.

**ENQ.** You do not accept, then, the well-known explanations of biology and physiology to account for the dream state?

<summary>The Theosophist explains that different planes of existence possess their own unique senses and perceptions, using the rapid and immersive experience of dreaming as an illustration of a different state of consciousness. <keywords>consciousness, dream-life, seven planes of being, seven states of consciousness, Eastern metaphysics, objectivity, subjectivity, perception</keywords>

**THEO.** We do not. We reject even the hypotheses of your psychologists, preferring the teachings of Eastern Wisdom. Believing in seven planes of cosmic being and states of consciousness regarding the universe or the macrocosm, we stop at the fourth plane, finding it impossible to go beyond it with any degree of certainty. But with respect to the microcosm, or man, we speculate freely on his seven states and principles.

**ENQ.** How do you explain these?

**THEO.** We find, first of all, two distinct beings in man: the spiritual and the physical—the man who thinks, and the man who records as much of these thoughts as he is able to assimilate. Therefore, we divide him into two distinct natures: the upper or spiritual being, composed of three "principles" or aspects; and the lower or physical quaternary, composed of four—totaling *seven*.

### *THE SEPTENARY NATURE OF MAN.*

**ENQ.** Is it what we call Spirit and Soul, and the man of flesh?

**THEO.** It is not. That is the old Platonic division. Plato was an initiate and therefore could not go into forbidden details; but anyone acquainted with the archaic doctrine finds the seven in Plato’s various combinations of Soul and Spirit. He regarded man as being constituted of two parts—one eternal, formed of the same essence as the Absoluteness, and the other mortal and corruptible, deriving its constituent parts from the minor "created" gods. He shows that man is composed of (1) A mortal

This is the division adopted by Paul, another Initiate, who maintains that there is a psychical body which is sown in the corruptible (astral soul or body), and a spiritual body that is raised in incorruptible substance. Even James (iii. 15) corroborates the same by saying that the “wisdom” (of our lower soul) descends not from above, but is terrestrial (“psychical,” “demoniacal,” see Greek text); while the other is heavenly wisdom. Now it is so plain that Plato and even Pythagoras, while speaking only of three “principles,” give them seven separate functions in their various combinations, that if we contrast our teachings this will become quite plain. ## THE DISTINCTION BETWEEN SOUL AND SPIRIT.

**ENQ.** Do you really teach, as you are accused of doing by some Spiritualists and French Spiritists, the annihilation of every personality?

**THEO.** We do not. But as this question of the duality—the *individuality* of the Divine <term>Ego</term>, and the *personality* of the human animal—involves that of the possibility of the real immortal <term>Ego</term> appearing in *séance* rooms as a “materialized spirit,” which we deny as already explained, our opponents have started this nonsensical charge.

We include <term>Atma</term> among the human “principles” in order not to create additional confusion. In reality, it is not “human,” but the universal absolute principle of which <term>Buddhi</term>, the soul-spirit, is the carrier.</note>

**ENQ.** You have just spoken of <term>psuche</term> running toward its entire annihilation if it attaches itself to <term>Anoia</term>. What did Plato mean by this, and what do you mean?

**THEO.** I think the entire annihilation of the *personal* consciousness is an exceptional and rare case. The general and almost invariable rule is the merging of the *personal* into the *individual* or *immortal* consciousness of the <term>Ego</term>—a transformation or a *divine transfiguration*—and the entire annihilation only of the *lower quaternary*. Would you expect the man of flesh, or the temporary personality—his shadow, the "astral," his animal instincts, and even physical life—to survive with the "spiritual <term>Ego</term>" and become eternal? Naturally, all this ceases to exist, either at or soon after bodily death. In time, it becomes entirely disintegrated and disappears from view, being annihilated as a whole.

**ENQ.** Then you also reject *resurrection in the flesh*?

**THEO.** Most decidedly we do! Why should we, who believe in the archaic esoteric philosophy of the ancients, accept the unphilosophical speculations of later Christian theology, which were borrowed from the Egyptian and Greek exoteric systems of the <term>Gnostics</term>?

**ENQ.** The Egyptians revered nature spirits and even deified onions; your Hindus are idolaters to this day; the Zoroastrians worshipped, and still worship, the sun; and the best Greek philosophers were either dreamers or materialists—look at Plato and <term>Democritus</term>. How can you compare them!

**THEO.** It may be so in your modern Christian and even scientific catechism, but it is not so for unbiased minds. The Egyptians revered the "One-Only-One" as <term>Nout</term>; and it is from this

To him, the *Nous* was God, and the *logos* was man, his emanation. The *Nous* is the spirit (whether in the Cosmos or in man), and the *logos*—whether the Universe or the astral body—is the emanation of the former, while the physical body is merely the animal. Our external faculties perceive *phenomena*; our *Nous* alone is able to recognize their *noumena*. It is the *logos* alone, or the *noumenon*, that survives, because it is immortal in its very nature and essence; and the *logos* in man is the Eternal Ego—that which reincarnates and lasts forever. But how can the fleeting or external shadow, the temporary clothing of that divine Emanation which returns to the source from which it came, be *that which is raised in incorruptibility*?

* "Plato and Pythagoras," says Plutarch, "divide the soul into two parts: the rational (noetic) and the irrational (agnoia). The modern term Agnostic comes from *Agnosis*, a related word. We wonder why Mr. Huxley, who coined the term, would connect his great intellect with "the soul stripped of reason" which dies? The fact that Pythagoras and others state that *phren* and *thumos* are shared by us with animals proves that, in this case, the *lower Manasic* reflection (instinct) and *Kama-rupa* (living animal passions) are meant. Since Socrates and Plato accepted and followed this clue, it is easy to demonstrate that their ideas were identical to ours if we add the *eidolon* of the Mysteries (the *shadowy form* or human double) and the *physical body* to these five elements—namely, *Agathon* (Deity or *Atma*), *Psuche* (Soul in its collective sense), *Nous* (Spirit or Mind), *Phren* (physical mind), and *Thumos* (*Kama-rupa* or passions). Even the Egyptians adhered to the septenary division. They taught that upon its departure, the Soul (Ego) had to pass through its seven chambers or principles—leaving some behind and taking others with it. The only difference is that the Egyptians, always mindful that the penalty for revealing Mystery-doctrines was death, only provided the teaching in broad outline, while we elaborate on it and explain it in detail. However, although we provide the world with as much as is permitted, even in our doctrine more than one important detail is withheld; only those who study esoteric philosophy and are sworn to silence are *alone* entitled to know these.

### THE GREEK TEACHINGS.

ENQ. We have magnificent Greek and Latin, Sanskrit and Hebrew scholars. How is it that we find nothing in their translations that would provide us a clue to what you are saying?

THEO. Because your translators, despite their great learning, have made the philosophers—the Greeks especially—into *misty* instead of *mystic* writers. Take Plutarch as an example, and read what he says about “the principles” of man. What he describes was taken literally and attributed to metaphysical superstition and ignorance. Let me give you a relevant illustration: 

> “Man,” says Plutarch, “is a composite being; and those who think him to be composed of *only* two parts are mistaken. For they imagine that the understanding (brain intellect) is a part of the soul (the upper Triad), but they err in this no less than those who make the soul a part of the body—that is, those who make the Triad part of the corruptible, mortal quaternary. For the understanding (*nous*) exceeds the soul as much as the soul is better and more divine than the body. Now, this composition of the soul (*psyche*) with the understanding (*nous*) creates reason; and with the body (or *thumos*, the animal soul) it creates passion; of which one is the beginning or principle of pleasure and pain, and the other of virtue and vice. Of these three parts joined and compacted together, the earth has given the body, the moon the soul, and the sun the understanding for the generation of man.”

This last sentence is purely allegorical, and will be compre-

Plutarch divides the latter into three groups and makes the body a compound of the physical frame, *astral shadow*, and breath—the triple lower part, which “was taken from earth and returns to earth.” Of the middle principle and the instinctual soul, the second part, he says it is derived *from* and *through* and ever influenced by the *moon*; and only of the higher part or the *Spiritual Soul*, with the Atmic and Manasic elements in it, does he make a direct emanation of the Sun, who stands here for *Agathon*, the Supreme Deity. This is proven by what he says further, as follows:

> “Now of the deaths we die, one makes man two out of three and the other one out of two. The former is in the region and jurisdiction of Demeter, where the name given to the Mysteries, τελεῖν (*telein*), resembled that given to death, τελευτᾶν (*teleutan*). The Athenians also previously called the deceased ‘sacred to Demeter.’ As for the other death, it is in the moon or the region of Persephone.”

Here you have our doctrine, which shows man as a *septenary* during life; a *quintile* just after death, in *Kamaloka*; and a threefold *Ego*, Spirit-Soul, and consciousness in *Devachan*. This separation—first in “the Meadows of Hades,” as Plutarch calls *Kamaloka*, then in *Devachan*—was part and parcel of the performances during the sacred Mysteries, when the candidates for initiation enacted the whole drama of death and the resurrection as a glorified spirit, by which name we

This suddenly and with violence plucks the soul from the body; but Proserpina mildly and over a long period separates the understanding from the soul. <note>Proserpina, or Persephone, represents here the *post-mortem Karma*, which is said to regulate the separation of the lower from the higher “principles”: the *Soul*, as *Nephesh* (the breath of animal life), which remains for a time in *Kama-loka*, from the higher compound *Ego*, which enters the state of *Devachan*, or bliss.</note> For this reason she is called *Monogenes*, the only begotten, or rather the one who begets alone; *for the better part of man becomes solitary when it is separated by her*. Now both the one and the other happen in this way according to nature. It is ordained by Fate (*Fatum* or *Karma*) that every soul, whether with or without understanding (mind), after leaving the body, should wander for a time—though not all for the same length of time—in the region lying between the earth and moon (*Kamaloka*). <note>Until the separation of the higher, spiritual “principle” takes place from the lower ones, which remain in the *Kama-loka* until they disintegrate.</note> For those who have been unjust and dissolute then suffer the punishment due for their offenses; but the good and virtuous are detained there until they are purified, and have, through expiation, purged away all the infections they might have contracted from the contagion of the body, as if from ill health, living in the mildest part of the air, called the *Meadows of Hades*, where they must remain for a certain fixed and appointed time. And then, as if they were returning to their country from a wandering pilgrimage or long exile, they experience a taste of joy—similar to that received by those who are initiated into the Sacred Mysteries—mixed with trouble, wonder, and each person’s own specific hope.”

This is *Nirvanic* bliss, and no Theosophist could describe in plainer though esoteric language the mental joys of *Devachan*, where every man has his paradise around him, created by his consciousness. But you must beware of the general error

<summary>The author uses Plutarch's descriptions of the afterlife to illustrate the Theosophical stages of Kama-loka and Devachan. Plutarch describes the separation of the understanding from the soul and a period of purification before the individual experiences spiritual joy.</summary>

->THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

100

into which too many even of our Theosophists fall. Do not imagine that because man is called *septenary*, then *quintuple* and a *triad*, he is a compound of seven, five, or three entities; or, as well expressed by a Theosophical writer, of skins to be peeled off like the skins of an onion. The “principles,” as already said—excepting the body, the life, and the astral *eidolon*, all of which disperse at death—are simply *aspects and states of consciousness*. There is but one real man, enduring through the cycle of life and immortal in essence, if not in form, and this is *Manas*, the Mind-man or embodied Consciousness. The objection made by the materialists, who deny the possibility of mind and consciousness acting without matter, is worthless in our case. We do not deny the soundness of their argument; but we simply ask our opponents, “*Are you acquainted with all the states of matter, you who until now have known of only three? And how do you know whether that which we refer to as* ABSOLUTE CONSCIOUSNESS *or* Deity, *forever invisible and unknowable, is not that which, though it forever eludes our finite human conception, is still universal Spirit-matter or matter-Spirit in its absolute infinitude?*” It is, then, one of the lowest—and in its *manvantaric* manifestations, fractioned—aspects of this Spirit-matter, which is the conscious *Ego* that creates its own paradise; a fool’s paradise, it may be, yet still a state of bliss.

ENQ. But what is *Devachan*?

THEO. Literally, the “*land of gods*”; a condition, a state of mental bliss. Philosophically, it is a mental condition analogous to, but far more vivid and real than, the most vivid dream. It is the state after death of most mortals.

# VII.

## ON THE VARIOUS POST-MORTEM STATES.

---

### *THE PHYSICAL AND THE SPIRITUAL MAN.*

ENQ. I am glad to hear you believe in the immortality of the Soul.
THEO. Not of “the Soul,” but of the divine Spirit; or rather in the immortality of the reincarnating Ego.
ENQ. What is the difference?
THEO. A very great one in our philosophy, but this is too complex and difficult a question to touch upon lightly. We shall have to analyze them separately, and then together. We may begin with Spirit.

We say that the Spirit (the “Father in secret” of Jesus), or *Atman*, is not the individual property of any person, but is the Divine essence which has no body and no form. It is weightless, invisible, and indivisible; it is that which does not *exist* and yet *is*, as the Buddhists say of Nirvana. It only overshadows the mortal being; that which enters into him and pervades the whole body is only its omnipresent rays—the light radiated through *Buddhi*, its vehicle and direct emanation. This is the

<column-break/>

->THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

error into which too many even of our Theosophists fall. The “principles,” as already said—excepting the body, the life, and the astral *image* <term>eidolon</term>, all of which disperse at death—are simply *aspects and states of consciousness*. There is but one real man, enduring through the cycle of life and immortal in essence, if not in form, and this is *Manas*, the Mind-man or embodied Consciousness. ENQ. I was under the impression that the “Animal Soul” alone was irrational, not the Divine.

THEO. You have to learn the difference between that which is negatively or passively “irrational” because it is undifferentiated, and that which is irrational because it is too active and positive. Man is a correlation of spiritual powers, as well as a correlation of chemical and physical forces, brought into function by what we call “principles.”

**ENQ.** If the "Spirit," or the divine portion of the soul, has pre-existed as a distinct being from all eternity—as Origen, Synesius, and other semi-Christian and semi-Platonic philosophers taught—and if it is the same as, and nothing more than, the metaphysically-objective soul, how can it be anything other than eternal? According to these philosophers, the reasoning soul comes from *within* the universal soul, and the other from *without*.

...progressive scale of “principles” from meta-Spirit down to the grossest matter, how can we ever come to regard the *inner* man as immortal, and at the same time as both a spiritual Entity and a mortal man?

ENQ. Then why should you not believe in God as such an Entity?

THEO. Because that which is infinite and unconditioned can have no form and cannot be a being—at least not in any Eastern philosophy worthy of the name. An “entity” is immortal, but only in its ultimate essence, not in its individual form. When it reaches the final point of its cycle, it is absorbed into its primordial nature; it becomes spirit when it loses its name of Entity.

Its immortality as a form is limited only to its life-cycle or the *Mahamanvantara*; after which it is one and identical with the Universal Spirit and is no longer a separate Entity. As for the *personal* Soul—by which we mean the spark of consciousness that preserves within the Spiritual Ego the idea of the personal “I” from the last incarnation—this lasts as a separate, distinct recollection only throughout the Devachanic period. After that time, it is added to the series of other innumerable incarnations of the Ego, like the remembrance in our memory of one specific day out of a series of days at the end of a year. Will you bind the infinity you claim for your God to finite conditions? Only that which is indissolubly joined by *Atma* (that is, *Buddhi-Manas*) is immortal. The Soul of man (that is, the personality) *by itself* is neither immortal, eternal, nor divine. The *Zohar* (vol. iii, p. 616) says, “the soul, when sent to this earth, puts on an earthly garment to preserve herself here; so she...

ENQ. How can that which—if not breathed by God into man, yet is by your own admission of an identical substance with the divine—fail to be immortal?

THEO. Every atom and speck of matter, not just substance, is imperishable in its essence, but not in its individual consciousness. Immortality is nothing but one’s unbroken consciousness; and the personal consciousness can hardly last longer than the personality itself, can it? And such consciousness, as I already told you, survives only throughout *Devachan*, after which it is reabsorbed, first into the *individual*, and then into the *universal* consciousness. You would do better to inquire of your theologians how it is that they have so greatly confused the Jewish Scriptures.
