<meta>This page continues the argument that the Jewish Scriptures do not support the modern concept of an immortal personal soul, specifically by analyzing the use of the Hebrew term *nephesh*.</meta>

->THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

*nephesh*, that which God breathes into Adam (Gen. ch. ii.), as the immortal soul. Here are some instances: — "And God created . . . . every *nephesh* (life) that moves" (Gen. i. 21), meaning animals; and (Gen. ii. 7) it is said: "And man became a *nephesh*" (living soul), which shows that the word *nephesh* was applied without distinction to immortal man and to mortal beast. "And surely I will require the blood of your *nepheshim* (lives); from the hand of every beast I will require it, and from the hand of man" (Gen. ix. 5), "Escape for *nephesh*" (translated as "escape for your life"), (Gen. xix. 17). "Let us not kill him," reads the English version (Gen. xxxvii. 21). "Let us not kill his *nephesh*," is the Hebrew text. "*Nephesh* for *nephesh*," says Leviticus (xvii. 8). "He who kills any man shall surely be put to death," literally "He who strikes the *nephesh* of a man" (Lev. xxiv. 17); and from verse 18 and following it reads: "And he who kills a beast (*nephesh*) shall make it good . . . . Beast for beast," whereas the original text has it "*nephesh* for *nephesh*." How could man kill that which is immortal? And this also explains why the Sadducees denied the immortality of the soul, as it also affords another proof that very probably the Mosaic Jews — the uninitiated at any rate — never believed in the soul's survival at all.

## ON ETERNAL REWARD AND PUNISHMENT; AND ON NIRVANA.

**ENQ.** I suppose it is hardly necessary to ask you whether you believe in the Christian dogmas of Paradise and Hell, or in future rewards and punishments as taught by the Orthodox churches?

<summary>The author argues that the Hebrew term *nephesh* refers to biological life common to both humans and animals rather than an immortal soul, citing various biblical passages as evidence. This linguistic analysis is used to explain why groups like the Sadducees did not believe in an afterlife.</summary>
<keywords>nephesh, nepheshim, Genesis, Leviticus, immortality, Sadducees, Mosaic Jews, Paradise, Hell, Orthodox dogmas</keywords>

--- Page 126 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the soul, transitioning from the biblical analysis of *nephesh* to the Theosophical rejection of eternal reward and punishment in favor of Karma and reincarnation.</meta>

->THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

**Theo.** As described in your catechisms, we reject them absolutely; least of all would we accept their eternity. But we believe firmly in what we call the *Law of Retribution*, and in the absolute justice and wisdom guiding this Law, or *Karma*. Hence we positively refuse to accept the cruel and unphilosophical belief in eternal reward or eternal punishment. We say with Horace:—

> “Let rules be fixed that may our rage contain,
> And punish faults with a proportional pain;
> But do not flay him who deserves only
> A whipping for the fault that he has done.”

This is a rule for all men, and a just one. Are we to believe that God, whom you make the embodiment of wisdom, love, and mercy, is less entitled to these attributes than mortal man?

**Enq.** Do you have any other reasons for rejecting this dogma?

**Theo.** Our chief reason for it lies in the fact of *reincarnation*. As already stated, we reject the idea of a new soul created for every newborn baby. We believe that every human being is the bearer, or *Vehicle*, of an *Ego* coexistent with every other *Ego*; because all *Egos* are of the same essence and belong to the primeval emanation from one universal infinite *Ego*. Plato calls the latter the *logos* (or the second manifested God); and we mean the manifested divine principle, which is one with the universal mind or soul—not the anthropomorphic, extra-cosmic, and *personal* God in which so many Theists believe. Please do not confuse the two.

**Enq.** But where is the difficulty, once you accept a manifested

<summary>The author explains the Theosophical rejection of eternal reward and punishment, proposing instead the Law of Retribution (Karma) and the concept of reincarnation. The soul, or Ego, is described as a primeval emanation from a universal divine principle rather than a unique creation for each individual.</summary>
<keywords>Karma, Law of Retribution, reincarnation, Ego, Logos, Horace, anthropomorphic God, Theosophy vs Orthodoxy</keywords>

--- Page 127 ---
<meta>This page continues the Enquirer's question regarding the nature of the soul's creation, following the discussion on the rejection of a personal God in favor of a universal divine principle.</meta>

->THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

principle, in believing that the soul of every new mortal is *created* by that Principle, just as all the souls before it were created?

**Theo.** Because that which is *impersonal* can hardly create, plan, and think at its own sweet will and pleasure. Being a universal *Law*, immutable in its periodic manifestations—those of radiating and manifesting its own essence at the beginning of every new cycle of life—it is not supposed to *create* humanity, only to repent a few years later for having *created* them. If we are to believe in a divine principle at all, it must be one that is absolute harmony, logic, and justice, just as it is absolute love, wisdom, and impartiality. A God who would create every soul for the space of *one* brief span of life, regardless of whether it has to animate the body of a wealthy, happy man or that of a poor, suffering wretch—miserable from birth to death though he has done nothing to deserve his cruel fate—would be more like a senseless fiend than a God. (<note>See below</note>, “On the Punishment of the Ego.”) Why, even the Jewish philosophers who believed in the Mosaic Bible (esoterically, of course) never entertained such an idea; moreover, they believed in reincarnation, just as we do.

**Enq.** Can you give me some examples as proof of this?

**Theo.** I most certainly can. Philo Judæus says (in “De Somniis,” p. 455): “The air is full of them (souls); those which are nearest the earth, descending to be tied to mortal bodies, παλινδρομοῦσι αὖθις, *return to other bodies, desiring to live in them.*” In the Zohar, the soul is made to plead for her freedom before God: “Lord of the Universe! The former have smothered the word <term>ψυχικος</term>, so that no reader imagines it to have any relation with the soul; and with this confusion of *soul* and *spirit* together, *Bible* readers get only a distorted sense of anything on the subject. On the other hand, the interpreters of Buddha have failed to understand the meaning and object of the four Buddhist degrees of <term>*Dhyâna*</term>. Ask the Pythagoreans, “Can that spirit, which gives life and motion and partakes of the nature of light, be reduced to nonexistence?” “Can even that sensitive spirit in animals which exercises memory—one of the rational faculties—die and become nothing?” observe the Occultists. In Buddhist philosophy, *annihilation* means only a dispersion of matter, in whatever form or likeness of form it may be, for everything that has form is temporary, and is, therefore, really an illusion. For in eternity, the longest periods of time are as a wink of the eye. So it is with form. Before we have time to realize that we have seen it, it is gone like an instantaneous flash of lightning, and passed away forever. When the *Spiritual* entity breaks loose forever from every particle of matter, substance, or form, and becomes once again a Spiritual breath: then only does it enter upon the eternal and unchangeable <term>*Nirvana*</term>, lasting as long as the cycle of life has lasted—an eternity, truly. And then that *Breath*, existing in *Spirit*, is "nothing" because it is "all"; as a form, a likeness, or a shape, it is completely annihilated; as absolute Spirit it still *is*, for it has become *Be-ness* itself. The very word used, “absorbed in the universal essence,” when spoken of the “Soul” as Spirit, means “union with.” It can never mean annihilation, as that would mean eternal separation.

ENQ. Do you not open yourself up to the accusation of preaching annihilation by the language you use yourself? You have just spoken of the Soul of man returning to its primordial elements.

THEO. But you forget that I have explained the differences between the various meanings of the word "Soul," and shown the imprecise way in which the term "Spirit" has been translated so far. We speak of an animal, a human, and a spiritual Soul, and we distinguish between them. Plato, for instance, calls "rational Soul" what we call <term>Buddhi</term>, though he adds the adjective "spiritual" to it; but what we call the reincarnating Ego, <term>Manas</term>, he calls Spirit, <term>Nous</term>, etc., whereas we apply the term *Spirit*, when standing alone and without any qualification, to <term>Atma</term> only. Pythagoras repeats our ancient doctrine when stating that the *Ego* (*Nous*) is eternal with Deity; that the soul only passed through various stages to arrive at divine excellence; while <term>thumos</term> returned to the earth, and even the <term>phren</term>, the lower <term>Manas</term>, was eliminated. Again, Plato defines Soul (<term>Buddhi</term>) as "the motion that is able to move itself." "Soul," he adds (Laws X.), "is the most ancient of all things, and the beginning of motion," thus calling <term>Atma-Buddhi</term> "Soul," and <term>Manas</term> "Spirit," which we do not.

In this terminology, just as in Buddhist texts, the negative is treated as essential existence. Annihilation is subject to a similar interpretation. The positive state is essential being, but without any physical manifestation. When the spirit, in Buddhist terms, enters *Nirvana*, it loses objective existence but retains subjective being. To objective minds, this means becoming an absolute “nothing”; to subjective minds, it is <term>No-thing</term>—meaning nothing that can be perceived by the senses. Thus, their Nirvana signifies the certainty of individual immortality in *Spirit*, not in the Soul. The Soul, although it is “the most ancient of all things,” is still—along with all the other gods—a finite emanation in terms of *forms* and individuality, even if not in its basic substance.

**ENQ.** I don’t quite grasp the idea yet, and I would be grateful if you could explain this to me using some illustrations.

**THEO.** No doubt it is very difficult to understand, especially for someone raised with the standard orthodox ideas of the Christian Church. Moreover, I must tell you one thing: unless you have thoroughly studied the separate functions assigned to all the human “principles” and the state of these *after death*, you will hardly understand our Eastern philosophy.

# VIII.

## ON REINCARNATION OR REBIRTH.

### *WHAT IS MEMORY ACCORDING TO THEOSOPHICAL TEACHING?*

**ENQ.** The most difficult thing for you to do will be to explain and provide reasonable grounds for such a belief. No Theosophist has yet succeeded in bringing forward a single valid proof to shake my skepticism. First of all, you have against this theory of reincarnation the fact that no single man has yet been found who remembers that he has lived, much less who he was during his previous life.

**THEO.** I see your argument leads to the same old objection: the loss of memory in each of us regarding our previous incarnation. Do you think it invalidates our doctrine? My answer is that it does not, and at any rate, such an objection cannot be final.

**ENQ.** I would like to hear your arguments.

**THEO.** They are short and few. Yet when you take into account (*a*) the complete inability of the best modern psychologists to explain the nature of the mind to the world; and (*b*) their total ignorance of its potentialities and higher states, you must admit that this objection is based on an

->THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

a priori conclusion drawn from *prima facie* and circumstantial evidence more than anything else. Now, what is "memory" in your conception, I ask?

**ENQ.** That which is generally accepted: the faculty in our mind of remembering and of retaining the knowledge of previous thoughts, deeds, and events.

**THEO.** Please add to it that there is a great difference between the three accepted forms of memory. Besides memory in general, you have *Remembrance*, *Recollection*, and *Reminiscence*, do you not? Have you ever thought over the difference? Memory, remember, is a generic name.

**ENQ.** Yet, all these are only synonyms.

**THEO.** Indeed, they are not—not in philosophy, at all events. Memory is simply an innate power in thinking beings, and even in animals, of reproducing past impressions by an association of ideas principally suggested by objective things or by some action on our external sensory organs. Memory is a faculty depending entirely on the more or less healthy and normal functioning of our physical brain; and remembrance and recollection are the attributes and handmaidens of that memory. But reminiscence is an entirely different thing. "Reminiscence" is defined by the modern psychologist as something intermediate between remembrance and recollection, or "a conscious process of recalling past occurrences, but without that full and varied reference to particular things which characterizes recollection." Locke, speaking of recollection and remembrance, says: "When an *idea* again recurs without the operation of the like object on the external sensory <note>Locke uses "sensory" here as a noun meaning the organ or seat of sensation.</note>, it is *remembrance*; if it be

<summary>The Theosophist defines memory as a physical faculty of the brain and distinguishes it from remembrance, recollection, and reminiscence. This distinction is foundational to explaining how knowledge of past lives might exist in a form other than ordinary physical memory.</summary>
<keywords>memory, remembrance, recollection, reminiscence, philosophy, psychology, John Locke, physical brain, association of ideas</keywords>

--- Page 141 ---
<meta>This page continues the distinction between different forms of memory, specifically contrasting physical memory with "reminiscence" to explain the soul's intuitive knowledge of past lives.</meta>

->THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

sought after by the mind, and with pain and effort found and brought again into view, it is recollection.” But even Locke leaves *reminiscence* without any clear definition, because it is not a faculty or attribute of our *physical* memory. Instead, it is an intuitional perception apart from and outside our physical brain. This perception covers all those visions in humans that are regarded as abnormal—from the insights suggested by genius to the ravings of fever and even madness—because it is activated by the ever-present knowledge of our spiritual Ego. Science classifies these as having no existence outside our imagination. Occultism and Theosophy, however, regard reminiscence in an entirely different light. For us, *memory* is physical and fleeting, depending on the physiological state of the brain—a fundamental claim made by all teachers of mnemonics, who have the research of modern scientific psychologists to back them. In contrast, we call reminiscence the memory of the soul. And it is *this memory* that gives almost every human being the assurance, whether they understand it or not, that they have lived before and will have to live again. Indeed, as Wordsworth wrote:

> “Our birth is but a sleep and a forgetting,
>   The soul that rises with us, our life’s star,
> Has elsewhere had its setting,
>   And comes from afar.”

**ENQ.** If it is on this kind of memory—poetry and abnormal fancies, by your own admission—that you base your doctrine, then I am afraid you will convince very few people.

**THEO.** I did not “admit” it was a fancy. I simply said that physiologists and scientists in general regard such reminiscences

Should it be asked whether the soul is able to act without the imagination, we reply that its perception of universal truths proves that it can. **ENQ.** But don’t you think these are distinctions too subtle to be accepted by the majority of people?

**THEO.** Say rather by the majority of materialists. And to them we say, look: even in the short span of an ordinary existence, memory is too weak to register all the events of a lifetime. How frequently do even the most important events lie dormant in our memory until awakened by some association of ideas, or aroused to function and activity by some other link? This is especially the case with elderly people, who are often found suffering from a failing recollection. Therefore, when we remember what we know about the physical and the spiritual principles in humans, it is not the fact that our memory has failed to record our previous life and lives that should surprise us, but rather the opposite, if it were to happen.

### *WHY DO WE NOT REMEMBER OUR PAST LIVES?*

**ENQ.** You have given me a bird's-eye view of the seven principles; now how do they account for our complete loss of any recollection of having lived before?

**THEO.** Very easily. Since those “principles” which we call physical—none of which is denied by science, though it

<meta>This page continues the explanation of why the physical memory does not retain information from past lives, using the "seven principles" to show that the physical apparatus of memory is destroyed at death.</meta>

calls them by other names,* are disintegrated after death into their constituent elements. Memory, along with its brain—this vanished memory of a vanished personality—can neither remember nor record anything in the subsequent reincarnation of the Ego. Reincarnation means that this Ego will be furnished with a *new* body, a *new* brain, and a *new* memory. Therefore, it would be as absurd to expect this memory to remember what it has never recorded as it would be useless to examine a shirt never worn by a murderer under a microscope, seeking for bloodstains that are found only on the clothes he actually wore. It is not the clean shirt that we have to examine, but the clothes worn during the commission of the crime; and if these are burned and destroyed, how can you reach them?

**ENQ.** Yes! How can you reach the certainty that the crime was ever committed at all, or that the "man in the clean shirt" ever lived before?

**THEO.** Not by physical processes, most assuredly, nor by relying on the testimony of that which no longer exists. But there is such a thing as circumstantial evidence, since our wise laws accept it—perhaps even more than they should. To become convinced of the fact of reincarnation and past lives, one must put oneself in harmony with one’s real permanent Ego, not one’s fleeting memory.

It is a far more logical and important “working hypothesis” than any other.

**ENQ.** Finally, what is this mysterious eternal principle? Can you explain its nature in a way that is understandable to everyone?

**THEO.** It is the <term>Ego</term> that reincarnates—the *individual* and immortal “I,” rather than the personal one. In short, it is the vehicle of the <term>Atma-Buddhic MONAD</term>. This is what is rewarded in <term>Devachan</term> and punished on Earth; and it is finally that to which only the reflection of the *<term>Skandhas</term>*, or attributes, of every incarnation attaches itself.*

**THEO.** Not quite; something of each personality must survive—unless the person was an absolute materialist with not even a crack in his nature for a spiritual ray to pass through—as it leaves its eternal impression on the incarnating permanent Self.

**ENQ.** Then how does it happen that the real or Spiritual man does not impress his new personal “I” with this knowledge?

The Spiritual “I” in man is omniscient and has all knowledge innate within it, while the personal self is a creature of its environment and a slave to physical memory. If the former could manifest itself uninterruptedly and without impediment, there would no longer be men on earth; we would all be gods.

In this case, the higher principle acted upon the <term>Skandhas</term> and moved them; in the other, because the personality was paralyzed, the individuality manifested itself. Please do not confuse the two.

## ON INDIVIDUALITY AND PERSONALITY.*

ENQ. But what is the difference between the two? I confess that I am still in the dark. Indeed, it is just that difference, then, that you cannot emphasize too much to us.

---

<note>* Even in his *Buddhist Catechism*, Col. Olcott—forced to it by the logic of Esoteric philosophy—found himself obliged to correct the mistakes of previous Orientalists who made no such distinction, and he gives the reader his reasons for it. Thus he says: “The successive appearances upon the earth, or ‘descents into generation,’ of the <term>tanhaically</term> coherent parts (<term>Skandhas</term>) of a certain being, are a succession of personalities. In each birth the **PERSONALITY** differs from that of a previous or next succeeding birth. <term>Karma</term>, the *DEUS EX MACHINA*, masks (or shall we say reflects?) itself now in the personality of a sage, again as an artisan, and so on throughout the string of births. But though personalities ever shift, the one line of life along which they are strung, like beads, runs unbroken; it is always that *particular line*, never any other. It is therefore individual, an individual vital undulation, which began in <term>Nirvana</term>, or the subjective side of nature, just as the light or heat undulation through ether began at its dynamic source; it is careering through the objective side of nature under the impulse of <term>Karma</term> and the creative direction of <term>Tanha</term> (the unsatisfied desire for existence); and leads through many cyclic changes back to <term>Nirvana</term>. Mr. Rhys-Davids calls that which passes from personality to personality along the individual chain ‘character,’ or ‘doing.’ Since ‘character’ is not a mere metaphysical abstraction, but the sum of one’s mental qualities and moral propensities, would it not help to dispel what Mr. Rhys-Davids calls ‘the desperate expedient of a mystery’ (*Buddhism*, p. 101) if we regarded the life-undulation as individuality, and each of its series of natal manifestations as a separate personality? The perfect individual, Buddhistically speaking, is a <term>Buddha</term>, I should say; for <term>Buddha</term> is but the rare flower of humanity, without the least supernatural admixture. And as countless generations (‘four *asankheyyas* and a hundred thousand cycles,’ Fausböll and Rhys-Davids’ *BUDDHIST BIRTH STORIES*, p. 13) are required to develop a man into a <term>Buddha</term>, and *the iron will to become one* runs throughout all the successive births, what shall we call that which thus wills and perseveres? Character? One’s individuality: an individuality but partly manifested in any one birth, but built up of fragments from all the births?” (*Bud. Cat.*, Appendix A. 137.)</note>

### ON THE REWARD AND PUNISHMENT OF THE EGO.

**ENQ.** I have heard you say that the *Ego*, whatever the life of the person it incarnated in may have been on Earth, is never subjected to *post-mortem* punishment.

**THEO.** Never, except in very exceptional and rare cases which we will not discuss here, as the nature of the "punishment" in no way resembles any of your theological concepts of damnation.

<summary>The author explains that intuition and conscience are forms of communication from the Spiritual Ego and asserts that the Ego is almost never subjected to punishment after death in the theological sense.</summary>

--- Page 154 ---
<meta>This page transitions from discussing the communication between the spiritual and human consciousness to explaining the Theosophical view on post-mortem reward and punishment, specifically addressing why the Ego is not punished in a "hell" after death.</meta>

->THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

**ENQ.** But if it is punished in this life for the misdeeds committed in a previous one, then it is this <term>Ego</term> that ought to be rewarded also, whether here, or when disincarnated.

**THEO.** And so it is. If we do not admit any punishment outside of this earth, it is because the only state the Spiritual Self knows of hereafter is that of unalloyed bliss.

**ENQ.** What do you mean?

**THEO.** Simply this: *crimes and sins committed on a plane of objectivity and in a world of matter cannot receive punishment in a world of pure subjectivity.* We do not believe in hell or paradise as physical localities; we do not believe in objective hell-fires and worms that never die, nor in any Jerusalems with streets paved with sapphires and diamonds. What we believe in is a *post-mortem* state or mental condition, such as we are in during a vivid dream. We believe in an immutable law of absolute Love, Justice, and Mercy. And believing in it, we say: “Whatever the sin and dire results of the original <term>Karmic</term> transgression of the now incarnated <term>Egos</term>* no man (or the outer material and

---

<note>
* It is on this transgression that the cruel and illogical dogma of the Fallen Angels has been built. It is explained in Vol. II of the *Secret Doctrine*. All our <term>Egos</term> are thinking and rational entities (<term>Manasa-putras</term>) who had lived, whether under human or other forms, in the preceding life-cycle (<term>Manvantara</term>), and whose <term>Karma</term> it was to incarnate in the humanity of this one. It was taught in the <term>MYSTERIES</term> that, having delayed complying with this law (or having “refused to create” as Hinduism says of the <term>Kumaras</term> and Christian legend says of the Archangel Michael)—that is, having failed to incarnate in due time—the bodies predestined for them became defiled (See Stanzas VIII and IX in the “Slokas of Dzyan,” Vol. II *Secret Doctrine*, pp. 19 and 20); hence the original sin of the mindless forms and the punishment of the <term>Egos</term>. That which is meant by the rebellious angels being hurled down into Hell is simply explained by these pure Spirits or <term>Egos</term> being imprisoned in bodies of unclean matter, flesh.
</note>

periodical form of the Spiritual Entity) can be held, with any degree of justice, responsible for the consequences of his birth. He does not ask to be born, nor can he choose the parents who will give him life. In every respect, he is a victim of his environment, the child of circumstances over which he has no control; and if each of his transgressions were impartially investigated, there would be found nine out of every ten cases where he was the one sinned against, rather than the sinner. Life is at best a heartless play, a stormy sea to cross, and a heavy burden often too difficult to bear. The greatest philosophers have tried in vain to fathom and find out its *raison d'être*, and have all failed except those who had the key to it, namely, the Eastern sages. Life is, as Shakespeare describes it:—

> That struts and frets his hour upon the stage,
> And then is heard no more. It is a tale
> Told by an idiot, full of sound and fury,
> Signifying nothing . . . .”

Nothing in its separate parts, yet of the greatest importance in its collectivity or series of lives. At any rate, almost every individual life is, in its full development, a sorrow. And are we to believe that poor, helpless man, after being tossed about like a piece of rotten timber on the angry waves of life, is—if he proves too weak to resist them—to be punished by an eternity of damnation, or even a temporary punishment? Never! Whether a great or an average sinner, good or bad, guilty or innocent, once delivered of the burden of physical life, the tired and worn-out <term>Manu</term> (“thinking Ego”) has won

Plotinus, who said that our body was the true river of Lethe, for “souls plunged into it forget all,” meant more than he said. For, just as our terrestrial body is like Lethe, so is our celestial body in Devachan, and much more.

<term>ENQ.</term> Then am I to understand that the murderer—the transgressor of divine and human law in every form—is allowed to go unpunished?

<term>THEO.</term> Who ever said that? Our philosophy has a doctrine of punishment as stern as that of the most rigid Calvinist, only far more philosophical and consistent with absolute justice. No deed, not even a sinful thought, will go unpunished; the latter even more severely than the former, as a thought is far more powerful in creating evil results than even a deed.* We believe in an unerring law of Retribution, called <term>KARMA</term>, which asserts itself in a natural chain of causes and their unavoidable results.

It is at this moment that the future destiny of the now-rested Ego trembles in the scales of just Retribution, as *it* now falls once again under the sway of active Karmic law. It is in this rebirth which is ready for it—a rebirth selected and prepared by this mysterious, inexorable, but in the equity and wisdom of its decrees infallible LAW—that the sins of the Ego's previous life are punished. However, the Ego is not cast into an imaginary Hell with theatrical flames and ridiculous devils with tails and horns, but truly back onto this earth—the plane and region of its sins—where it will have to atone for every bad thought and deed. As he has sown, so he will reap. Reincarnation will gather around him all those other Egos who have suffered, whether directly or indirectly, at the hands or even through the unconscious influence of the past personality. They will be thrown by Nemesis into the path of the *new* man, who conceals the *old*, the eternal Ego, and . . . .

->THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY<-

"personality" is no better than a fresh suit of clothes with its specific characteristics, color, form, and qualities; but the real man who wears it is the same culprit as before. It is the individuality that suffers through its "personality." And it is this, and this alone, that can account for the terrible—yet only apparent—injustice in the distribution of lots in life to humanity. When your modern philosophers succeed in showing us a good reason why so many apparently innocent and good men are born only to suffer during a whole lifetime; why so many are born poor to the point of starvation in the slums of great cities, abandoned by fate and people; why, while these are born in the gutter, others open their eyes to the light in palaces; while a noble birth and fortune seem often given to the worst of men and only rarely to those who are worthy; while there are beggars whose inner selves are equals to the highest and noblest of men; when this, and much more, is satisfactorily explained by either your philosophers or theologians—then only, but not until then, will you have the right to reject the theory of reincarnation. The greatest and most magnificent poets have dimly perceived this ultimate truth. Shelley believed in it, and Shakespeare must have thought of it when writing on the worthlessness of birth. Remember his words:

> "Why should my birth keep down my mounting spirit?
> Are not all creatures subject to time?
> There are legions of beggars on the earth now,
> Whose origins sprang from Kings,
> And many monarchs now, whose fathers were
> The riff-raff of their age . . . . . ."

Change the word "fathers" to "Egos"—and you will have the truth.

# IX.

## ON KAMA-LOKA AND DEVACHAN.

---

->*ON THE FATE OF THE LOWER "PRINCIPLES."*<-

**ENQUIRER.** You spoke of *Kama-loka*; what is it?

**THEOSOPHIST.** When a person dies, their lower three principles leave them forever; that is, the body, life, and the vehicle of life—the astral body or the double of the *living* person. Then, their four principles—the central or middle principle, the animal soul or *Kama-rupa* (along with what it has absorbed from the lower *Manas*), and the higher triad—find themselves in *Kama-loka*. This latter is an astral location, the *limbus* of scholastic theology, the *Hades* of the ancients, and, strictly speaking, a location only in a relative sense. It has neither a definite area nor a boundary, but exists within subjective space; that is, it is beyond our sensory perceptions. Nevertheless, it exists, and it is there that the astral *eidolons* of all the beings that have lived, including animals, await their *second death*. For animals, this death comes with the disintegration and the entire fading out of their astral particles to the very last. For the human *eidolon*, it begins when the Atma-Buddhi-Manasic triad is said to

<summary>This page defines Kama-loka as a subjective astral realm where the four higher principles of a being reside after physical death. It describes this state as a transitional period where the "astral double" of both humans and animals awaits a "second death."</summary>

<keywords>Kama-loka, post-mortem states, astral body, Kama-rupa, Manas, higher triad, second death, eidolons, Hades, limbus</keywords>

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

"separate" itself from its lower principles, or the reflection of the *former personality*, by falling into the Devachanic state.

**ENQUIRER.** And what happens after this?

**THEOSOPHIST.** Then the *Kama-rupic* phantom collapses. It remains deprived of its informing thinking principle, the higher *Manas*, and the lower aspect of that principle, the animal intelligence. It no longer receives light from the higher mind and no longer has a physical brain to work through.

**ENQUIRER.** In what way does it collapse?

**THEOSOPHIST.** Well, it falls into the same state as a frog when certain portions of its brain are removed by a vivisector. It can think no more, even on the lowest animal plane. From then on, it is no longer even the lower Manas, since this "lower" aspect is nothing without the "higher."

**ENQUIRER.** And is it *this* nonentity which we find materializing in seance rooms with mediums?

**THEOSOPHIST.** It is this nonentity. However, it is a true nonentity only regarding reasoning or thinking powers; it is still an *Entity*, however astral and fluidic. This is shown in certain cases when, having been magnetically and unconsciously drawn toward a medium, it is revived for a time and lives in him *by proxy*, so to speak. This "spook," or the <term>Kama-rupa</term>, may be compared with a *jellyfish*, which has an ethereal, gelatinous appearance as long as it is in its own element, or water (the *medium's specific* aura), but which, as soon as it is thrown out of it, dissolves in the hand or on the sand, especially in sunlight. In the medium's aura, it lives a kind of vicarious life and reasons and speaks either through the medium’s brain or

**ENQ.** What of the latter? How long does the incarnating *Ego* remain in the Devachanic state?

**THEO.** This, we are taught, depends on the degree of spirituality and the merit or demerit of the last incarnation. The average time is from ten to fifteen centuries, as I already told you.

**ENQ.** But why could not this Ego manifest and communicate with mortals as Spiritualists claim? What is there to prevent a mother from communicating with the children she left on earth, a husband with his wife, and so on? It is a most consoling belief, I must confess; nor do I wonder that those who believe in it are so reluctant to give it up.

**THEO.** Nor are they forced to, unless they happen to prefer truth to fiction, however “consoling” it may be. Our doctrines may be uncongenial to Spiritualists; yet, nothing of what we believe in and teach is half as selfish and cruel as what they preach.

**ENQ.** I do not understand you. What is selfish?

**THEO.** Their doctrine of the return of Spirits, the real “personalities” as they say; and I will tell you why. If *Devachan*—call it “paradise” if you like, a “place of bliss and of supreme felicity,” if it is anything—is such a place (or say *state*), logic tells us that no sorrow or even a shade of pain can be experienced there. “God shall wipe away all the tears from the eyes” of those in paradise, we read in the book of many promises. And if the “Spirits of the dead” are enabled to return and see all that is going on on earth, and especially *in their homes*, what kind of bliss can be in store for them?

<summary>This page discusses the duration of the Ego's stay in Devachan, typically lasting ten to fifteen centuries, and argues against the possibility of spirits communicating with the living. The Theosophical view suggests that if spirits could witness earthly suffering, it would negate the state of perfect bliss that defines Devachan.</summary>

### WHY THEOSOPHISTS DO NOT BELIEVE IN THE RETURN OF PURE “SPIRITS.”

**ENQ.** What do you mean? Why should this interfere with their bliss?

**THEO.** Simply this; and here is an example. A mother dies, leaving behind her little helpless children—orphans whom she adores—and perhaps a beloved husband as well. We say that her “Spirit” or <term>Ego</term>—that individuality which is now entirely filled, for the whole <term>Devachanic period</term>, with the noblest feelings held by its former personality (such as love for her children, pity for those who suffer, and so on)—is now completely separated from this “vale of tears.” We say that its future bliss consists in that blessed ignorance of all the sorrows it left behind. Spiritualists say, on the contrary, that the spirit is just as vividly aware of them—even more so than before—because “spirits see more than mortals in the flesh do.” We say that the bliss of the <term>*Devachanee*</term> consists in its complete conviction that it has never left the earth, and that there is no such thing as death at all. The post-mortem spiritual consciousness of the mother will represent to her that she lives surrounded by her children and all those whom she loved; no gap and no link will be missing to make her disembodied state the most perfect and absolute happiness. The Spiritualists deny this point-blank. According to their doctrine, an unfortunate person is not liberated even by death from the sorrows of this life. Not a single drop from the life-cup of pain and suffering will miss his lips;

<summary>The author explains that the Theosophical view of Devachan provides a state of absolute happiness by shielding the Ego from the knowledge of earthly suffering, unlike the Spiritualist view where spirits remain aware of the pain of their loved ones.</summary>
<keywords>Devachan, Ego, Spiritualism, post-mortem consciousness, bliss, suffering, afterlife, mother's spirit</keywords>

<header>THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.</header>

and, whether he likes it or not, since he sees everything now, he will drink it to the bitter dregs. Thus, the loving wife, who during her lifetime was ready to save her husband from sorrow at the cost of her heart's blood, is now doomed to witness his despair in utter helplessness and to record every hot tear he sheds for her loss. Worse than that, she may see the tears dry too soon and another beloved face shine on him—the father of her children. She finds another woman replacing her in his affections and is doomed to hear her orphans giving the holy name of "mother" to one who is indifferent to them, and to see those little children neglected, if not mistreated. According to this doctrine, the "gentle passage to immortal life" becomes, without any transition, the beginning of a new path of mental suffering! And yet, the columns of the "*Banner of Light*," the long-standing journal of the American Spiritualists, are filled with messages from the dead—the "dear departed ones"—who all write to say how very happy they are! Is such a state of knowledge consistent with bliss? If so, then "bliss" in such a case stands for the greatest curse, and orthodox damnation must be a relief in comparison to it!

**ENQ.** But how does your theory avoid this? How can you reconcile the theory of the Soul's omniscience with its blindness to what is taking place on earth?

**THEO.** Because such is the law of love and mercy. During every <term>Devachanic</term> period, the <term>Ego</term>, as omniscient as it is by its very nature, clothes itself, so to speak, with the reflection of the "personality" that once was. I have just told you that the ideal blossoming of all the abstract—and therefore undying and eternal—qualities or attributes, such as love and mercy, the love of the

<keywords>Devachan, Ego, Spiritualism, Banner of Light, afterlife, omniscience, suffering, mercy, personality</keywords>

good, the true, and the beautiful, which ever spoke in the heart of the living "personality," stayed with the <term>Ego</term> after death and therefore followed it into <term>Devachan</term>. For the time being, then, the <term>Ego</term> becomes the ideal reflection of the human being it was when last on earth, and *that* reflection is not omniscient. If it were, it would never be in the state we call <term>Devachan</term> at all.

**ENQ.** What are your reasons for this?

**THEO.** If you want an answer based strictly on our philosophy, then I will say it is because everything outside of eternal truth—which has neither form, color, nor limitation—is illusion (<term>Maya</term>). One who has placed himself beyond the veil of <term>Maya</term>—and such are the highest <term>Adepts</term> and <term>Initiates</term>—cannot experience <term>Devachan</term>. As for the ordinary mortal, his bliss within it is complete. It is an *absolute* forgetting of everything that caused him pain or sorrow in the past <term>incarnation</term>, and even a forgetting of the fact that such things as pain or sorrow exist at all. The <term>Devachanee</term> lives its intermediate cycle between two <term>incarnations</term> surrounded by everything it had unsuccessfully aspired to, and in the companionship of everyone it loved on earth. It has reached the fulfillment of all its soul's yearnings. Thus, it lives throughout long centuries an existence of pure happiness, which is the reward for its sufferings in earthly life. In short, it bathes in a sea of uninterrupted joy, marked only by events of even greater joy.

**THEO.** From your standpoint it may be, but not from the perspective of philosophy. Besides, is not our whole terrestrial life filled

I think not. I say it again, such oblivion and *hallucination*—if you wish to call it that—are only a merciful law of nature and strict justice. At any rate, it is a far more fascinating prospect than the orthodox golden harp with a pair of wings. The assurance that “the living soul frequently ascends and runs familiarly through the streets of the heavenly Jerusalem, visiting the patriarchs and prophets, saluting the apostles, and admiring the army of martyrs” may seem to be of a more pious character to some. Nevertheless, it is a hallucination of a far more delusive nature; we all know that mothers love their children with an immortal love, while the personages mentioned in the “heavenly Jerusalem” are still of a rather doubtful nature. But I would still rather accept the “new Jerusalem,” with its streets paved like the display windows of a jeweler’s shop, than find consolation in the heartless doctrine of the Spiritualists. The mere idea that the *intellectually conscious* souls of one’s father, mother, daughter, or brother find their bliss in a “Summer Land”—which is only slightly more natural, but just as ridiculous in its description as the “New Jerusalem”—would be enough to make one lose all respect for one’s “departed ones.” To believe that a pure spirit can feel happy while doomed to witness the sins, mistakes, treachery, and, above all,

ENQ. There is something in your argument. I confess to having never seen it in this light.

THEO. Just so, and one must be selfish to the core and utterly devoid of the sense of retributive justice to have ever imagined such a thing. We are with those whom we have lost in material form, and far, far nearer to them now than when they were alive. And it is not only in the fancy of the <term>*Devachanee*</term>, as some may imagine, but in reality. For pure divine love is not merely the blossom of a human heart, but has its roots in eternity. Spiritual holy love is immortal, and <term>Karma</term> brings sooner or later all those who loved each other with such a spiritual affection to incarnate once more in the same family group. Again we say that love beyond the grave, illusion though you may call it, has a magic and divine potency which reacts on the living. A mother’s <term>*Ego*</term> filled with love for the imaginary children it sees near itself, living a life of happiness, as real to *it* as when on earth—that love will always be felt by the children in the flesh. It will manifest in their dreams, and often in various events—in providential protections and escapes, for love is a strong shield, and is not limited by space or time. As with this <term>*Devachanic*</term> “mother,” so with the rest of human relationships and attachments, except for the purely selfish or material. Analogy will suggest to you the rest.

ENQ. In no case, then, do you admit the possibility of the communication of the living with the disembodied spirit?

<summary>The author explains that the subjective state of the deceased (Devachan) allows for a spiritual connection with the living that acts as a protective shield, arguing that true spiritual love leads to future re-incarnation within the same family group. # THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

THEO. Yes, there is a case, and even two exceptions to the rule.

The first exception is during the few days that follow immediately after the death of a person and before the <term>Ego</term> passes into the <term>Devachanic</term> state. Whether any living mortal, except for a few exceptional cases—(when the intensity of the desire in the dying person to return for some purpose forced the higher consciousness to remain awake, and therefore it was really the <term>individuality</term>, the “<term>Spirit</term>,” that communicated)—has derived much benefit from the return of the spirit into the objective plane is another question. The spirit is dazed after death and falls very soon into what we call “<term>pre-devachanic unconsciousness</term>.” The second exception is found in the <term>Nirmanakayas</term>.

ENQ. What about them? And what does the name mean for you?

THEO. It is the name given to those who—though they have won the right to <term>Nirvana</term> and cyclic rest (not “<term>Devachan</term>,” as the latter is an illusion of our consciousness, a happy dream, and as those who are fit for <term>Nirvana</term> must have entirely lost every desire or possibility of the world’s illusions)—have renounced the <term>Nirvanic</term> state out of pity for mankind and those they left on earth. Such an adept, or <term>Saint</term>, or whatever you may call him, believing it a selfish act to rest in bliss while mankind groans under the burden of misery produced by ignorance, renounces <term>Nirvana</term> and determines to remain invisible *in spirit* on this earth. They have no material body, as they have left it behind; but otherwise they remain with all their principles even *in <term>astral life</term> in our sphere*. And such beings can and do communicate with a few elect ones, though surely not with ordinary mediums.

<summary>The author explains two exceptions to the rule against spiritual communication: the brief period of dazed consciousness immediately following death and the rare interventions of Nirmanakayas, enlightened beings who forgo Nirvana to assist humanity.</summary>
<keywords>Ego, Devachan, Nirmanakayas, Nirvana, afterlife communication, altruism, Spirit, astral life</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue regarding the nature of Nirmanakayas and addresses scientific and spiritualist perspectives on the survival of intelligence after death.</meta>

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

ENQ. I have asked you the question about Nirmanakayas because I read in some German and other works that it was the name given to the terrestrial appearances or bodies assumed by Buddhas in the Northern Buddhist teachings.

THEO. So they are; however, the Orientalists have confused this terrestrial body by understanding it to be *objective and physical* instead of *purely astral and subjective*.

ENQ. And what good can they do on earth?

THEO. Not much regarding individuals, as they have no right to interfere with Karma and can only advise and inspire mortals for the general good. Yet they perform more beneficent actions than you imagine.

ENQ. Science would never subscribe to this, not even modern psychology. For them, no portion of intelligence can survive the physical brain. How would you answer them?

THEO. I would not even go to the trouble of answering, but would simply say, in the words given to "M.A. Oxon," "Intelligence is perpetuated after the body is dead. Though it is not a question of the brain only. . . . It is reasonable to propose the indestructibility of the human spirit from what we know" (*Spirit Identity*, p. 69).

ENQ. But "M.A. Oxon" is a Spiritualist?

THEO. Quite so, and the only true Spiritualist I know of, though we may still disagree with him on many minor questions. Apart from this, no Spiritualist comes closer to the occult truths than he does. Like any one of us, he speaks incessantly "of the surface dangers that beset the ill-equipped, feather-headed muddler with the occult, who crosses the threshold without

<summary>The Theosophist clarifies that Nirmanakayas are astral rather than physical beings who inspire humanity without interfering with Karma, while defending the survival of intelligence after death against scientific materialism.</summary>
<keywords>Nirmanakayas, Buddhas, astral body, Karma, intelligence after death, M.A. Oxon, Spirit Identity, Spiritualism, occultism</keywords>

ENQ. What are these propositions?

THEO. “1. That there is a life coinciding with, and independent of, the physical life of the body.”
“2. That, as a necessary consequence, this life extends beyond the life of the body” (we say it extends throughout <term>Devachan</term>).
“3. That there is communication between the inhabitants of that state of existence and those of the world in which we now live.”

All depends, you see, on the minor and secondary aspects of these fundamental propositions. Everything depends on the views we take of Spirit and Soul, or *Individuality* and *Personality*. Spiritualists confuse the two “into one”; we separate them and say that, with the exceptions listed above, no Spirit will revisit the earth, though the animal Soul may. But let us return once more to our direct subject, the <term>Skandhas</term>.

### *A FEW WORDS ABOUT THE SKANDHAS.*

ENQ. What becomes of the other, the lower <term>Skandhas</term> of the personality, after the death of the body? Are they completely destroyed?

THEO. They are and yet they are not—a new metaphysical and occult mystery for you. They are destroyed as the working stock on hand of the personality; they remain as Karmic effects, like germs, hanging in the atmosphere of the terrestrial plane, ready to come to life like so many avenging fiends, to attach themselves to the new personality of the <term>Ego</term> when it reincarnates.

ENQ. This really surpasses my comprehension and is very difficult to understand.

THEO. Not once you have assimilated all the details. For then you will see that for logic, consistency, profound philosophy, divine mercy, and equity, this doctrine of <term>Reincarnation</term> has no equal on earth. It is a belief in a perpetual progress for

->THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

each incarnating <term>Ego</term>, or divine soul, in an evolution from the outward into the inward, from the material to the spiritual, arriving at the end of each stage at absolute unity with the divine Principle. From strength to strength, from the beauty and perfection of one plane to the greater beauty and perfection of another, with the addition of new glory, of fresh knowledge and power in each cycle—such is the destiny of every <term>Ego</term>, which thus becomes its own Savior in each world and incarnation.

ENQ. But Christianity teaches the same. It also preaches progression.

THEO. Yes, only with the addition of something else. It tells us of the impossibility of attaining salvation without the aid of a miraculous Savior, and therefore dooms to damnation all those who will not accept the dogma. This is precisely the difference between Christian theology and Theosophy. The former enforces belief in the descent of the spiritual <term>Ego</term> into the *Lower Self*; the latter teaches the necessity of striving to elevate oneself to the <term>Christos</term>, or <term>Buddhi</term> state.

ENQ. By teaching the annihilation of consciousness in case of failure, however, don't you think that it amounts to the annihilation of the *Self* in the opinion of those who are not metaphysical?

THEO. From the standpoint of those who believe in the literal resurrection of the body, and insist that every bone, every artery, and every atom of flesh will be raised physically on the Judgment Day—of course it does. If you still insist that it is the perishable form and finite qualities that make up the immortal man, then we shall hardly understand each other. And if you do not understand that, by limiting the existence of every <term>Ego</term> to one life—

THEO. In one sense, we can acquire more knowledge; that is, we can further develop any faculty that we loved and strived for during life, provided it is concerned with abstract and ideal things—such as music, painting, poetry, etc.—since <term>Devachan</term> is merely an idealized and subjective continuation of earth-life.

ENQ. But if in <term>Devachan</term> the Spirit is free from matter, why should it not possess all knowledge?

THEO. Because, as I told you, the <term>Ego</term> is, so to speak, wedded to the memory of its last <term>incarnation</term>. Thus, if you think over what I have said and connect all the facts together, you will realize that the <term>Devachanic</term> state is not one of <term>omniscience</term>, but a transcendental continuation of the personal life just ended. It is the rest of the soul from the hardships of life.

ENQ. But the scientific <term>materialists</term> assert that after the death of a person, nothing remains; that the human body simply disintegrates into its component elements; and that what we call the soul is merely a temporary self-consciousness produced as a by-product of organic action, which will evaporate like steam. Is theirs not a strange state of mind?

After all, there are exceptions to every rule.

### ON POST-MORTEM AND POST-NATAL CONSCIOUSNESS.*

ENQ. But if human self-consciousness generally survives death, why should there be exceptions?

THEO. In the fundamental principles of the spiritual world, no exception is possible. But there are rules for those who see, and rules for those who prefer to remain blind.

ENQ. Exactly, I understand. This is merely an error of a blind man, who denies the existence of the sun because he does not see it. But after death, his spiritual eyes will certainly force him to see. Is this what you mean?

THEO. He will not be forced, nor will he see anything. Having persistently denied the continuation of existence after death during his life, he will be unable to see it; because his spiritual capacity, having been stunted in life, cannot develop after death, and he will remain blind. By insisting that he must see it, you evidently mean one thing and I mean another. <unclear>»</unclear> You

I answer: it is possible. Because I believe firmly in our Esoteric Doctrine, which refers to the post-mortem period—the interval between two lives or births—as merely a transitory state, I say that whether that interval between two acts of the illusory drama of life lasts one year or a million, that post-mortem state may, without any breach of the fundamental law, prove to be exactly the same state as that of a man who is in a dead faint.

ENQ. But since you have just said that the fundamental laws of the after-death state admit no exceptions, how can this be?

THEO. I do not say that it admits an exception. But the spiritual law of continuity applies only to things which are truly real. To one who has read and understood the *Mundakya Upanishad* and *Vedanta-Sara*, all this becomes very clear. I will say more: it is sufficient to understand what we mean by Buddhi and the duality of Manas to gain a clear perception of why the materialist may fail to have a self-conscious survival after death. Since Manas, in its lower aspect, is the seat of the terrestrial mind, it can, therefore, give only that perception of the Universe which is based on the evidence of that mind; it cannot give spiritual vision. Whether one or hundreds of trees die from lack of vitality or are uprooted, they are still incapable of preventing the forest from remaining a forest.

ENQ. But, as I understand it, Buddhi represents the forest in this simile, and *Manas-taijasi*† represents the trees. If Buddhi is immortal, how can that which is similar to it—that is, *Manas-taijasi*—entirely lose its consciousness until the day of its new incarnation? I cannot understand it.

THEO. You cannot understand it because you are mixing up an abstract representation of the whole with its temporary changes of form. Remember that while it can be said that Buddhi-Manas is unconditionally immortal, the same cannot be said of the lower Manas, and even less of Taijasi, which is merely an attribute. Neither of these—neither Manas nor Taijasi—can exist apart from Buddhi, the divine soul. This is because the first (*Manas*) is, in its lower aspect, a qualifying attribute of the earthly personality, and the second (*Taijasi*) is identical to the first, because it is the same Manas, only with the light of Buddhi reflected upon it. In its turn, Buddhi would remain only an impersonal spirit with—

<note>*Taijasi* means "the radiant" as a result of its union with Buddhi; that is, Manas (the human soul) illuminated by the radiance of the divine soul. Therefore, Manas-taijasi may be described as the radiant mind—the human reason lit by the light of the spirit. Buddhi-Manas is the revelation of the divine *plus* human intellect and self-consciousness.</note>

ENQ. Do I understand you to say that we must not confuse in our minds the noumenon with the phenomenon, the cause with its effect?

THEO. I do say so, and repeat that, when limited to <term>Manas</term> or the human soul alone, the radiance of <term>Taijasi</term> itself becomes a mere matter of time. This is because both immortality and consciousness after death become, for the earthly personality of man, simply conditional attributes, as they depend entirely on conditions and beliefs created by the human soul itself during the life of its body. <term>Karma</term> acts constantly: we reap *in our after-life only the fruit of what we have ourselves sown in this one*.

ENQ. But if my Ego can, after the destruction of my body, be plunged into a state of total unconsciousness, then how can there be punishment for the sins of my past life?

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the afterlife and Karma, moving from the state of the materialist to how Karma balances suffering and reward for the Ego between incarnations.</meta>

->THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

**THEO.** Our philosophy teaches that Karmic punishment reaches the Ego only in its next incarnation. After death, it receives only the reward for the unmerited sufferings endured during its past incarnation.* The entire punishment after death, even for the materialist, consists, therefore, in the absence of any reward and the complete loss of the consciousness of one’s bliss and rest. Karma is the child of the earthly Ego, the fruit of the actions of the tree (which is the objective personality visible to all) as much as the fruit of all the thoughts and even motives of the spiritual “I”; but Karma is also the tender mother who heals the wounds she inflicted during the preceding life, before she will begin to torture this Ego by inflicting new ones upon him. If it may be said that there is no mental or physical suffering in the life of a mortal that is not the direct fruit and consequence of some sin in a preceding existence, on the other hand, since he does not preserve the slightest recollection of it in his current life and feels himself not deserving of such punishment—and therefore thinks he suffers for no guilt of his own—this alone is sufficient to entitle the human soul to the fullest consolation, rest, and bliss in his post-mortem existence. Death always comes to our spiritual selves as a deliverer and friend. For the materialist, who, not—

In the T.P.S. pamphlet No. 6, a phrase, later criticized in *Lucifer*, was used which was intended to convey the same idea. In form, however, it was awkward and open to the criticism directed against it; but the essential idea was that people often suffer from the effects of actions performed by others—effects which thus do not strictly belong to their own Karma—and for these sufferings they of course deserve compensation.</note>

**ENQ.** Then the personal man must always continue to suffer blindly the Karmic penalties which the Ego has incurred?

**THEO.** Not exactly. At the solemn moment of death every person, even when death is sudden, sees the whole of their past life marshaled before them in its smallest details. For one short instant, the personal becomes one with the *individual* and all-knowing *Ego*. But this instant is enough to show him the whole chain of causes that have been at work during his life. He sees and now understands himself as he is, unadorned by flattery or self-deception. He reads his life, remaining like a spectator looking down into the arena he is leaving; he feels and knows the justice of all the suffering that has overtaken him.

**ENQ.** Does this happen to everyone?

**THEO.** Without any exception. We are taught that very good and holy men see not only the life they are leaving, but even several preceding lives in which the causes were produced that made them what they were in the life just closing. They recognize the law of Karma in all its majesty and justice.

**THEO.** There is. Just as the man at the moment of death has a

### *WHAT IS REALLY MEANT BY ANNIHILATION.*

**ENQ.** I have heard some Theosophists speak of a golden thread on which their lives were strung. What do they mean by this?

**THEO.** In the Hindu Sacred books, it is said that what undergoes periodical incarnation is the *Sutratma*, which means literally the "Thread Soul." It is a synonym for the reincarnating Ego—*Manas* joined with *Buddhi*—which absorbs the Manasic recollections of all our preceding lives. It is called this because, like pearls on a thread, the long series of human lives is strung together on that one thread. In certain Upanishads, these recurrent rebirths are compared to the life of a mortal which oscillates periodically between sleep and waking.

**ENQ.** This, I must say, does not seem very clear, and I will tell you why. For the man who awakes, another day begins, but that man is the same in soul and body as he was the day before;

<summary>This page describes how the Ego receives a vision of its upcoming life before rebirth, regaining its full consciousness momentarily. It also introduces the concept of the Sutratma, or "Thread Soul," which links all of an individual's incarnations together like pearls on a string.</summary>
<keywords>Ego, Devachan, manasic consciousness, Karmic law, Sutratma, Thread Soul, reincarnation, Manas, Buddhi, Upanishads</keywords>

But none of us has the slightest recollection of a preceding life or of any fact or event concerning it. . . . **THEO.** Some people do remember their past incarnations during life; but these are Buddhas and Initiates. This is what the Yogis call <term>Samma-Sambuddha</term>, or the knowledge of the whole series of one's past incarnations.

**ENQ.** But for us ordinary mortals who have not reached <term>Samma-Sambuddha</term>, how are we to understand this comparison?

**THEO.** By studying it and trying to understand more correctly the characteristics and the three kinds of sleep. Sleep is a general and unchangeable law for humans as it is for animals, but there are different kinds of sleep and still more varied dreams and visions.

**ENQ.** But this takes us to another subject. Let us return to the materialist who—while not denying dreams, which he could hardly do—still denies immortality in general and the survival of his own individuality.

**THEO.** And the materialist, without knowing it, is right. In a person who has no inner perception of, and faith in, the immortality of his soul, the soul can never become <term>Buddhi-taijasi</term>, but will remain simply <term>Manas</term>, and for <term>Manas</term> alone there is no

**THEO.** Almost. Remember the practically universal teaching of the two kinds of conscious existence: the terrestrial and the spiritual. The latter must be considered real for the very reason that it is inhabited by the eternal, changeless, and immortal Monad; whereas the incarnating Ego dresses itself in new garments entirely different from those of its previous incarnations, in which everything except its spiritual prototype is doomed to a change so radical that it leaves no trace behind.

**ENQ.** How so? Can my conscious terrestrial "I" perish not only for a time, like the consciousness of the materialist, but so completely that it leaves no trace behind?

**THEO.** According to the teaching, it must perish in its entirety, except for the principle which, having united itself with the Monad, has thereby become a purely spiritual and indestructible essence, one with it in Eternity. But in the case of an out-and-out materialist, in whose personal "I" no Buddhi has ever reflected itself, how can the latter carry away into Eternity even one particle of that terrestrial personality? Your spiritual "I" is immortal; but from your present self, it can carry away into Eternity only that which has become worthy of immortality—namely, the mere aroma of the flower that has been mown by death.

Your present “I,” as you yourself know, is not the body now sitting before me, nor is it what I would call <term>Manas-Sutratma</term>, but <term>Sutratma-Buddhi</term>.

**ENQ.** But this does not explain to me at all why you call life after death immortal, infinite, and real, and earthly life a simple phantom or illusion; since even that *post-mortem* life has limits, however much wider they may be than those of earthly life.

**THEO.** No doubt. The spiritual <term>Ego</term> of man moves in eternity like a pendulum between the hours of birth and death. But even if these hours, which mark the periods of earthly life and spiritual life, are limited in their duration, and if the very number of such stages in Eternity—between sleep and awakening, illusion and reality—has its beginning and its end, on the other hand, the spiritual pilgrim is eternal. Therefore, the hours of his *post-mortem* life—when, disembodied, he stands face to face with truth and not the mirages of his temporary earthly existences during the period of that pilgrimage which we call “the cycle of rebirths”—are the only reality in our view. Such intervals, despite their limitations, do not prevent the <term>Ego</term>, while constantly perfecting itself, from following steadily, though gradually and slowly, the path to its final transformation, when that <term>Ego</term>, having reached its goal, becomes a divine being. These intervals and stages help toward this final result instead of hindering it; and without such limited

It is so much the worse for those terrestrial personalities from which it could collect nothing. Such personalities certainly cannot consciously outlive their terrestrial existence.

Matter is the opposite pole of spirit, and yet the two are one. What with truncated angels, brass trumpets, golden harps, and physical hell-fires, the Christian heaven seems like a fairy scene from a Christmas pantomime.

It is because of these narrow conceptions that you find such difficulty in understanding. It is precisely because the life of the disembodied soul—while possessing all the vividness of reality, as in certain dreams—is devoid of every coarsely objective form of earthly life, that the Eastern philosophers have compared it with visions seen during sleep.

The process of incarnation, therefore, is not fully described when we speak of an alternating existence on the physical and spiritual planes, and thus picture the soul as a complete entity slipping entirely from one state of existence to the other. <meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the "Higher Self," specifically addressing the definitions used by A. P. Sinnett and clarifying the distinction between Atman, Buddhi, and Manas.</meta>

->THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

"*that non-materializable portion of the Soul which abides permanently on the spiritual plane may appropriately, perhaps, be spoken of as the HIGHER SELF.*"

This "Higher Self" is ATMA, and of course it is "non-materializable," as Mr. Sinnett says. Even more, it can never be "objective" under any circumstances, even to the highest spiritual perception. For Atman or the "Higher Self" is really Brahma, the ABSOLUTE, and indistinguishable from it. In hours of Samadhi, the higher spiritual consciousness of the Initiate is entirely absorbed in the ONE essence, which is Atman; and therefore, being one with the whole, there can be nothing objective for it. Now some of our Theosophists have developed the habit of using the words "Self" and "Ego" as synonyms, and of associating the term "Self" with only a human's higher individual or even personal "Self" or Ego, whereas this term should never be applied except to the *One* universal *Self*. This is the cause of the confusion. Speaking of Manas, the "causal body," we may call it—when connecting it with the Buddhic radiance—the "HIGHER EGO," never the "HIGHER SELF." For even Buddhi, the "Spiritual Soul," is not the SELF, but only the vehicle of SELF. All the other "Selves"—such as the "individual" self and "personal" self—should never be spoken or written about without their qualifying and characteristic adjectives.

Thus, in this most excellent essay on the "Higher Self," this term is applied to the *sixth principle* or *Buddhi* (of course in conjunction with Manas, as without such a union there would be no *thinking* principle or element in the spiritual soul); and this has consequently given rise to just such misunderstandings. The statement that "a child does not acquire its *sixth principle*

But those who limit the term "Higher Self" to the Universal Divine Principle are also "justified" in misunderstanding him. To avoid such misunderstandings from now on, I propose to translate the literal equivalents of the Occult Eastern terms into English and offer these for future use.

<meta>This page continues the effort to standardize the terminology of the human principles, specifically defining the Spiritual, Higher, and Lower Egos and the role of Prana (Life) to resolve metaphysical confusion for both Theosophists and the public.</meta>

->THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

**THE SPIRITUAL divine Ego is** the <term>Spiritualsoul</term> or <term>Buddhi</term>, in close union with <term>Manas</term>, the mind-principle. Without this union, it is no <term>Ego</term> at all, but only the <term>Atmic Vehicle</term>.

**THE INNER, or HIGHER “Ego” is** <term>Manas</term>, the so-called “Fifth” Principle, viewed independently of <term>Buddhi</term>. The Mind-Principle is only the Spiritual <term>Ego</term> when it is merged into one with <term>Buddhi</term>—as no materialist is supposed to have such an <term>Ego</term> within him, however great his intellectual capacities may be. It is the permanent <term>Individuality</term> or the “Re-incarnating <term>Ego</term>.”

**THE LOWER, or PERSONAL “Ego” is** the physical man in conjunction with his *lower Self*, that is, his animal instincts, passions, desires, and so on. It is called the “false personality,” and consists of the *lower Manas* combined with <term>Kama-rupa</term>, operating through the <term>Physical body</term> and its phantom or “double.”

The remaining “<term>Principle</term>,” “<term>Prana</term>,” or “<term>Life</term>,” is, strictly speaking, the radiating force or <term>Energy</term> of <term>Atma</term>—viewed as the <term>Universal Life</term> and the <term>ONE SELF</term>. It is its lower or rather (in its effects) its more physical aspect, because it is the aspect that manifests. <term>Prana</term> or <term>Life</term> permeates the whole being of the objective <term>Universe</term>; it is called a “<term>principle</term>” only because it is an indispensable factor and the *<term>deus ex machina</term>* of the living man.

**Enquirer.** This division, being so much simplified in its combinations, will serve better, I believe. The other is much too metaphysical.
**Theosophist.** If outsiders as well as <term>Theosophists</term> would agree to it, it would certainly make matters much more comprehensible.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the Ego and its relationship to the personality. # THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

existence, the *Ego* has to identify itself with the *personal* consciousness of that life if anything is to remain of it.

<term>ENQ.</term> This means that the *Ego*, despite its divine nature, passes every such period between two incarnations in a state of mental obscuration or temporary insanity.

<term>THEO.</term> You may view it however you like. Believing that, outside the ONE Reality, everything is nothing more than a passing illusion—including the whole Universe—we do not view it as insanity, but as a very natural consequence or development of earthly life. What is life? A bundle of the most varied experiences, of daily changing ideas, emotions, and opinions. In our youth, we are often enthusiastically devoted to an ideal, to some hero or heroine whom we try to follow and emulate; a few years later, when the freshness of our youthful feelings has faded and sobered, we are the first to laugh at our fancies. And yet, there was a day when we had so thoroughly identified our own personality with that of the ideal in our mind—especially if it was that of a living being—that the former was entirely merged and lost in the latter. Can it be said of a fifty-year-old man that he is the same being he was at twenty? The *inner man* is the same; the *outward living personality* is completely transformed and changed. Would you also call these changes in human mental states "insanity"?

<term>ENQ.</term> What would you call them, and especially how would you explain the permanence of one and the fleeting nature of the other?

<term>THEO.</term> We have our own doctrine ready, and it offers us no difficulty. The clue lies in the double consciousness of our mind, and also in the dual nature of the mental "principle."

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

There is a spiritual consciousness—the Manasic mind illuminated by the light of Buddhi—which subjectively perceives abstractions; and there is the sentient consciousness (the lower *Manasic light*), which is inseparable from our physical brain and senses. This latter consciousness is held in subjection by the brain and physical senses and, being in its turn equally dependent on them, must of course fade out and finally die with the disappearance of the brain and physical senses. It is only the former kind of consciousness, whose root lies in eternity, which survives and lives forever, and may, therefore, be regarded as immortal. Everything else belongs to passing illusions.

<term>ENQ.</term> What do you really understand by illusion in this case?

<term>THEO.</term> It is very well described in the just mentioned essay on “The Higher Self.” Its author says:

> “The theory we are considering (the interchange of ideas between the *Higher Ego* and the *lower self*) harmonizes very well with the treatment of this world in which we live as a phenomenal world of illusion, while the spiritual planes of nature are, on the other hand, the noumenal world or plane of reality. That region of nature in which, so to speak, the permanent soul is rooted is more real than that in which its transitory blossoms appear for a brief space to wither and fall to pieces, while the plant recovers energy to send forth a fresh flower. Supposing flowers only were perceptible to ordinary senses, and their roots existed in a state of Nature intangible and invisible to us, philosophers in such a world who divined that there were such things as roots in another

plane of existence would be likely to say of the flowers, 'These are not the real plants; they are of no relative importance, merely illusive phenomena of the moment.'”

This is what I mean. The world in which the transitory and fleeting flowers of personal lives blossom is not the real, permanent world; rather, it is the one in which we find the root of consciousness—that root which is beyond illusion and dwells in eternity.

**ENQ.** What do you mean by the root dwelling in eternity?

**THEO.** I mean by this root the thinking entity, the Ego which incarnates, whether we regard it as an “Angel,” “Spirit,” or a Force. Of everything that falls under our sensory perceptions, only what grows directly from, or is attached to, this invisible root above can partake of its immortal life. Therefore, every noble thought, idea, and aspiration of the personality it inhabits, proceeding from and fed by this root, must become permanent. As for the physical consciousness, since it is a quality of the sentient but lower “principle” (*Kama-rupa* or animal instinct, illuminated by the lower *manasic* reflection) or the human Soul—it must disappear. That which displays activity while the body is asleep or paralyzed is the higher consciousness; our memory registers such experiences only feebly and inaccurately—because it does so automatically—and often fails to be even slightly impressed by them.

**ENQ.** But how is it that MANAS, although you call it *Nous*, a “God,” is so weak during its incarnations that it is actually conquered and fettered by its body?
