You will surely reply again: "This is a Mystery; and we are forbidden to pry into the mysteries of God." Since our religious philosophy does not forbid us from doing so, I will answer your question: unless a God descends as an *Avatar*, no divine principle can be anything but cramped and paralyzed by turbulent, animal matter. Heterogeneity will always have the upper hand over homogeneity on this plane of illusions; the closer an essence is to its root-principle, Primordial Homogeneity, the more difficult it is for that principle to assert itself on earth. Spiritual and divine powers lie dormant in every human being; and the wider the sweep of one's spiritual vision, the mightier the God within them will be. But because few people can feel that God, and since—as a general rule—deity is always bound and limited in our thoughts by earlier conceptions (those ideas instilled in us from childhood), it is very difficult for you to understand our philosophy.

**ENQ.** And is it this Ego of ours which is our God?

**THEO.** Not at all; "A God" is not the universal deity, but only a spark from the one ocean of Divine Fire. Our God within us, or "our Father in Secret," is what we call the "HIGHER SELF," *Atma*. <meta>This page continues the explanation of the Ego’s journey through the cycle of incarnations, specifically addressing how the "divine spark" becomes a "pilgrim" entangled in matter, and cites "Isis Unveiled" to clarify the nature of the Inner Man and Karma.</meta>

> "From the remotest antiquity, humanity as a whole has always been convinced of the existence of a personal spiritual entity within the personal physical man. This inner entity was more or less divine, according to its proximity to the crown. The closer the union, the more serene a person's destiny, and the less dangerous the external conditions. This belief is neither bigotry nor superstition, but only an ever-present, instinctive feeling of the proximity of another spiritual and invisible world; though it is subjective to the senses of the outward man, it is perfectly objective to the inner ego. Furthermore, they believed that there are external and internal conditions which affect the determination of our will upon our actions. They rejected fatalism, for fatalism implies the blind course of some even blinder power. But they believed in destiny or Karma, which every person weaves around themselves thread by thread from birth to death, just as a spider weaves its web; and this destiny is guided by that presence termed by some the guardian angel, or our more intimate astral inner man, who is all too often the evil genius of the man of flesh, or the personality. Both of these lead <term>MAN</term> forward, but one of them must prevail; and from the very beginning of the invisible struggle, the stern and implacable law of compensation and retribution steps in and takes its course, faithfully following the fluctuations of the conflict. When the last strand is woven, and man is seemingly wrapped in the network of his own doing, he then finds himself completely under the power of this self-made destiny. It then either fixes him like an inert shell against an immovable rock, or carries him away like a feather in a whirlwind raised by his own actions."

Such is the destiny of <term>MAN</term>—the true *Ego*, not the *Automaton*, the *shell* that goes by that name. It is for him to become the conqueror over matter.

### *THE COMPLEX NATURE OF MANAS.*

ENQ. But you wanted to tell me something about the essential nature of <term>Manas</term>, and the relationship in which the <term>Skandhas</term> of the physical man stand to it?

THEO. It is this nature—mysterious, <term>Protean</term>, beyond any grasp, and almost shadowy in its connections with the other principles—that is most difficult to realize, and even more so to explain. <term>Manas</term> is a "principle," and yet it is an "<term>Entity</term>" and an individuality or <term>Ego</term>. He is a "God," and yet he is doomed to an endless cycle of <term>incarnations</term>, for each of which he is made responsible, and for each of which he has to suffer. All of this seems as contradictory as it is puzzling; nevertheless, there are hundreds of people, even in Europe, who realize all this perfectly, because they understand the <term>Ego</term> not only in its integrity but in its many aspects. Finally, if I am to make myself understood, I must start at the beginning and give you the genealogy of this <term>Ego</term> in a few lines.

ENQ. Go on.

THEO. Try to imagine a "<term>Spirit</term>," a celestial Being—whether we call it by one name or another—divine in its essential nature, yet not pure enough to be *one with* the <term>ALL</term>. To achieve this, it must purify its nature so as to finally reach 그 goal. It can do so only by passing individually and personally—that is, spiritually and physically—through every experience and feeling that exists in the diverse or differentiated <term>Universe</term>. But once imprisoned or incarnate, their essence becomes dual: that is to say, the rays of the eternal divine Mind, considered as individual entities, assume a twofold attribute: (a) their essential inherent characteristic, the heaven-aspiring mind (higher *<term>Manas</term>*), and (b) the human quality of thinking, or animal cogitation—rationalized due to the superiority of the human brain—the *<term>Kama</term>*-tending or lower *<term>Manas</term>*. One gravitates toward <term>Buddhi</term>; the other, tending downward, moves toward the seat of passions and animal desires. Yet it is the <term>Ego</term>, the <term>Manasic Entity</term>, which is held responsible for all the sins of the lower attributes, just as a parent is answerable for the transgressions of his child as long as the child remains irresponsible.

(or <term>Buddhi</term>) is the centrifugal and the soul (<term>Manas</term>) the centripetal spiritual energy; and to produce one result, they must be in perfect union and harmony. If the centripetal motion of the earthly soul—which tends toward the center that attracts it—is broken or damaged; if its progress is arrested by clogging it with a heavier weight of matter than it can bear, or than is fit for the <term>Devachanic</term> state, then the harmony of the whole will be destroyed. Personal life, or perhaps rather its ideal reflection, can only continue if sustained by this twofold force—that is, by the close union of <term>Buddhi</term> and <term>Manas</term> in every rebirth or personal life. Even the slightest deviation from harmony damages it; and when it is destroyed beyond redemption, the two forces separate at the moment of death. During a brief interval, the *personal form* (called interchangeably <term>Kama rupa</term> and <term>Mayavi rupa</term>)—the spiritual essence of which attaches itself to the Ego, follows it into <term>Devachan</term>, and gives the permanent individuality its personal coloring (temporarily, so to speak)—is carried off to remain in <term>Kamaloka</term> and be gradually annihilated. For it is after the death of the utterly depraved, the unspiritual, and those who are wicked beyond redemption, that the critical and supreme moment arrives. If, during life, the ultimate and desperate effort of the <term>INNER SELF</term> (<term>Manas</term>) to unite some part of the personality with itself and the high, glimmering ray of the divine <term>Buddhi</term> is thwarted; if this ray is allowed to be shut out more and more by the ever-thickening crust of the physical brain, the <term>Spiritual Ego</term> or <term>Manas</term>, once freed from the body, remains severed entirely from the ethereal relic of the personality. Why vilify and abuse us, then, for saying precisely the same? Henceforward, we will have nothing more to do with Spiritualism. And now let us return to Reincarnation.

### XI.
# ON THE MYSTERIES OF REINCARNATION.
---
## *PERIODIC REBIRTHS.*

ENQ. You mean, then, that we have all lived on earth before, in many past incarnations, and shall continue to live in this way?

THEO. I do. The life-cycle, or rather the cycle of conscious life, begins with the separation of the mortal animal-man into sexes, and will end with the close of the last generation of humans, in the seventh round and seventh race of mankind. Considering we are only in the fourth round and fifth race, its duration is more easily imagined than expressed.

ENQ. And we keep incarnating into new personalities all the time?

THEO. Most certainly; because this life-cycle or period of incarnation may best be compared to a human life. Just as each such life is composed of days of activity separated by nights of sleep or inaction, so, in the reincarnation cycle, an active life is followed by a <term>Devachanic</term> rest.

ENQ. And is it this succession of births that is generally defined as reincarnation?

THEO. Exactly. It is only through these births that the perpetual

<summary>The Enquirer asks for clarification on the concept of reincarnation, leading to an explanation of the human life-cycle through various evolutionary rounds and races interspersed with periods of rest.</summary>
<keywords>reincarnation, life-cycle, animal-man, seventh round, seventh race, Devachanic rest, personalities, evolution</keywords>

--- Page 214 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the mysteries of reincarnation, specifically addressing the law that governs the cycle of rebirths and the path of Egos toward perfection.</meta>

progress of the countless millions of Egos toward final perfection and final rest (lasting as long as the period of activity) can be achieved.

ENQ. And what is it that regulates the duration or special qualities of these incarnations?

THEO. Karma, the universal law of retributive justice.

ENQ. Is it an intelligent law?

THEO. For the Materialist—who calls the law of periodicity that regulates the arrangement of the several bodies and all other laws in nature "blind forces" and "mechanical laws"—no doubt Karma would be a law of chance and nothing more. For us, no adjective or qualification could describe that which is impersonal and not an entity, but a universal operative law. If you question me about the causative intelligence behind it, I must answer you that I do not know. But if you ask me to define its effects and tell you what these are in our belief, I may say that the experience of thousands of ages has shown us that they are absolute and unerring equity, wisdom, and intelligence. For Karma, in its effects, is an unfailing redresser of human injustice and of all the failures of nature; a stern adjuster of wrongs; a retributive law which rewards and punishes with equal impartiality. It is, in the strictest sense, “no respecter of persons,” though, on the other hand, it can neither be propitiated nor turned aside by prayer. This is a belief common to Hindus and Buddhists, who both believe in Karma.

ENQ. In this, Christian dogmas contradict both, and I doubt whether any Christian will accept the teaching.

THEO. No; and Inman <note>Likely referring to Thomas Inman, a 19th-century writer on ancient mythology and symbolism.</note> gave the reason for it many years ago.

<summary>The Enquirer and Theosophist discuss Karma as the impersonal, universal law of retributive justice that governs incarnations with unerring equity. Unlike Christian dogma, Karma cannot be influenced by prayer or propitiation, a concept central to both Hindu and Buddhist beliefs.</summary>

-># THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

As he puts it, while “<term>Christians</term> will accept any nonsense if promoted by the Church as a matter of faith . . . <term>Buddhists</term> hold that nothing which is contradicted by sound reason can be a true doctrine of <term>Buddha</term>.” They do not believe in any pardon for their sins, except after an adequate and just punishment for each evil deed or thought in a future incarnation, along with proportionate compensation to the injured parties.

ENQ. Where is that stated?

THEO. In most of their sacred works. In *The <term>Wheel of the Law</term>* (p. 57), you may find the following Theosophical tenet:
> “Buddhists believe that every act, word, or thought has its consequence, which will appear sooner or later in the present or in the future state. Evil acts will produce evil consequences, good acts will produce good consequences: prosperity in this world, or birth in heaven (<term>Devachan</term>) . . . in the future state.”

ENQ. <term>Christians</term> believe the same thing, don’t they?

THEO. Oh, no; they believe in the pardon and the forgiveness of all sins. They are promised that if they only believe in the blood of Christ (an innocent victim!), in the blood offered by him to pay for the sins of all humanity, it will atone for every mortal sin. And we believe neither in <term>vicarious atonement</term> nor in the possibility of the forgiveness of even the smallest sin by any god—not even by a “personal Absolute” or “Infinite,” if such a thing could even exist. What we believe in is strict and impartial justice. Our idea of the unknown <term>Universal Deity</term>, represented by <term>Karma</term>, is that

<meta>This page continues the explanation of Karma as an impartial, universal law of justice, contrasting it with the concept of divine mercy or vicarious atonement.</meta>

-># THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

It is a Power that cannot fail and can, therefore, have neither wrath nor mercy, only absolute <term>Equity</term>, which leaves every cause, great or small, to work out its inevitable effects. The saying of <term>Jesus</term>: “With what measure you measure, it shall be measured back to you” (<term>Matthew 7:2</term>), points to no hope of future mercy or salvation by proxy, either by expression or implication. This is why, recognizing as we do in our philosophy the justice of this statement, we cannot recommend mercy, charity, and forgiveness of mutual offenses too strongly. *Do not resist evil, and return good for evil*, are <term>Buddhist</term> precepts, and were first preached in view of the implacability of <term>Karmic law</term>. For man to take the law into his own hands is, in any case, a sacrilegious presumption. Human Law may use restrictive, not punitive, measures; but a man who, believing in <term>Karma</term>, still revenges himself and refuses to forgive every injury—thereby returning good for evil—is a criminal and only hurts himself. Since <term>Karma</term> is sure to punish the man who wronged him, by seeking to inflict an additional punishment on his enemy, the person who—instead of leaving that punishment to the great Law—adds to it his own small portion, only creates a cause for the future reward of his own enemy and a future punishment for himself. The unfailing <term>Regulator</term> affects the quality of each successor in every <term>incarnation</term>; and the sum of the <term>merit</term> or <term>demerit</term> in preceding ones determines it.

ENQ. Are we, then, to infer a man’s past from his present?

THEO. Only so far as to believe that his present life is what it justly should be, to atone for the sins of the past life. Of course—except for <term>seers</term> and great <term>adepts</term>—we cannot as

<summary>This page describes Karma as an unfailing power of absolute equity that requires individuals to take responsibility for their actions rather than seeking revenge or divine forgiveness. average mortals know what those sins were. Because of our lack of data, it is impossible for us to even determine what an old man’s youth must have been; similarly, for the same reasons, we cannot draw final conclusions about what a person's past life may have been based merely on what we see in their current life.

### WHAT IS KARMA?

ENQ. But what is Karma?
THEO. As I have said, we consider it to be the *Ultimate Law* of the Universe—the source, origin, and fountain of all other laws that exist throughout Nature. Karma is the unerring law that adjusts effect to cause on the physical, mental, and spiritual planes of being. Since no cause remains without its due effect, from the greatest to the least—from a cosmic disturbance down to the movement of your hand—and since like produces like, *Karma* is that unseen and unknown law which wisely, intelligently, and equitably adjusts each effect to its cause, tracing the effect back to its producer. Although the law itself is unknowable, its action is perceivable.
ENQ. Then it is the “Absolute” or the “Unknowable” again, and is not of much value as an explanation for the problems of life?
THEO. On the contrary. For, although we do not know what *Karma* is *per se* and in its essence, we do know *how it works*, and we can define and describe its mode of action with accuracy. We only do not know its ultimate *Cause*, just as modern philosophy universally admits that the ultimate *Cause* of anything is “unknowable.”

<vocab>Karma, Theosophy, Ultimate Law, Absolute, Unknowable, Cause, per se</vocab>

<summary>This page defines Karma as the ultimate and unerring law of the universe that perfectly balances effects with their causes across all planes of existence. While the fundamental essence of Karma remains unknowable, its practical operations and effects are observable and can be described accurately.</summary>
<keywords>Karma, Ultimate Law, Cause and Effect, Theosophy, Unknowable, Universal Justice, Philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of Karma, shifting focus from the difficulties of judging individual past lives to the social application of Karma and the concept of collective or National Karma.</meta>

-># THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

ENQ. And what does Theosophy have to say regarding the solution to the more practical needs of humanity? What explanation does it offer regarding the terrible suffering and dire need prevalent among the so-called "lower classes"?

THEO. To be direct, according to our teaching, all these great social evils—the distinction of classes in society, the distinction of the sexes in the affairs of life, and the unequal distribution of capital and labor—are all due to what we briefly but accurately call KARMA.

ENQ. But surely all these evils, which seem to fall upon the masses somewhat indiscriminately, are not actually deserved and INDIVIDUAL Karma?

THEO. No, their effects cannot be so strictly defined as to show that every individual's environment, and the particular conditions of life in which each person finds themselves, are nothing more than the retributive Karma which that individual generated in a previous life. We must not lose sight of the fact that every atom is subject to the general law governing the whole body to which it belongs; here we encounter the broader path of the Karmic law. Do you not see that the collective individual Karma becomes the Karma of the nation to which those individuals belong? And further, do you not see that the sum total of National Karma is the Karma of the World? The evils you speak of are not specific to the individual or even to the nation; they are more or less universal. It is upon this broad line of human interdependence that the law of Karma finds its legitimate and fair expression.

ENQ. Do I understand, then, that the law of Karma is not necessarily an individual law?

<summary>This page explains that social inequalities and suffering are the results of Karma, which operates not only on an individual level but also collectively through nations and the entire world. It highlights the concept of human interdependence, suggesting that large-scale social evils are the expression of National and Global Karma.</summary>
<keywords>Theosophy, Karma, social evils, lower classes, individual Karma, National Karma, human interdependence, Karmic law, social inequality</keywords>

THEO. That is exactly what I mean. It is impossible for Karma to readjust the balance of power in the world’s life and progress unless it has a broad and general line of action. Theosophists hold it as a truth that the interdependence of Humanity is the cause of what is called <term>Distributive Karma</term>, and it is this law that provides the solution to the great question of collective suffering and its relief. Moreover, it is an <term>occult law</term> that no one can rise above their individual failings without lifting—even if only a little—the whole body of which they are an integral part. In the same way, no one can sin, nor suffer the effects of sin, alone. In reality, there is no such thing as "<term>Separateness</term>"; the nearest approach to that selfish state permitted by the laws of life is in one's intent or motive.

ENQ. And are there no ways by which distributive or national <term>Karma</term> might be concentrated or collected, so to speak, and brought to its natural and legitimate fulfillment without all this prolonged suffering?

THEO. As a general rule, and within certain limits that define the age we live in, the law of Karma cannot be hastened or delayed in its fulfillment. But I am certain of this: the limit of what is possible in either of these directions has never yet been reached. Listen to the following account of one phase of national suffering, and then ask yourself whether—admitting the working power of individual, relative, and distributive Karma—these evils might not be capable of significant modification and general relief. What I am about to read to you is from the pen of a <term>National Savior</term>, someone who, having overcome Self and being free to choose, has chosen to serve Humanity, in

> “Yes, Nature always does speak, don’t you think? It is only that sometimes we make so much noise that we drown out her voice. That is why it is so restful to leave the town and nestle for a while in the Mother’s arms. I am thinking of the evening on Hampstead Heath when we watched the sun go down; but oh! upon what suffering and misery that sun had set! A lady brought me a large hamper of wild flowers yesterday. I thought some of my East End family had a better right to it than I, and so I took it down to a very poor school in Whitechapel this morning. You should have seen those pallid little faces brighten! From there I went to pay for some dinners at a little cookshop for some children. It was in a narrow back street, full of jostling people; the stench was indescribable, from fish, meat, and other food items, all reeking in a sun that, in Whitechapel, festers instead of purifying. The cookshop was the quintessence of all the smells. There were indescribable meat pies for 1d., loathsome lumps of ‘food’ and swarms of flies—a true altar of Beelzebub! All around, babies were on the prowl for scraps; one, with the face of an angel, was gathering up cherry stones as a light and nutritious form of diet. I came westward with every nerve shuddering and jarred, wondering whether anything can be done with some parts of London except swallowing them up in an earthquake and starting their inhabitants afresh, after a plunge into some purifying Lethe, out of which not a single memory might emerge! If by any sacrifice one could win the power to save these people, the cost would not be worth counting; but, you see, <term>THEY</term> must be changed—and how can that be achieved? In the condition they are in now, they would not profit from any environment in which they might be placed; and yet, in their present surroundings they must continue to putrefy. It breaks my heart, this endless, hopeless misery, and the brutish degradation that is at once its outgrowth and its root. *(Signed by a name too respected and too well-known to be shared with scoffers.)*

ENQ. That is a sad but beautiful letter, and I think it presents with painful conspicuousness the terrible workings of what you have called “Relative and Distributive Karma.” But alas! There seems to be no immediate hope of any relief short of an earthquake, or some such general engulfment!

THEO. What right do we have to think so while one-half of humanity is in a position to effect an immediate relief of the hardships suffered by their fellows? When every individual has contributed to the general good what they can of money, of labor, and of ennobling thought, then, and only then, will the balance of National Karma be struck; until then, we have no right or any reason to say that there is more life on the earth than Nature can support. It is reserved for heroic souls, the Saviors of our Race and Nation, to find the cause of this unequal pressure of retributive Karma, and by a supreme effort to readjust the balance of power. They must save the people from a moral engulfment a thousand times more disastrous and more permanently evil than a physical catastrophe, in which you seem to see the only possible outlet for this accumulated misery.

ENQ. Well, then, tell me generally: how do you describe this law of Karma?

THEO. We describe Karma as <term>that Law of readjustment</term> which always tends to restore disturbed equilibrium in the physical world and broken harmony in the moral world. We say that Karma does not always act in this or that particular way; but that

<summary>The author argues that social suffering is a result of National Karma that can be mitigated by individual contributions of labor and wealth. Karma is defined as a universal law of readjustment that restores balance and harmony to the world.</summary>
<keywords>National Karma, social relief, poverty, retributive Karma, Law of readjustment, moral harmony, equilibrium</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of Karma, specifically defining it as a universal law that functions to restore harmony and balance whenever it is disturbed by human action.</meta>

-># THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

it always does act so as to restore Harmony and preserve the balance of equilibrium, in virtue of which the Universe exists.

ENQ. Give me an illustration.

THEO. Later on I will give you a full illustration. Think now of a pond. A stone falls into the water and creates disturbing waves. These waves oscillate backwards and forwards until at last, owing to the operation of what physicists call the law of the dissipation of energy, they are brought to rest, and the water returns to its condition of calm tranquility. Similarly *all action, on every plane, produces disturbance in the balanced harmony of the Universe, and the vibrations so produced will continue to roll backwards and forwards, if its area is limited, until equilibrium is restored.* But since each such disturbance starts from some particular point, it is clear that equilibrium and harmony can only be restored by the reconverging to that same point of all the forces which were set in motion from it. And here you have proof that the consequences of a person's deeds, thoughts, etc., must all react upon themselves with the same force with which they were set in motion.

ENQ. But I see nothing of a moral character about this law. It looks to me like the simple physical law that action and reaction are equal and opposite.

THEO. I am not surprised to hear you say that. Europeans have developed such an ingrained habit of considering right and wrong, good and evil, as matters of an arbitrary code of law laid down either by men, or imposed upon them by a Personal God. We Theosophists, however, say that "Good" and

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

"Harmony" and "Good," and "Evil" and "Disharmony," are synonymous. Furthermore, we maintain that all pain and suffering are results of a lack of Harmony, and that the one terrible and only cause of the disturbance of Harmony is *selfishness* in some form or another. Therefore, Karma gives back to every man the *actual* consequences of his own actions, without any regard to their moral character; but since he receives his due for *all*, it is obvious that he will be made to atone for all sufferings which he has caused, just as he will reap in joy and gladness the fruits of all the happiness and harmony he had helped to produce. I can do no better than quote for your benefit certain passages from books and articles written by our Theosophists—those who have a correct idea of Karma.

<term>ENQ.</term> I wish you would, as your literature seems to be very sparse on this subject.

<term>THEO.</term> Because it is *the* most difficult of all our tenets. A short time ago, the following objection appeared from a Christian writer:

> "Granting that the teaching regarding Theosophy is correct, and that 'man must be his own savior, must overcome self and conquer the evil that is in his dual nature, to obtain the emancipation of his soul,' what is man to do after he has been awakened and converted to a certain extent from evil or wickedness? How is he to get emancipation, or pardon, or the blotting out of the evil or wickedness he has already done?"

To this, Mr. J. H. Conelly replies very pertinently that no one can hope to "make the theosophical engine run on the theological track." As he puts it:

> "The possibility of shirking individual responsibility is not among the

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Karma, specifically addressing the impossibility of vicarious atonement and the necessity of personal responsibility for one's actions.</meta>

concepts of Theosophy. In this faith, there is no such thing as pardoning or "blotting out of evil or wickedness already done," except through the adequate punishment for it of the wrongdoer and the restoration of the universal harmony that had been disturbed by his wrongful act. The evil has been his own, and while others must suffer its consequences, atonement can be made by nobody but himself.

> "The condition contemplated . . . . in which a man shall have been 'awakened and converted to a certain extent from evil or wickedness,' is that in which a man shall have realized that his deeds are evil and deserving of punishment. In that realization, a sense of personal responsibility is inevitable, and just in proportion to the extent of his awakening or 'conversion' must be the sense of that awful responsibility. While it is strong upon him is the time when he is urged to accept the doctrine of vicarious atonement.

> "He is told that he must also repent, but nothing is easier than that. It is an amiable weakness of human nature that we are quite prone to regret the evil we have done when our attention is called to it, and we have either suffered from it ourselves or enjoyed its fruits. Possibly, a close analysis of the feeling would show us that what we regret is rather the necessity that seemed to require the evil as a means of attaining our selfish ends than the evil itself."

> "Attractive as this prospect of casting our burden of sins 'at the foot of the cross' may be to the ordinary mind, it does not appeal to the Theosophical student. He does not understand why the sinner, by attaining knowledge of his evil, can thereby merit any pardon for or the blotting out of his past wickedness; or why repentance and future right living entitle him to a suspension in his favor of the universal law of relation between cause and effect. The results of his evil deeds continue to exist; the suffering caused to others by his wickedness is not blotted out. The Theosophical student takes the result of wickedness upon the innocent into his problem. He considers not only the guilty person, but his victims.

> "Evil is a violation of the laws of harmony governing the universe, and the penalty for it must fall upon the violator of that law himself."

Christ uttered the warning, "Sin no more, lest a worse thing come upon you," and St. Paul said, "Work out your own salvation. Whatever a man sows, that shall he also reap." That, by the way, is a fine metaphorical rendering of the sentence from the Puranas far predating him—that "every man reaps the consequences of his own acts."

"This is the principle of the law of Karma which is taught by Theosophy. Sinnett, in his *Esoteric Buddhism*, rendered Karma as 'the law of ethical causation.' 'The law of retribution,' as Madame Blavatsky translates its meaning, is better. It is the power which

->Just though mysterious, leads us on unerring<-
->Through ways unmarked from guilt to punishment.<-

"But it is more. It rewards merit as unerringly and amply as it punishes demerit. It is the outcome of every act, of thought, word and deed, and by it men mold themselves, their lives and events. Eastern philosophy rejects the idea of a newly created soul for every baby born. It believes in a limited number of monads, evolving and growing more and more perfect through their assimilation of many successive personalities. Those personalities are the product of Karma and it is by Karma and reincarnation that the human monad in time returns to its source—absolute deity."

E. D. Walker, in his *Reincarnation*, offers the following explanation:—

"Briefly, the doctrine of Karma is that we have made ourselves what we are by former actions, and are building our future eternity by present actions. There is no destiny but what we ourselves determine. There is no salvation or condemnation except what we ourselves bring about. . . . Because it offers no shelter for culpable actions and necessitates a sterling manliness, it is less welcome to weak natures than the easy religious tenets of vicarious atonement, intercession, forgiveness and deathbed conversions. . . . In the domain of eternal justice the offense and the punishment are inseparably connected as the same event, because there is no real distinction between the action and its outcome. . . . It is Karma, or our old acts, that draws us back into

This law, whether conscious or unconscious, predestines nothing and no one. It exists from and in eternity truly, for it is eternity itself; and as such, since no act can be coequal with eternity, it cannot be said to act, for it is action itself. It is not the wave which drowns the man, but the personal action of the unfortunate person who goes deliberately and places himself under the impersonal action of the laws that govern the ocean’s motion. <term>Karma</term> creates nothing, nor does it design. > “Every individual is making <term>Karma</term>, either good or bad, in each action and thought of his daily round, and is at the same time working out in this life the <term>Karma</term> brought about by the acts and desires of the last. And now I advise you to compare our Theosophical views on <term>Karma</term>, the law of retribution, and say whether they are not both more philosophical and just than this cruel and idiotic dogma which makes "God" into a senseless fiend; namely, the principle that only the "elect" will be saved, and the rest doomed to eternal damnation!

ENQUIRER: Yes, I see what you mean generally; but I wish you could give some concrete example of how <term>Karma</term> works.

THEOSOPHIST: That I cannot do. We can only feel sure, as I said before, that our present lives and circumstances are the direct results of our own deeds and thoughts in past lives. But we, who are not seers or initiates, cannot know anything about the details of the working of the law of <term>Karma</term>.

ENQUIRER: Can anyone, even an adept or seer, trace this Karmic process of readjustment in detail?

THEOSOPHIST: Certainly. "Those who know" can do so by exercising powers that are latent in all people.

### *WHO ARE THOSE WHO KNOW?*

ENQUIRER: I am afraid such a conception would only embitter us.

THEOSOPHIST: I believe it is precisely the reverse. It is disbelief in the just law of <term>retribution</term> that is more likely to awaken every combative feeling in man. A child, as much as a man, resents a punishment—or even a reproof—that he believes to be unmerited far more than he does a severer punishment if he feels that it is merited. Belief in <term>Karma</term> is the highest reason for reconciling oneself to one’s lot in this life, and the very strongest incentive toward making an effort to better the succeeding rebirth. Both of these, indeed, would be destroyed if we supposed that our lot was the result of anything but strict *<term>Law</term>*, or that <term>destiny</term> was in any hands other than our own.

ENQUIRER: You have just asserted that this system of <term>Re-incarnation</term> under <term>Karmic law</term> appeals to reason, justice, and the moral sense. THEOSOPHIST: Only apparently, not really. No man can receive more or less than he deserves without a corresponding injustice or partiality to others; and a law which could be set aside through compassion would bring about more misery than it saved, and more irritation and curses than thanks. Remember also that we do not administer the law, even if we do create causes for its effects; it administers itself. And again, remember that the most copious...

ENQUIRER: You speak of <term>Adepts</term> as being an exception to the rule of our general ignorance. Do they really know more than we do of <term>Re-incarnation</term> and after-states?

THEOSOPHIST: They do, indeed. Through the training of faculties we all possess, but which they alone have developed to perfection, they have entered in spirit these various planes and states we have been discussing. For long ages, one generation of <term>Adepts</term> after another has studied the mysteries of being, of life, death, and rebirth, and all have taught in their turn some of the facts they learned.

ENQUIRER: And is the development of <term>Adepts</term> the aim of Theosophy?

THEOSOPHIST: Theosophy considers humanity as an emanation from divinity on its return path back to it. At an advanced point upon the path, <term>Adeptship</term> is reached by those who have devoted several <term>incarnations</term> to its achievement. For, remember well, no person has ever reached <term>Adeptship</term> in the <term>Secret Sciences</term> in one life; instead, many <term>incarnations</term> are necessary for it after the formation of a conscious purpose and the beginning of the required training. There may be many men and women in the very midst of our Society who began this uphill work toward <term>illumination</term> several <term>incarnations</term> ago, and who yet, owing to the personal illusions of the present life, are either ignorant of the fact or on the road to losing every chance in this existence of progressing any further. They feel an irresistible attraction toward <term>occultism</term> and the *<term>Higher Life</term>*, and yet are too personal and self-

<summary>The author explains that Adepts gain direct knowledge of rebirth and spiritual planes through developed internal faculties. Achieving Adeptship is a process spanning many lifetimes, and many people currently drawn to the "Higher Life" may have begun their training in previous incarnations.</summary>

THEOSOPHIST: Many are interested in our doctrines and feel instinctively that they are truer than those of any dogmatic religion. Others have formed a fixed resolve to attain the highest ideal of human duty.

### *THE DIFFERENCE BETWEEN FAITH AND KNOWLEDGE; OR, BLIND AND REASONED FAITH.*

ENQUIRER: You say that they accept and believe in the doctrines of Theosophy. But as they do not belong to those <term>Adepts</term> you have just mentioned, they must accept your teachings on *blind faith*. How does this differ from that of conventional religions?

<margin>❦</margin>
THEOSOPHIST: Just as it differs on almost all other points, it differs on this one as well. What you call "faith"—which is actually *blind faith* in reality regarding the dogmas of the Christian religions—becomes with us "*knowledge*": the logical sequence of things we *know* concerning facts in nature. Your doctrines are based upon interpretation, and therefore upon the second-hand testimony of <term>Seers</term>; ours are based upon the invariable and unvarying

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

testimony of <term>Seers</term>. Ordinary Christian theology, for instance, holds that man is a creature of God, made of three component parts—body, soul, and spirit—all essential to his wholeness. All are needed to constitute him forever, whether in the coarse form of physical earthly existence or in the ethereal form of post-resurrection experience; each man thus has a permanent existence separate from other men and from the Divine. Theosophy, on the other hand, holds that since man is an emanation from the Unknown, yet ever-present and infinite Divine Essence, his body and everything else is impermanent and therefore an illusion. Spirit alone within him is the one enduring substance, and even that loses its separate individuality at the moment of its complete reunion with the *Universal Spirit*.

ENQUIRER: If we lose even our individuality, then it simply becomes annihilation.

THEOSOPHIST: I say it *does not*, since I am speaking of *separate* individuality, not universal individuality. The latter becomes like a part transformed into the whole; the dewdrop is not evaporated but becomes the sea. Is physical man *annihilated* when he develops from a fetus into an old man? What kind of arrogant pride must be ours if we place our infinitesimally small consciousness and individuality higher than the universal and infinite consciousness!

ENQUIRER: It follows, then, that there is, *in fact*, no man, but everything is Spirit?

THEOSOPHIST: You are mistaken. It follows that the union of Spirit with matter is only temporary; or, to put it more clearly—since Spirit and matter are one, being the two opposite poles of the *universal manifested substance*—that Spirit loses its right to

ENQUIRER: And is it that “intuition” which forces you to reject God as a personal Father, Ruler, and Governor of the Universe?

THEOSOPHIST: Precisely. We believe in an ever unknowable Principle, because only blind aberration could make one maintain that the Universe, thinking man, and all the marvels contained even in the world of matter, could have grown without some *intelligent powers* to bring about the extraordinarily wise arrangement of all its parts. Nature may err, and often does, in its details and the external manifestations of its materials, but never in its inner causes and results. Ancient pagans held far more philosophical views on this question than modern philosophers, whether Agnostics, Materialists, or Christians; and no pagan writer has ever yet proposed that cruelty and mercy are not finite feelings, and can therefore be made the attributes of an *infinite god*. Their gods, therefore, were all finite. The Siamese author of the *Wheel of the Law* expresses the same idea about your personal god as we do; he says (p. 25)—

> “A Buddhist might believe in the existence of a god, sublime above all human qualities and attributes—a perfect god, above love, hatred, and jealousy, calmly resting in a quietude that nothing could disturb—and of such a god he would speak no disparagement, not from a desire to please him or fear of offending him, but from natural veneration; but he

Your faith is like the logic of certain emotional women, of whom Turgenev said that for them 2 and 2 were generally 5, and a tallow candle to boot. Furthermore, yours is a faith that clashes not only with every conceivable view of justice and logic, but which, if analyzed, leads man to moral ruin, halts the progress of mankind, and—effectively making "might" into "right"—transforms every second man into a Cain to his brother Abel.

### *HAS GOD THE RIGHT TO FORGIVE?*

**THEO.** I am referring to the Doctrine of Atonement—that dangerous dogma you believe in, which teaches us that no matter how enormous our crimes against the laws of God and man, we only have to believe in the self-sacrifice of Jesus for the salvation of mankind, and his blood will wash out every stain. I have been preaching against it for twenty years, and I will now draw your attention to a paragraph from *Isis Unveiled*, written in 1875. This is what Christianity teaches, and what we oppose:—

> “God’s mercy is boundless and unfathomable. It is impossible to imagine a human sin so damnable that the price paid in advance for the redemption of the sinner would not wipe it out, even if it were a thousand times worse. Furthermore, it is never too late to repent. Even if the offender waits until the last minute of the last hour of the last day of his mortal life before his pale lips utter the confession of faith, he may go to Paradise; the dying thief did it, and so may all others just as vile. These are the assumptions of the Church and the Clergy—assumptions hammered into the heads of your countrymen by England’s favorite preachers, right in the ‘light of the 19th century,’ this most paradoxical age of all. Now, where does this lead?”

**ENQ.** Doesn't it make the Christian happier than the Buddhist or Brahmin?

**THEO.** No; at least not the educated man, since the majority of them have long since virtually lost all belief in this cruel

> “If we step outside the narrow circle of creed and consider the universe as a whole, balanced by the exquisite adjustment of its parts, how all sound logic and the faintest glimmering sense of Justice revolt against this Vicarious Atonement! Can the results of a crime be erased even if the crime itself were pardoned? The effects of a cause are never limited to the boundaries of the cause itself, nor can the results of a crime be confined only to the offender and the victim. Every good as well as evil action has its effects, as clearly as a stone thrown into calm water. The comparison is common, but it is the best ever conceived, so let us use it. The rippling circles are larger and faster as the disturbing object is larger or smaller, but the smallest pebble—even the tiniest speck—makes its ripples. And this disturbance is not only visible on the surface. Below, unseen, in every direction—outward and downward—one drop pushes another until the sides and bottom are touched by the force. Furthermore, the air above the water is agitated, and this disturbance passes, as the physicists tell us, from layer to layer out into space forever and ever; an impulse has been given to matter, and that impulse is never lost and can never be recalled! . . .

ENQ. Yet millions believe in the Christian dogma and are happy.

THEO. Pure sentimentality overpowering their thinking faculties, which no true philanthropist or <term>Altruist</term> will ever accept. Look where it leads, and tell me the name of any pagan country where crimes are more easily committed or more numerous than in Christian lands. There, conversions achieved in prisons are more numerous than those made by public revivals and preaching. See how the balance sheet of Christian justice (!) stands: Red-handed murderers—driven by the demons of lust, revenge, greed, fanaticism, or a mere brutal thirst for blood—kill their victims, in most cases, without giving them time to repent or call on Jesus. These victims, perhaps, died in sin, and, of course—consistent with theological logic—met the reward for their greater or lesser offenses. But the murderer, caught by human justice, is imprisoned, wept over by sentimentalists, prayed with and for, and pronounces the charmed words of conver-

ENQ. The ultimate destiny of humanity, then, is not a Heaven presided over by God, but the gradual transformation of matter into its primordial element, Spirit?

THEO. That is the final goal toward which everything in nature tends.

ENQ. Do not some of you regard this association or “fall of spirit into matter” as evil, and rebirth as a sorrow?

THEO. Some do, and therefore strive to shorten their period of probation on earth. However, it is not an unmixed evil, since it ensures the experience by which we ascend to knowledge and wisdom. I mean that experience which teaches that the needs of our spiritual nature can never be met by anything other than spiritual happiness. As long as we are in the body, we are subjected to pain, suffering, and all the disappointing incidents that occur during life. Therefore, to alleviate this, we finally acquire knowledge, which alone can provide us relief and hope for a better future.

## XII.

# WHAT IS PRACTICAL THEOSOPHY?

---

->*DUTY.*<-

ENQUIRER: Why, then, is there a need for rebirths, since they all fail to achieve a permanent peace?

THEOSOPHIST: Because the final goal cannot be reached in any way except through life experiences, and because the majority of these consist of pain and suffering. It is only through suffering that we can learn. Joys and pleasures teach us nothing; they are fleeting and can only bring weariness in the long run. Moreover, our constant failure to find any permanent satisfaction in life that would meet the requirements of our higher nature shows us plainly that those needs can be met only on their own plane—namely, the spiritual.

ENQUIRER: Is the natural result of this a desire to end life by one means or another?

THEOSOPHIST: If you mean by such a desire “suicide,” then I say, most definitely not. Such a result can never be a “natural” one, but is always due to a morbid brain disease, or to very definite and strong materialistic views. It is the worst of crimes and

It also argues that suicide is never a natural spiritual result, but rather a consequence of mental illness or materialistic beliefs.</summary>

<keywords>rebirth, suffering, spiritual growth, higher nature, suicide, materialism, Practical Theosophy, duty</keywords>

terrible in its results. But if by desire, you mean simply an aspiration to reach a spiritual existence, and not a wish to leave the earth, then I would indeed call it a very natural desire. Otherwise, voluntary death would be an abandonment of our current post and of the duties that are our responsibility, as well as an attempt to evade <term>Karmic</term> responsibilities, and would thus involve the creation of new <term>Karma</term>.

ENQUIRER: But if actions on the material plane are unsatisfying, why should duties, which are such actions, be mandatory?

THEOSOPHIST: First of all, because our philosophy teaches us that the goal of performing our duties toward all people—and to ourselves last—is not the attainment of personal happiness, but the happiness of others; it is the fulfillment of what is right for the sake of right, not for what it may bring us. Happiness, or rather contentment, may indeed follow the performance of duty, but it is not and must not be the motive for it.

ENQUIRER: What precisely do you understand by “duty” in <term>Theosophy</term>? It cannot be the Christian duties preached by Jesus and his Apostles, since you recognize neither?

THEOSOPHIST: You are mistaken once more. What you call “Christian duties” were instilled by every great moral and religious reformer ages before the Christian era. All that was great, generous, and heroic was, in ancient days, not only talked about and preached from pulpits as in our own time, but sometimes acted upon by entire nations. The history of the Buddhist reform is full of the most noble and most heroically unselfish acts.

"...returning evil for evil, or insult for insult; but on the contrary, blessing" was practically practiced by the followers of Buddha several centuries before Peter. The ethics of Christianity are grand, no doubt; but undeniably they are not new, and they originated as "pagan" duties.

ENQUIRER: And how would you define these duties, or "duty" in general, as you understand the term?

THEOSOPHIST: Duty is that which *is due* to humanity, to our fellow human beings, neighbors, family, and especially that which we owe to all those who are poorer and more helpless than we are. This is a debt which, if left unpaid during life, leaves us spiritually insolvent and morally bankrupt in our next incarnation. Theosophy is the essence of duty.

ENQUIRER: So is Christianity, when rightly understood and practiced.

THEOSOPHIST: No doubt it is; but if it were not a *religion of mere words* in practice, Theosophy would have little to do among Christians. Unfortunately, it is only such superficial ethics. Those who practice their duty toward everyone, and for duty's own sake, are few; and fewer still are those who perform that duty while remaining content with the satisfaction of their own private conscience. It is—

> ". . . . . the public voice
> Of praise that honors virtue and rewards it,"

which is always foremost in the minds of "world-renowned" philanthropists. Modern ethics are beautiful to read about and hear discussed; but what are words unless they are converted into actions? Finally, if you ask me how we understand Theosophical duty practically and in light of <term>Karma</term>, I may answer you that our duty is to drink without a murmur to the last

ENQUIRER: All this is very vague. What do you do more than Christians do?

THEOSOPHIST: It is not what we members of the Theosophical Society do—though some of us try our best—but how much further Theosophy leads toward good than modern Christianity does. I say—*action*, enforced action, instead of mere intention and talk. A man may be whatever he likes—the most worldly, selfish, and hard-hearted of men, even a deep-dyed rascal—and it will not prevent him from calling himself a Christian, or others from viewing him as one. But no Theosophist has the right to this name unless he is thoroughly convinced of the truth of Carlyle’s truism: “The end of man is an *action* and not a *thought*, even if it were the noblest”—and unless he sets and models his daily life upon this truth. Professing a truth is not yet enacting it; and the more beautiful and grand it sounds, and the more loudly virtue or duty is talked about instead of being acted upon, the more strongly it will always remind one of Dead Sea fruit <note>A metaphor for something that appears promising but is actually deceptive or disappointing.</note>. Cant <note>Hypocritical or sanctimonious talk, especially regarding religion or morals.</note> is the most loathsome of all vices; and *cant* is the most prominent feature of the greatest Protestant country of this century—England.

ENQUIRER: What do you consider as due to humanity at large?

THEOSOPHIST: Full recognition of equal rights and privileges for all, without distinction of race, color, social position, or birth.

ENQUIRER: When would you consider what is due as not being given?

THEOSOPHIST: When there is the slightest violation of another’s right—whether that other is an individual or a nation; when there is any failure to show them the same justice, kindness, consideration, or mercy which we desire for ourselves. The entire current political system is built on the disregard of such rights and the aggressive assertion of national selfishness. The French say, “As the master is, so is the servant”; they should add, “As the national policy is, so is the citizen.”

ENQUIRER: Do you take any part in politics?

THEOSOPHIST: As a Society, we carefully avoid them for the reasons given below. To seek to achieve political reforms before we have brought about a reform *in human nature* is like putting *new wine into old bottles*. Make people feel and recognize in their innermost hearts what their real, true duty to all others is, and every old abuse of power, every wicked law in national policy based on human, social, or political selfishness, will disappear on its own. The gardener is foolish who seeks to weed his flowerbed of poisonous plants by cutting them off from the surface of the soil instead of tearing them out by the roots. No lasting political reform can ever be achieved with the same selfish men at the head of affairs as in the past.

### *THE RELATIONSHIP OF THE T.S. TO POLITICAL REFORMS.*

ENQUIRER: The Theosophical Society is not, then, a political organization?

THEOSOPHIST: Certainly not. It is international in the highest sense, as its members include men and women of all races and creeds,

ENQUIRER: Why is this?

While, from the very nature of their position as Theosophists, the members of the T.S. agree on the principles of Theosophy—otherwise they would not belong to the society at all—it does not follow that they agree on every other subject. As a society, they can only act together in matters common to all—that is, in Theosophy itself. As individuals, each is left perfectly free to follow his or her own particular line of political thought and action, as long as this does not conflict with Theosophical principles or harm the Theosophical Society.

ENQUIRER: But surely the T.S. does not stand completely aloof from the social questions that are now coming to the forefront so rapidly?

THEOSOPHIST: The very principles of the T.S. are proof that it does not—or rather, that most of its members do not—stand aloof. If humanity can only be developed mentally and spiritually by first enforcing the soundest and most scientific physiological laws, it is the solemn duty of all who strive for this development to do their utmost to see that those laws are generally carried out. All Theosophists are only too sadly aware that, in Western countries especially, the social condition of large masses of people makes it impossible for either their bodies or their spirits to be properly trained,

Both are the effects of the conditions surrounding those who are subject to them, and the neglect of social duty on one side is most closely connected with the stunted and arrested development on the other. In sociology, as in all branches of true science, the law of universal causation holds good. But this causation necessarily implies, as its logical outcome, that human solidarity on which Theosophy so strongly insists. If the action of one person reacts on the lives of *all*—and this is the true scientific idea—then it is only by all men becoming brothers and all women sisters, and by everyone practicing true brotherhood and sisterhood in their daily lives, that the real human solidarity, which lies at the root of the elevation of the race, can ever be attained. It is this action and interaction—this true brotherhood and sisterhood, in which each person shall live for *all* and all for each—which is one of the fundamental Theosophical principles that every Theosophist should be bound, not only to teach, but to carry out in his or her individual life.

**THEOSOPHIST.** It is this very law of Karma which gives strength to all that I have said. The individual cannot separate himself from the human race, nor the race from the individual. The law of Karma applies equally to all, although all are not equally developed. In helping the development of others, the Theosophist believes that he is not only helping them to fulfill their Karma, but that he is also, in the strictest sense, fulfilling his own. It is the development of humanity, of which both he and they are integral parts, that he always has in view, and he knows that any failure on his part to respond to the highest within him hinders not only himself but everyone in their progressive march. By his actions, he can make it either more difficult or easier for humanity to attain the next higher plane of being.

**THEOSOPHIST.** The connection is most intimate. If our present lives depend upon the development of certain principles that are a growth from the seeds left by a previous existence, the law holds true regarding the future. Once you grasp the idea that universal causation is not merely present, but past, present, and future, every action on our present plane falls naturally and easily into its true place, and is seen in its true relation to ourselves and to others. Every mean and selfish action sends us backward and not forward, while every noble thought and every unselfish deed are stepping stones to higher and more glorious planes of being. If this life were all, then in many respects it would indeed be poor and mean; but regarded as a preparation for the next sphere of existence, it may be used as the golden gate through which we may pass—not selfishly and alone, but in company with our fellows—to the palaces that lie beyond.

### *ON SELF-SACRIFICE.*

**ENQUIRER.** Is equal justice to all and love for every creature the highest standard of Theosophy?

**THEOSOPHIST.** No; there is one that is even far higher.

**ENQUIRER.** What can it be?

**THEOSOPHIST.** Giving to others *more* than to oneself—*self-sacrifice*. Such was the standard and abundant measure that so preeminently marked the greatest Teachers and Masters of Humanity—for example, Gautama Buddha in history, and Jesus of Nazareth as shown in the Gospels. This trait alone was enough to

ensure for them the perpetual reverence and gratitude of the generations of men that come after them. We say, however, that self-sacrifice must be performed with discrimination; and such self-abandonment, if made without justice or blindly, regardless of subsequent results, may often prove to be not only in vain, but harmful. One of the fundamental rules of Theosophy is justice to oneself—viewed as a unit of collective humanity, not as a personal self—justice that is no more but no less than what is given to others; unless, indeed, by the sacrifice of the *one* self we can benefit the many.

**ENQUIRER.** Could you make your idea clearer by giving an example?

**THEOSOPHIST.** There are many examples in history to illustrate it. Theosophy holds that self-sacrifice for a practical good to save many, or even several people, is far higher than self-denial for a sectarian idea, such as "saving the heathen from damnation," for instance. In our opinion, Father Damien—the young man of thirty who offered his whole life as a sacrifice for the benefit and alleviation of the suffering of the lepers at Molokai, and who went to live for eighteen years alone with them, only to finally catch the loathsome disease and die—*did not die in vain.* He gave relief and relative happiness to thousands of miserable people. He brought them mental and physical consolation. He threw a beam of light into the black and dreary night of an existence whose hopelessness is unparalleled in the records of human suffering. He was a *true* Theosophist, and his memory will live forever in our annals. In our view, this poor Belgian priest stands immeasurably higher than—for instance—all those sincere but

**ENQ.** But how are we to reach such an elevated status?

**THEO.** By the enlightened application of our principles to practice. **ENQ.** If these are our duties to humanity at large, what do you understand as our duties to our immediate surroundings?

**THEO.** Exactly the same, *plus* those that arise from special obligations regarding family ties.

**ENQ.** Then it is not true, as is often said, that as soon as a man enters the Theosophical Society, he begins to be gradually separated from his wife, children, and family duties?

**THEO.** It is a groundless slander, like so many others. The first Theosophical duty is to do one’s duty by *all* people, and especially by those to whom one has specific responsibilities—either because one has voluntarily undertaken them, such as marriage ties, or because destiny has linked one to them; I mean those duties we owe to parents or next of kin.

**ENQ.** And what is the duty of a Theosophist to himself?

**THEO.** To control and conquer the *lower self* through the *Higher self*. To purify himself inwardly and morally; to fear no one and nothing, except for the tribunal of his own conscience. Never to do anything by halves; that is, if he thinks it is the right thing to do, let him do it openly and boldly, and if it is wrong, never touch it at all. It is the duty of a Theosophist to lighten his burden by thinking of the wise aphorism of Epictetus, who says: “*Do not be diverted from your duty by any idle reflection the foolish world may make upon you, for their criticisms are not in your power, and consequently should not be any part of your concern.*”

**ENQ.** But suppose a member of your Society should plead an inability to practice altruism toward other people, on the ground that “charity begins at home”; arguing that he is too busy or too poor to benefit mankind or even any of its individuals—what are your rules in such a case?

**THEO.** No man has a right to say that he can do nothing for others, on any pretext whatever. “By doing the proper duty in the proper place, a man may make the world his debtor,” says an English writer. A cup of cold water given in time to a thirsty wayfarer is a nobler duty and worth more than a dozen dinners given away, out of season, to men who can afford to pay for them. No man who does not have it in him will ever become a *Theosophist*; but he may remain a member of our Society all the same. We have no rules by which we could force any man to become a practical Theosophist if he does not desire to be one.

**ENQ.** Then why does he enter the Society at all?

**THEO.** That is best known to him who does so. For, here again,

In our day, *vox populi* <note>The voice of the people.</note> (so far as it regards the voice of the educated, at any rate) is no longer *vox dei* <note>The voice of God.</note>, but instead that of prejudice, of selfish motives, and often simply that of unpopularity. Our duty is to sow seeds broadcast for the future, and see that they are good; not to stop to inquire *why* we should do so, and how and for what reason we are obliged to spend our time, since those who will reap the harvest in days to come will never be ourselves.

### ON CHARITY.

**ENQ.** How do you Theosophists regard the Christian duty of charity?

**THEO.** What charity do you mean? Charity of mind, or practical charity on the physical plane?

**ENQ.** I mean practical charity, as your idea of Universal brotherhood would include, of course, charity of mind.

**THEO.** Then you have in your mind the practical carrying out of the commandments given by Jesus in the Sermon on the Mount?

**ENQ.** Precisely so.

**THEO.** Then why call them “Christian”? Because, although your Savior preached and practiced them, the last thing the Christians of today think of is to carry them out in their lives.

**ENQ.** And yet many are those who spend their lives giving charity?

**THEO.** Yes, out of the surplus of their great fortunes. **ENQ.** Ah, but you must remember that these precepts are not to be taken literally. Times and circumstances have changed since Christ’s day. Moreover, He spoke in parables.

**THEO.** Then why don’t your Churches teach that the doctrine of damnation and hellfire is to be understood as a parable too? Why do some of your most popular preachers—while virtually allowing these "parables" to be understood as you take them—insist on the literal meaning of the fires of Hell and the *physical* tortures of an "asbestos-like" soul? If one is a "parable," then the other is. If hellfire is a literal truth, then Christ’s commandments in the Sermon on the Mount must be obeyed to the very letter. And I tell you that many who do not believe in the divinity of Christ—like Count Leo Tolstoy and more than one Theosophist—actually carry out these noble, because universal, precepts literally; and many more good men and women would do so, were they not more than certain that such a way of life would very probably land them in a mental asylum—so *Christian* are your laws!

**ENQ.** But surely everyone knows that millions and millions are spent annually on private and public charities?

**THEO.** Oh, yes; half of which sticks to the hands it passes through

**ENQ.** What would you do, then?

**ENQ.** But how can this be applied practically?

**THEO.** The Theosophical ideas of charity mean *personal exertion* for others; *personal mercy and kindness*; *personal interest* in the welfare of those who suffer; *personal sympathy*, forethought, and assistance in their troubles or needs. We Theosophists do not believe in giving money (N.B., if we had it) through other people's hands or organizations. We believe in giving to that money a thousandfold greater power and effectiveness through our personal contact and sympathy with those who need it. Where is the gratitude that your “millions of pounds” should have inspired, or the good feelings they should have provoked? Is it shown in the hatred the East End poor have for the rich? In the growth of the parties of anarchy and disorder? Or by those thousands of unfortunate working girls, victims of the “sweating” system, who are driven daily to scrape together a living by turning to the streets? Do your helpless old men and women thank you for the workhouses? Or do your poor thank you for the poisonously unhealthy dwellings where they are allowed to breed new generations of diseased, scrofulous, and rickety children, only to put money into the pockets of the insatiable Shylocks who own the houses? This is why every sovereign out of all those “millions,” contributed by good and well-meaning charitable people, falls like a burning curse instead of a blessing on the poor it is meant to relieve. We call this *generating national Karma*, and its results will be terrible on the day of reckoning.

**THEO.** If we had more money, and if most Theosophists did not have to work for their daily bread, I firmly believe we could.

**ENQ.** How? Do you expect that your doctrines could ever take hold

And what is more likely to produce such a feeling than a philosophy so grand, so consistent, so logical, and so all-embracing as our Eastern Doctrines?

Once you teach them that greatest of all laws, *Karma* and *Reincarnation*, they will not only feel the true dignity of human nature within themselves, but they will also turn from evil and avoid it as they would a physical danger.

The few who have the means to perform what are usually called charitable actions follow the Buddhist precepts and do their work themselves, rather than through a representative or by subscribing publicly to charitable funds. Justice consists in doing no injury to any living being; but justice also commands us never to allow injury to be done to the many, or even to a single innocent person, by allowing the guilty one to go unchecked.

ENQ. What are the other negative clauses?

THEO. No Theosophist should be content with an idle or frivolous life, doing no real good to himself and even less to others. One cancerous limb infects the entire body.

ENQ. Is it the duty of every member to teach others and preach Theosophy?

THEO. It is indeed. No member has a right to remain idle on the excuse that they know too little to teach. For they can always be sure that they will find others who know even less than themselves. Also, it is not until a person begins to try to teach others that they discover their own ignorance and try to correct it. But this is a minor clause.

ENQ. What do you consider, then, to be the chief of these negative Theosophical duties?

He thus shifts the blame from his own shoulders onto those of the Society in general, and its leaders especially. Such persons remind one of the old fable about the man with a distorted face who broke his mirror because it reflected his countenance as crooked.

Still another wanted to, and actually *did*, practice *black magic*—that is, undue personal psychological influence on certain <term>Fellows</term>, while pretending devotion and every Theosophical virtue. When this was stopped, the member broke with Theosophy and now slanders and lies against those same hapless leaders in the most virulent manner, endeavoring to break up the society by blackening the reputation of those whom that worthy "Fellow" was unable to deceive.

**THEO.** If by allowing them to go on unchecked other people may be injured, it is certainly our duty to prevent the danger by warning them privately. But whether true or false, no accusation against another person should ever be spread abroad. If it is true, and the fault hurts no one but the sinner, then leave him to his Karma. If it is false, then you will have avoided adding to the injustice in the world. Therefore, keep silent about such things with everyone not directly concerned. But if your discretion and silence are likely to hurt or endanger others, then I add: Speak the truth at all costs, and say, with Annesly, “Consult duty, not events.” There are cases when one is forced to exclaim, “Perish discretion, rather than allow it to interfere with duty.”

**ENQ.** I think that if you carry out these maxims, you are likely to reap a significant crop of troubles!

**THEO.** And so we do. We have to admit that we are now open to the same taunt that the early Christians were. “See how these Theosophists love one another!” may now be said of us without a shadow of injustice.

The former is only too ready to believe anything said against a society it hates. As for the latter—but I will say no more, fearing I have already said too much.

They have already found a few such noble and devoted souls to replace them. Thanks to these few, the coming generations will find the path to peace a little less thorny and the way a little wider. Thus, all this suffering will have produced good results, and their self-sacrifice will not have been in vain. At present, the main, fundamental object of the Society is to sow seeds in the hearts of people, which may in time sprout and, under more favorable circumstances, lead to a healthy reform that brings more happiness *to the masses* than they have enjoyed until now.

# XIII.

## ON THE MISCONCEPTIONS ABOUT THE THEOSOPHICAL SOCIETY.

### *THEOSOPHY AND ASCETICISM.*

ENQ. I have heard people say that your rules require all members to be vegetarians, celibates, and rigid <term>ascetics</term>; but you have not told me anything of the sort yet. Can you tell me the truth once and for all about this?

THEO. The truth is that our rules require nothing of the kind. The <term>Theosophical Society</term> does not even expect, much less require, of *any* of its members that they should be <term>ascetics</term> in any way—unless you call it asceticism to try to benefit other people and be unselfish in their own lives.

ENQ. But many of your members are still strict vegetarians and openly declare their intention to remain unmarried. This is also most often the case with those who take a prominent part in the work of your Society.

THEO. That is only natural, because most of our truly earnest workers are members of the <term>Inner Section</term> of the Society, which I mentioned to you before.

<summary>The Theosophist clarifies that the Society does not require members to practice asceticism, vegetarianism, or celibacy, though many dedicated members of the Inner Section choose to do so voluntarily.</summary>
<keywords>Theosophical Society, asceticism, vegetarianism, celibacy, Inner Section, unselfishness, membership rules</keywords>

--- Page 275 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion regarding the practices of the Inner Section and how they relate to asceticism and personal development, transitioning from the general rules of the Society to the specific motivations of its most dedicated students.</meta>

-> THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY. <-

ENQ. Oh! Then do you require ascetic practices in that Inner Section?

THEO. No; we do not require or demand them even there; but I see that I had better give you an explanation of our views on the subject of <term>asceticism</term> in general, and then you will understand about <term>vegetarianism</term> and so forth.

ENQ. Please proceed.

THEO. As I have already told you, most people who become truly earnest students of <term>Theosophy</term> and active workers in our Society wish to do more than theoretically study the truths we teach. They wish to know the truth through their own direct personal experience and to study <term>Occultism</term> with the goal of acquiring the wisdom and power they feel they need to help others effectively and judiciously, instead of blindly and at random. Therefore, sooner or later, they join the <term>Inner Section</term>.

ENQ. But you said that “ascetic practices” are not obligatory even in that Inner Section?

THEO. Nor are they; but the first thing that members learn there is a true understanding of the relationship between the body, or physical sheath, to the inner, true man. The relationship and mutual interaction between these two aspects of human nature are explained and demonstrated to them, so that they soon become filled with the supreme importance of the inner man over the outer case or body. They are taught that blind, unintelligent <term>asceticism</term> is mere folly; that conduct such as that of <note>Saint Benedict Joseph Labre (1748–1783), a Catholic saint known for extreme poverty and mendicancy.</note> St. Labro, whom I mentioned before, or that of the Indian <term>Fakirs</term> and jungle ascetics, who cut, burn, and waste away their bodies in the most cruel and horrible manner, is simply self-

-> THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY. <-

torture for selfish ends—that is, to develop will-power—but is perfectly useless for the purpose of assisting true spiritual, or Theosophical, development.

ENQ. I see; you regard only moral asceticism as necessary. It is a means to an end, that end being the perfect equilibrium of the *inner* nature of human beings, and the attainment of complete mastery over the body with all its passions and desires?

THEO. Just so. But these means must be used intelligently and wisely, not blindly and foolishly; like an athlete who is training and preparing for a great contest, not like the miser who starves himself into illness so that he may gratify his passion for gold.

ENQ. I understand your general idea now; but let us see how you apply it in practice. How about <term>vegetarianism</term>, for instance?

THEO. One of the great German scientists has shown that every kind of animal tissue, however you may cook it, still retains certain marked characteristics of the animal to which it belonged, which can be recognized. And apart from that, everyone knows by the taste what meat they are eating. We go a step further and prove that when the flesh of animals is assimilated by humans as food, it imparts to them, physiologically, some of the characteristics of the animal it came from. Moreover, <term>occult science</term> teaches and proves this to its students by visible demonstration, showing also that this “coarsening” or “animalizing” effect on humans is greatest from the flesh of larger animals, less for birds, still less for fish and other cold-blooded animals, and least of all when one eats only vegetables.

ENQ. Then should one not eat at all?
