# GLOSSARY.

Soul (Buddhi-Manas) or *Ego* is taught in the Bible just as it is in all other ancient scriptures; "resurrection" means only the *rebirth of the Ego* in another form. (See *Theosophical Glossary*.)
**Reuchlin, John.** A great German philosopher and philologist, Kabbalist, and scholar. He was born at Pforzheim, Germany, in 1455, and was a diplomat in his early youth. At one period of his life, he held the high office of judge of the tribunal at Tübingen, where he remained for eleven years. He was also the teacher of Melanchthon and was greatly persecuted by the clergy for his glorification of the Hebrew Kabbalah, though he was also called the "Father of the Reformation." He died in 1522 in great poverty—the common fate of all those who, in those days, went against the literal interpretations of the Church.

->S.<-

**Sacred Science.** The title given to the occult sciences in general, and by the Rosicrucians to the Kabbalah, and especially to Hermetic philosophy.
**Samadhi.** The name in India for spiritual ecstasy. It is a state of complete trance induced by means of mystic concentration.
**Samkhara.** One of the five Buddhist *Skandhas* or attributes. (See "Skandhas.") "Tendencies of the mind."
**Samma Sambuddha.** The sudden remembrance of all of one’s past incarnations—a phenomenon of memory obtained through Yoga. A Buddhist mystic term.
**Samothrace.** An island in the Grecian Archipelago, famous in ancient times for the mysteries celebrated in its temples. These mysteries were world-renowned.
**Samyuttaka Nikaya.** One of the Buddhist *Sutras*.

<meta>This page continues the alphabetical glossary of terms, moving from the definition of Buddhist "Skandhas" and Kabbalistic concepts to modern psychological and spiritual movements such as Spiritism and Spiritualism.</meta>

<page-num>364</page-num>
# GLOSSARY.

**Sanna.** One of the five Skandhas, or attributes, meaning "abstract ideas."

**Séance.** A term now used to denote a session with a medium for various phenomena. Used chiefly among spiritualists.

**Self.** There are two Selves in human beings—the Higher and the Lower, the Impersonal and the Personal Self. One is divine, the other semi-animal. A great distinction should be made between the two.

**Sephiroth.** A Hebrew Kabbalistic word for the ten divine emanations from Ain-Soph, the impersonal, universal Principle, or DEITY. (*See Theosophical Glossary.*)

**Skandhas.** The attributes of every personality which, after death, form the basis, so to speak, for a new Karmic reincarnation. They are five in the popular or exoteric system of the Buddhists: namely, *Rupa*, form or body, which leaves behind its magnetic atoms and occult affinities; *Vedana*, sensations, which do the same; *Sanna*, or abstract ideas, which are the creative powers at work from one incarnation to another; *Samkhara*, tendencies of the mind; and *Vinnana*, mental powers.

**Somnambulism.** "Sleepwalking." A psycho-physiological state, too well known to need explanation.

**Spiritism.** The same as the above, with the difference that Spiritualists reject almost unanimously the doctrine of Reincarnation, while Spiritists make it the fundamental principle of their belief. There is, however, a vast difference between the views of the latter and the philosophical teachings of Eastern Occultists. Spiritists belong to the French School founded by Allan Kardec, and the Spiritualists of America and England to that of the "Fox girls," who inaugurated their theories at Rochester, U.S.A. Theosophists, while believing in the mediumistic phenomena of both Spiritualists and Spiritists, reject the idea of "spirits."

**Spiritualism.** The modern belief that the spirits of the dead return to earth to commune with the living. (See "Spiritism.")

<page-num>365</page-num>

# GLOSSARY.

**St. Germain (Count).** A mysterious figure who appeared in the 18th and early 19th centuries in France, England, and elsewhere.

**Sthula Sharîra.** The Sanskrit name for the human physical body in Occultism and Vedanta philosophy.

***Sthulopadhi.** The physical body in its waking, conscious state (*Jagrat*).

***Sukshmopadhi.** The physical body in the dreaming state (*Svapna*), and *Karanopadhi*, the "causal body."

**Summerland.** The fanciful name given by Spiritualists to the home of their disembodied "spirits," which they locate somewhere in the Milky Way. It is described on the testimony of returning "spirits" as a lovely land featuring beautiful cities and buildings, a Congress Hall, museums, and so on. (See the works of Andrew Jackson Davis.)

**Swedenborg (Emanuel).** A famous scholar and clairvoyant of the 18th century; a man of great learning who contributed significantly to science, but whose mysticism and transcendental philosophy placed him among visionary hallucinators. He is now universally known as the founder of the Swedenborgian sect, or the New Jerusalem Church. He was born in Stockholm, Sweden, in 1688, to Lutheran parents; his father was the Bishop of West Gothland. His original name was Swedberg, but after being ennobled and knighted in 1719, it was changed to Swedenborg. He became a mystic in 1743, and four years later (in 1747), he resigned his office as Assessor Extraordinary to the College of Mines and dedicated himself entirely to mysticism. He died in 1772.

### T.

**Taijas** (*Sans.*) From *tejas* “fire”; meaning the “radiant” or the “luminous.” It refers to the *manasa rupa*, “the body of Manas,” as well as to the stars and the star-like shining envelopes. This is a term in Vedanta philosophy that has other meanings besides the occult definition given here.

**Taraka Raj Yoga** (*Sans.*) One of the Brahmanical Yoga systems; it is the most philosophical and, in fact, the most secret of all, as its real tenets are never shared publicly. It is a purely intellectual and spiritual school of training.

**Tetragrammaton** (*Gr.*) The name of the deity in four letters, which in their English form are IHVH. It is a Kabbalistic term and corresponds on a more material plane to the sacred Pythagorean *Tetraktys*. (See the *Theosophical Glossary*.)

**Theodidaktos** (*Gr.*) The “God-taught,” a title applied to Ammonius Saccas.

**Theogony.** From the Greek *theogonia*, literally the “Genesis of the Gods.”

**Theosophia** (*Gr.*) Literally, “divine wisdom or the wisdom of the gods.” [For a fuller explanation of such words as “Theosophy,” “Theosophists,” “Theosophical Society,” etc., see the *Theosophical Glossary*.]

**Therapeutæ**, or **Therapeuts** (*Gr.*) A school of Jewish mystic healers or esotericists, whom some wrongly refer to as a sect. They lived in and near Alexandria, and their activities and beliefs remain a mystery to critics to this day, as their philosophy appears to be a combination of Orphic, Pythagorean, Essenian, and purely Kabbalistic practices. (See the *Theosophical Glossary*.)

<meta>This page continues the alphabetical glossary of terms, moving from definitions related to various systems of Yoga and Greek philosophy to terms concerning divine magic, the soul, and the organizational principles of the Theosophical Society.</meta>

<page-num>367</page-num>

<header>GLOSSARY.</header>

**Theurgy** (from the Greek *theiourgiá*). Rites for bringing planetary and other spirits or gods down to earth. To achieve such an objective, the theurgist had to be absolutely pure and unselfish in his motives. The practice of theurgy is very undesirable and even dangerous today. The world has become too corrupt and wicked for the practice of that which only such holy and learned men as Ammonius, Plotinus, Porphyry, and Iamblichus (the most learned theurgist of all) could attempt with impunity. In our day, theurgy—or divine, beneficent magic—is all too likely to become *goëtic*, or in other words, sorcery. Theurgy is the first of the three subdivisions of magic, which are theurgic, goëtic, and natural magic.

**Thread Soul.** The same as *Sutratma*, which see.

**Thumos** (*Gr.*) A Pythagorean and Platonic term applied to an aspect of the human soul to denote its passionate *Kamarupic* condition; it is almost equivalent to the Sanskrit word *tamas*, "the quality of darkness," and is probably derived from it.

**Timæus** (*of Locris*). A Pythagorean philosopher born at Locris. He differed somewhat from his teacher in the doctrine of metempsychosis. He wrote a treatise on the Soul of the World and its nature and essence, which is written in the Doric dialect and still survives.

**Triad or Trinity.** In every religion and philosophy—the three in One.

## U.

**Universal Brotherhood.** The sub-title of the Theosophical Society, and the first of the three objects professed by it.

**Upadhi** (*Sans.*) The basis of something, a substructure; as in Occultism—substance is the *upadhi* of Spirit.

**Upanishad** (*Sans.*) Literally, "Esoteric Doctrine." The third division

<keywords>Theurgy, divine magic, sorcery, Ammonius, Iamblichus, Thumos, Timæus of Locris, Universal Brotherhood, Upadhi, Upanishad, Theosophical Society</keywords>

--- Page 384 ---
<meta>This page continues the alphabetical glossary of terms, completing the entry for "Upanishad" and beginning the section for the letter "V." It follows the discussion of the "Upadhi" and the introductory definition of the "Upanishad."</meta>

<page-num>368</page-num>

<header>GLOSSARY.</header>

of the Vedas, and are classified with revelations (*Sruti* or the “revealed word”). About 150 of the Upanishads still exist, though no more than about twenty can be fully relied upon as being free from falsification. These all date from before the sixth century B.C. Just as the Kabala interprets the esoteric sense of the Bible, the *Upanishads* explain the mystical sense of the Vedas. Professor Cowell makes two statements regarding the *Upanishads* that are as interesting as they are accurate. Thus he says: (1) These works have “one remarkable peculiarity: the total absence of any Brahmanical exclusiveness in their doctrine. . . . They reflect an entirely different spirit—a freedom of thought unknown in any earlier work except the *Rig Veda* hymns themselves; and (2) the great teachers of the higher knowledge (*Gupta Vidya*) and the Brahmans are continually depicted as going to Kshatriya Kings to become their pupils” (*chelas*). This shows conclusively that (*a*) the *Upanishads* were written before the enforcement of caste and Brahmanical power, and are therefore second only to the Vedas in antiquity; and (*b*) the occult sciences or “higher knowledge,” as Cowell phrases it, are much older than the Brahmans in India, or even their existence as a caste. The *Upanishads* are, however, much later than Gupta Vidya, or the “Secret Science,” which is as old as human philosophical thought itself.

# V.

**Vahan** (*Sans.*) “Vehicle,” a synonym for *Upadhi*.

**Vallabachâryas Sect** (*Sans.*), or the “Sect of the *Maharajas*,” is a licentious phallic-worshipping community whose main branch is in Bombay. The object of their worship is the infant Krishna. The Anglo-Indian Government was forced to interfere several times to stop its rites and vile practices; its governing Maharajah, a

<summary>This page concludes the definition of the Upanishads, emphasizing their antiquity and lack of Brahmanical exclusiveness, and introduces the terms Vahan and the Vallabachâryas Sect.</summary>

<keywords>Upanishads, Vedas, Sruti, Professor Cowell, Gupta Vidya, Kshatriya, Vahan, Vallabachâryas Sect, Krishna, Brahmanism</keywords>

--- Page 385 ---
<meta>This page continues the alphabetical glossary, finishing the description of the Vallabachâryas Sect and providing definitions for terms beginning with V, W, and Y, including Vedanta, Wisdom-Religion, and Yoga.</meta>

<page-num>369</page-num>

<header>GLOSSARY.</header>

kind of High Priest, was more than once imprisoned, and very justly so. It is one of the darkest spots of India.

**Vedanta** (*Sans.*) Meaning literally, the “end of all knowledge.” Among the six *Darsanas* or schools of philosophy, it is also called *Uttaramimânsa*, or the “later” Mimansa. There are those who, unable to understand its esotericism, consider it atheistic; but this is not the case, as Sankarâchârya—the great apostle of this school and its popularizer—was one of the greatest mystics and adepts of India.

**Vidya** (*Sans.*) Knowledge, or rather “Wisdom Knowledge.”

**Vinnana** (*Sans.*) One of five Skandhas; meaning literally, “mental powers.” (See “Skandhas.”)

## W.

**Wisdom-Religion.** The same as Theosophy. The name given to the secret doctrine which underlies every exoteric scripture and religion.

## Y.

**Yoga** (*Sans.*) A school of philosophy founded by Patanjali, but which existed as a distinct teaching and system of life long before that sage. It is Yâjnawalkya, a famous and very ancient sage to whom the *White Yajur Veda*, the *Satapatha Brahmâna*, and the *Brihak Aranyaka* are attributed and who lived in pre-Mahabharatan times, who is credited with teaching the necessity and positive duty of religious meditation and retirement into the forests; he, therefore, is believed to have originated the Yoga doctrine. Professor Max Müller states that it is Yâjnawalkya who prepared the world for the preaching of Buddha.

<meta>This page continues the alphabetical glossary from the previous page, completing the entries for the letter Y and providing definitions for terms beginning with Z, including Yuga, the Zohar, and Zoroastrianism.</meta>

<page-num>370</page-num>

<header>GLOSSARY.</header>

Patanjali’s Yoga, however, is more definite and precise as a philosophy and incorporates more of the occult sciences than any of the works attributed to Yajnavalkya.

**Yogi or Yogin** (*Sanskrit*). A devotee; one who practices the Yoga system. There are various grades and kinds of Yogis, and the term has now become a generic name in India to designate every type of ascetic.

**Yuga** (*Sanskrit*). An age of the world. There are four of these which follow each other in a series: namely, *Krita* (or *Satya*) *Yuga*, the golden age; *Treta Yuga*; *Dwapara Yuga*; and finally *Kali Yuga*, the black age—the one in which we now live. (See *The Secret Doctrine* for a full description.)

### Z.

**Zenobia.** The Queen of Palmyra, who was defeated by the Emperor Aurelian. In the third century A.D., she had the famous critic and logician Longinus as her instructor. (See “Longinus.”)

**Zivo, Kabar** (or Yukabar). The name of one of the creative deities in the Nazarene Codex. (See *Isis Unveiled*.)

**Zohar** (*Hebrew*). The “Book of Splendor,” a Kabbalistic work attributed to Simeon Ben Iochai in the first century of our era. (See *The Theosophical Glossary* for a fuller explanation.)

**Zoroastrian.** One who follows the religion of the Parsis—those who worship the sun or fire.

# APPENDIX.

## THE THEOSOPHICAL SOCIETY.

### INFORMATION FOR INQUIRERS.

The Theosophical Society was formed in New York on November 17, 1875. Its founders believed that the best interests of religion and science would be promoted by the revival of Sanskrit, Pali, Zend, and other ancient literature, in which the sages and initiates had preserved for the use of humanity truths of the highest value regarding man and nature. A society of an absolutely non-sectarian character, whose work should be amicably pursued by the learned of all races, in a spirit of unselfish devotion to the research of truth, and with the purpose of disseminating it impartially, seemed likely to do much to check materialism and strengthen the waning religious spirit. The simplest expression of the objects of the Society is the following:

*First.*—To form the nucleus of a Universal Brotherhood of Humanity, without distinction of race, creed, sex, caste, or color.

*Second.*—To promote the study of Aryan and other Eastern literatures, religions, and sciences.

*Third.*—A third object—pursued by only a portion of the members of the Society—is to investigate unexplained laws of nature and the psychical powers of man.

No person’s religious opinions are asked upon joining, nor is interference with them permitted, but everyone is required, before admission, to promise to show toward fellow members the same tolerance in this respect as they claim for themselves.

The headquarters, offices, and managing staff are at Adyar, a suburb of Madras, where the Society has a property of twenty-seven acres and extensive buildings, including one for the Oriental Library, and a spacious hall where the General Council meets annually in Convention on the 27th of December.

The Society is not yet endowed, but there is the nucleus of a fund, the income from the investment of which will go toward defraying current expenses; these have hitherto been met by the proceeds of entrance fees, donations, and a small annual subscription from each member. However, by the Revised Rules of 1889, the Society has been placed on a basis of voluntary contributions and is, therefore, entirely dependent for maintenance upon the generosity of its Fellows and others, as entrance fees and annual dues are abolished. No salaries are paid; all work is done by volunteers, who receive simple food and necessary clothing when their private circumstances require such allowances.

The official trustee for all Society property is the President for the time being, and legacies and bequests *should invariably be made in his name*, in the legal phraseology of the code of the country where the testator executes his will. If left to the Society by name, the bequest becomes void in law. The President’s full address is Henry Steel Olcott, Adyar, Madras, India.

The Society, as a body, eschews politics and all subjects outside its declared sphere of work. The rules stringently forbid members to compromise its strict neutrality in these matters.

Many branches of the Society have been formed in various parts of the world, and new ones are constantly being organized. Each branch frames its own bylaws and manages its own local business without interference from headquarters, provided only that the fundamental rules of the Society are not violated. Branches lying within certain territorial limits (as for instance, America, the British Islands, Europe, India (four sections), Ceylon, etc.) have been grouped for purposes of administration into territorial sections. For particulars, see the Revised Rules of 1889, where all necessary information regarding joining the Society, etc., will also be found.

Up to this date (1889), more than 200 branches of the Society have been founded. For particulars, see the rules, etc., of the Theosophical Society, to be had on application to the Recording Secretary of the Theosophical Society, Adyar, Madras; or to the General Secretaries of the sections.

In England, Mr. W. R. Old, 19, Avenue Road, Regent’s Park, London. In America, William Q. Judge, P.O. Box 2659, New York.

<vocab>Theosophical Society, Universal Brotherhood, Sanskrit, Pali, Zend, Aryan, Adyar, Henry Steel Olcott, Revised Rules of 1889, William Q. Judge</vocab>

<summary>This appendix outlines the founding, objectives, and organizational structure of the Theosophical Society, emphasizing its commitment to universal brotherhood and the study of ancient Eastern wisdom. It also details the society's administrative policies, including its reliance on voluntary contributions and its neutrality in political matters as of 1889.</summary>

<page-type>appendix</page-type>
<header>APPENDIX.</header>

# THE LEGAL STATUS OF THE THEOSOPHICAL SOCIETY,

*The following Official Report, upon which a Decree of Incorporation was granted to the St. Louis Theosophical Society, is an important document, as it puts on record the view taken of the Theosophical Society—after a careful examination of witnesses under oath—by an American Court of Law.*

First—The petitioner is not a religious body. I report this negative finding for the reason that the word “Theosophical,” contained in the petitioner's name, conveys a possible religious implication. The statutory phrase “society formed for religious purposes” applies, I suppose, only to an organization formed in part for worship—worship being an individual act involving adoration and perhaps emotional power, both being of necessity individual acts—or else to an organization formed for the propagation of a religious faith. Merely to teach a religion as one may teach algebra is not, I think, a religious work, as the word “religious” is used in the Statute and the Constitution. A man may occupy a collegiate chair as a Professor of Religions and, as such, teach the tenets of many religions. These different religions being variant and antagonistic, the Professor could not by any possibility worship under all of them. Indeed, he might even be irreligious. Hence, merely teaching religions is not a religious work in the statutory sense. It will be noted that in Article 2 of this society's constitution, the word "religion" is used in the plural. To teach religions is educational, not religious. “To promote the study of religions” is, in part, to promote the study of the history of man. I add the subordinate finding that the society has no religious creed and practices no worship. 

Second—The petitioner proposes to promote the study of literature and sciences. These objects are expressly within the terms of the Statute. 

Third—Related to the last object is that of investigating “unexplained laws of nature and psychical powers latent in man.” These two phrases, taken in their apparent meaning, are unobjectionable. But there is reason to believe that they form a meaning other than the apparent one. The court will take notice of the commonly accepted meaning of the word “Theosophy.” Though I am ignorant of Theosophy, I think it is supposed to include, among other things, manifestations and phenomena—physical and psychical—that violate the laws now known by physicists and metaphysicians, and perhaps are not explained, or claimed to be explained or understood, even by Theosophists themselves. In this group may be included Spiritualism, mesmerism, clairvoyance, mind-healing, mind-reading, and the like. I took testimony on this question and found that while a belief in any of these sorts of manifestations and phenomena is not required, and while each member of the society is at liberty to hold his own opinion, yet such questions form topics of inquiry and discussion, and the members as a mass are probably individual believers in phenomena that are abnormal and in powers that

<summary>This page presents a legal report from an American court regarding the incorporation of the St. Louis Theosophical Society, defining it as an educational rather than a religious organization. The court official argues that studying various religions academically is distinct from practicing a religion, and notes the society's interest in investigating psychical phenomena and unexplained natural laws.</summary>
<keywords>Theosophical Society, Decree of Incorporation, St. Louis, legal status, religious vs. educational, worship, study of religions, psychical powers, unexplained laws of nature, Spiritualism, mesmerism.</keywords>

--- Page 389 ---
<meta>This page continues the legal analysis of the Theosophical Society's application for incorporation, specifically addressing whether the study of "occultism" and "supernatural" phenomena is a legitimate pursuit for a legally recognized organization.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<header>APPENDIX.</header>
<sig>BB</sig>

are superhuman as far as science currently knows. It is undoubtedly the right of any citizen to hold whatever opinions he pleases on these subjects, and to endeavor at his pleasure to investigate the unexplained and to reveal the hidden. But the question here is: Shall the Court grant a franchise in aid of such an endeavor? Voodooism is a word applied to the practices of deceitful men among the ignorant and superstitious who impose frauds upon innocent people among the ignorant and superstitious. No Court would grant a franchise in furtherance of such practices. The Court, then, will stop to inquire into the practices and perhaps the reputation of the enterprise seeking judicial aid. I do not mean to make a comparison between voodooism and this group of phenomena which, for convenience (though I do not know if accurately), I will call occultism. I only take voodooism as an extreme case to show that the Court ought to inquire. If we now inquire into occultism, we shall find that it has been occasionally used, as is reported, for the purposes of fraud. But this counts for nothing against its essential character. Always and everywhere, bad men will make a bad use of anything for selfish ends. The object of this society, whether attainable or not, is undeniably praiseworthy, assuming that there are physical and psychical phenomena currently unexplained, and that Theosophy seeks to explain them. Assuming that there are human powers still hidden, it seeks to discover them. It may be that absurdities and frauds are, in fact, incidental to the early stage of its development. Regarding a field like occultism, which asserts powers commonly thought superhuman and phenomena commonly thought supernatural, it seemed to me that the Court—though not claiming to judicially determine the question of their truth—would, before granting a franchise to occultism, inquire whether it had at least gained a position of respectability, or whether its adherents were merely men of narrow intelligence, low intellect, and indiscriminate belief. I accordingly took testimony on that point, and find that a number of gentlemen in different countries of Europe, and also in this country, eminent in science, are believers in occultism. Sir Edward Bulwer Lytton, a writer of large and varied learning and of solid intellect, is asserted to have been an occultist—an assertion supported by at least two of his books. The late President Wayland, of Brown University, writing of abnormal mental operations as shown in clairvoyance, says: “The subject seems to me well worthy of the most searching and candid examination. It is by no means deserving of ridicule, but demands the attention of the most philosophical inquiry.” Sir William Hamilton, probably the most acute and, undeniably, the most learned of English metaphysicians who ever lived, said at least thirty years ago: “However astonishing, it is now proved beyond all rational doubt that in certain abnormal states of the nervous organism, perceptions are possible through channels other than the ordinary senses.” By such testimony, Theosophy is at least placed on a respectable footing. Whether through further labor it can make partial truths complete truths, and whether it can eliminate extravagances and purge itself of impurities, if there are any, are probably questions upon which the Court will not feel called upon to rule. I see no other feature of the petitioners’ constitution that is liable to legal objection, and accordingly, I have the honor to report that I find no reason why the request of the petitioners should not be granted.

->AUGUST W. ALEXANDER,<-
->Amicus Curiæ.<-

<vocab>occultism, Voodooism, Theosophy, Sir Edward Bulwer Lytton, President Wayland, Sir William Hamilton, Amicus Curiæ, legal franchise, judicial aid</vocab>

<summary>In this legal report, the Amicus Curiae argues that while occultism is often associated with fraud, the Theosophical Society's goals are scientifically laudable and supported by reputable thinkers. Consequently, he recommends that the court grant the society's request for incorporation.</summary>
